Michael Gannotti

# Welcome

You don't need to be a programmer to teach an 11- to 14-year-old real computing.

You do need to ask "What do you predict will happen?" before anyone presses run, and to leave the program in your student's hands when it breaks.

That is what this book is for.

## The book in one minute

Here is a picture. It is an illustration, not a real family.

Nora is twelve. She has built dozens of Scratch games: a maze, a quiz, a cat that dodges falling pizza. Today she opens a Python editor for the first time and types three lines. The third one is supposed to greet the player by name. She presses run.

Red text fills the bottom of the screen.

Her mother is sitting beside her. She can see the problem, more or less. She doesn't reach for the keyboard. "What does it say?"

Nora reads it aloud, a little offended. "Something was never closed. Line 3."

"So where's line 3?"

Nora finds it. She counts the brackets with her finger: one open, none closed. She adds the missing one and runs it again. The greeting appears. She grins, then frowns. "It could have just said that."

"It did," says her mother. "It said line 3."

That is the book in one minute. The computer did exactly what it was told. The error message was information, not judgment. The parent asked a question instead of fixing it. And the program that works now is Nora's, because Nora fixed it.

## Who this book is for

This book has two readers.

The first is you: the parent, grandparent, or co-teacher at the table. You may never have written a line of code. That's fine. Each chapter teaches you the idea first, in plain words, so you can hear where your student's thinking goes off track and ask one good question.

The second is your student. At 11 to 14, young people can hold a plan in their heads, write it down, test it, and argue with you about it. They want more say in their own work, and they're ready for it. Every chapter has a section written to them directly, as "you," longer and more grown-up than the student pages in a book for younger children.

## What your student will learn

Over a year, your student will plan and write real programs in Python, a language you type rather than snap together, readable enough for beginners and used by professionals. They'll learn to find and fix their own bugs calmly. They'll build a small website. They'll find out how computers and networks actually move information around. They'll learn how to stay safe and kind online, how AI tools work, and whose work it is when one of them helps.

The pace is about two or three Computing Hours a week. Each Hour is forty to sixty minutes long, and about half of the book's activities need no screen at all: paper, index cards, a deck of cards, masking tape on the floor.

## The line the whole book runs on

**Predict before you run; debug before you rewrite; explain it before you call it yours.**

*Predict before you run* means writing down what you think a program will do before you start it, so there's something to check against.

*Debug before you rewrite* means that when something breaks, you find the one wrong thing and fix it, instead of deleting everything and starting over.

*Explain it before you call it yours* means that your work is what you planned, wrote, tested, and can explain, and that you say plainly what help you used.

Hold those three habits, and the rest of this manual is practice.

## You are not alone

About 3.4 percent of school-age children were homeschooled in 2022–23, by the federal count.^[1](#note-1)^ Many of those families have taught computing for years with very little help, piecing together a website here and a free course there. This book is the help: one clear way to run a week, and a menu of activities you can choose from.

## What this book will not do

It won't hand you a 180-day stack of worksheets. You'll get a weekly shape and a menu of named activities, and you choose.

It won't sell you a curriculum, a gadget, or a subscription. The home Python editor this book recommends is free and needs no account.

It won't promise a test score. It won't tell you that coding makes young people better at everything, either. Coding teaches coding. Other good things may come along, and that's a welcome possibility, not a promise.

It won't give anyone under 13 an account or a chatbot. At 11 and 12, you create and hold every login, and any AI tool runs on your account with you at the keyboard. At 13 and 14, your student can use some tools under their terms, with you nearby.

It won't title your student's year with a book name or hand out a certificate. If you keep records, use a plain title a stranger can read, such as **Computer Science**.

It isn't a reprint of any earlier computing book. It was written fresh for this age.

## Where this book sits

*Computing for Young Minds* is the second title in the SMF WisdomForge Computing series. *Computing for Little Thinkers* (ages 5–10) comes before it, and *Computing for Emerging Adults* (15–18) comes after. *Computing Refresh for Adults* is for grown-ups who want their own turn.

Little Thinkers is not required. A student who never used it starts at Chapter 1 and is fine. A student who did will recognize the shape of the week and the habit of predicting first, and will find almost everything else new.

## How the book is built

The front matter gives you a plan for this week, five things to remember, and **The Computing Hour**: the five-step session shape, Family safety basics, a short note on screen time, and the one box about AI. Each is said carefully, once, and the chapters point back to it.

Eight teaching chapters follow. They cover plans and algorithms; first programs in Python; debugging; functions, lists, and data; building for the web; how computers and networks work; staying safe online; and how AI works. Then come two chapters for you: records and a portfolio, and resources.

Every teaching chapter uses the same eight headings, a talk box with an exact opening question, and named activities for inside the home and outside it. Each chapter gives moves for two bands, **11–12** and **13–14**. Place your student by skill, not birthday.

## Where to begin

This week, you can learn one question, "What do you predict will happen?", and practice waiting after you ask it.

Your student can write instructions for something ordinary, like making a sandwich, and watch you follow them exactly as written. The results are usually funny, and very instructive.

You don't need to know Python yet. You need a kitchen table, a notebook, and a question you're willing to wait for.

Bring a pencil. The computer can wait five minutes.

— Michael Gannotti

\newpage

# How to Use This Book This Week

You can start this week. You don't need to read the whole book first, buy anything, or know any Python. Here is a plan for your first week, and then a short tour of how the rest of the book works.

## This week

**Monday (you, about 40 minutes).** Read **The Computing Hour**, the session shape every chapter uses. Then read the first two sections of Chapter 1, "Why this matters" and "For the parent: understand it yourself." That's all the preparation you need.

**First Computing Hour.** Unplug and Plan with PLAN-01 The Literal Sandwich Spec: your student writes instructions for making a sandwich, and you follow them exactly as written, like a very literal computer. Then Predict with PY-02 Predict the Print: a four-line program on paper, a written guess, and a check. No screen is needed today. Start the Debug Log with the sandwich bugs. The Debug Log is a dated notebook (paper or a plain file) that holds predictions, bugs, clues, and fixes. Photograph the marked-up spec. Close with the exit lines: "I predicted… It did… Next I'd change…" and "Help I used: …" (today: none).

**Second Computing Hour.** Play ALG-01 Guess My Number, where your student finds your secret number by halving the range each time. Then install Thonny together (PY-01 Hello, Editor). Thonny is a free Python editor for beginners. It comes with Python built in, needs no account, and works offline once it's installed.^[2](#note-2)^ Your student types a first program, runs it, and saves it as `2026-10-12-hello.py` in two places: the family drive (or a USB stick) and one backup.

**Optional, this week or next.** Write SAFE-08 Family Device Agreement together: where devices live, who holds the passwords, and what to do if something online feels wrong. It belongs to Chapter 7, but many families like to set the rules before the computer comes out. If a phone is arriving soon, do it now.

**Other days.** Ten minutes is plenty: a Paper Parsons (PLAN-07, cut-up lines of a program to put in order) or a short typing warm-up.

## What to save from week one

- the marked-up sandwich spec (a photo is fine);
- `2026-10-12-hello.py`, saved in two places;
- one dated page of the Debug Log;
- the family agreement, if you wrote it.

That is the beginning of a portfolio. Chapter 9 shows you how to keep it.

## How each chapter is built

Every teaching chapter uses the same eight headings, in the same order:

1. **Why this matters**: the idea in a page.
2. **For the parent: understand it yourself**: what you need to know to hear a wrong turn.
3. **How to teach it this week**: the first activities, and the chapter's **talk box**, which gives an exact opening question, follow-ups, and how to wait.
4. **Practice that actually builds learning**: more activities, each with a bank code.
5. **For the student**: written to your student directly.
6. **If it isn't clicking**: what to try when it's not working.
7. **Tools, including AI**: what to use, and what not to, at each age.
8. **What "done enough" looks like**: how to know it's time to move on.

Activities are marked **In-home** or **Out-of-home**, and each lists time, materials, safety and privacy, the fun, the skill, and the crumb (what to save). Moves for the two bands are marked **11–12** and **13–14**. "Ready 12" marks a 13–14 move that some 12-year-olds can take. Place by skill, not birthday.

## What order to go in

Do Chapters 1–3 in order: plans and algorithms, then first Python, then debugging. After that, Chapters 4–8 can go in any order that suits your family. If a phone or a first account is arriving, move Chapter 7 earlier. Chapters 9 and 10 are for you; dip into them whenever you need them.

## The bank

The book holds 100 named activities, each with a code such as PLAN-01 or PY-04. Forty-nine of them need no screen at all. You won't do them all, and you shouldn't try. Pick what fits the week and skip the rest without guilt.

## A year in outline (optional)

| Weeks | Focus | A project to finish |
|---|---|---|
| 1–4 | Chapter 1, plans and algorithms (agreement in week 1) | A flowchart and pseudocode for a family routine |
| 5–10 | Chapter 2, Python basics | A guess-the-number game |
| 11–14 | Chapter 3, debugging | A planted-bug swap |
| 15–20 | Chapter 4, functions, lists, and data | A quiz program; a small data investigation |
| 21–25 | Chapter 5, the web | A three-page site and a demo day |
| 26–30 | Chapter 6, computers and networks | A pixel picture; a secret-code challenge |
| 31–33 | Chapter 7, safety (going deeper) | A settings tour |
| 34–36 | Chapter 8, AI | A next-word guesser; a Whose Work audit |

A Cold Trace (AS-01) once a month and a demo day once a semester run all year. Treat this as a picture of one year, not a schedule to keep.

## Siblings and co-ops

Siblings can work as a pair: one types (the driver), one reads and checks (the navigator), and they switch every ten minutes. Two computers side by side, with lots of talk, works just as well. In one study of students entering sixth grade, pairs and students on their own computers learned about the same, and the solo students finished a little faster.^[3](#note-3)^ Pairing is a good way to work together, not a promise of better scores.

A younger sibling, say nine, can join the unplugged cards and play the literal computer. For their own Hour, *Computing for Little Thinkers* fits. A co-op can hold one demo day a semester.

## Special situations

**A student far ahead of you.** Your job doesn't change: ask for the prediction, be the first user, and ask for the explanation. Let your student teach it back to you.

**A student new to all of this at 13.** Start at Chapter 1. The 13–14 moves go quickly, and the Scratch side-by-side work is optional.

**No computer at home.** Use the 49 unplugged activities, plus Computing Hours at the public library (OUT-02).

**Learning differences.** Use shorter screen blocks, larger fonts, code read aloud, and cut-up programs to put in order instead of a blank file. The Hour's five steps stay the same.

**A student who has already used chatbots at 11.** No shame and no scolding. Move to parent-operated use, and talk through the terms and why they exist.

## A week with no screens

Sick laptop, travel, or a week at grandma's: run the Hour on paper. Trace a program by hand, sort cards, play Guess My Number in the car. Paper weeks are real computing weeks.

## When life happens

You will miss days. Pick up where you left off. Two short Hours in a busy week still count. The habits matter more than the calendar.

# If You Only Remember Five Things

Some weeks you won't open the book at all. On those weeks, these five are enough.

## 1. Predict before you run.

Before anything runs, your student writes one line: *I predict it will…* A prediction turns a program into an experiment. When the guess is wrong, you've learned exactly where your student's picture of the code differs from the computer's, and that's the most useful thing that can happen in an Hour. No run without a prediction line.

## 2. Debug before you rewrite — read the error, change one thing, log it.

When the program surprises you, nobody deletes anything. First write what you expected and what actually happened. Then read the error message, which usually names a line. Change one thing, run it again, and write one line in the Debug Log: the bug, the clue, the fix. "I'll just start over" becomes "Save this as a new version, then tell me the one thing that's wrong."

## 3. Explain it before you call it yours. "Help I used:" every time, even "none."

Your student's work is what they planned, wrote, tested, and can explain. If they can't explain a line, it isn't theirs yet. Every Computing Hour ends the same way:

"I predicted… It did… Next I'd change…"

"Help I used: …"

A tutorial, a sibling, you, an AI tool: all of it counts, and none of it is shameful. Anything a tool generated is made by a tool, not your student's own work.

## 4. Paper first, then screen; save in two places; screens out of the bedroom.

Every Hour starts with the screen dark and a plan on paper. About half of this book's activities never need a screen at all. When there is a file, it lives in two places: the family drive (or a USB stick) and one backup, with a date-first name a stranger could read. The computer lives in a shared room, and phones and laptops sleep outside bedrooms.

## 5. You hold the passwords at 11–12 and share them at 13–14; AI is parent-operated under 13 and supervised after.

At 11 and 12, you create and hold every login, and any AI tool runs on your account with you at the keyboard. At 13, many services let a young person sign up. A big reason is a federal privacy law that requires a parent's consent only for children under 13.^[4](#note-4)^ Thirteen is a rule for companies, not a readiness test. At 13 and 14, your student may hold passwords for tools the family has approved, with two-step login turned on and a copy kept by you, and may use AI tools whose terms allow their age, with you nearby. Check the current terms before you start. The details live in **The Computing Hour**, under Family safety basics and the AI box.

---

If you remember only one sentence, make it this one:

**Predict before you run; debug before you rewrite; explain it before you call it yours.**

# The Computing Hour

![A kitchen table set for the Hour by a sunny window: an open laptop showing indented colored bars, a small LED learning board, an open Debug Log notebook, an index card, a pencil and a mug — still life, no people, no logos, no readable text.](art/computing-hour-setup.jpg){width=100%}

Forty to sixty minutes. Five steps. Paper first, screen second, and a clear stop.

"Hour" is the family word. At 11–12 a session runs about 40–50 minutes, roughly 20–30 of them on a screen. At 13–14 it runs about 50–60, and a project day can stretch to about 75 with a movement break. Later chapters say "run the Computing Hour" and point back here, so you learn it once.

Every Hour runs on one line:

**Predict before you run; debug before you rewrite; explain it before you call it yours.**

## Parent warm-up (about three minutes)

Read today's talk-box opening aloud once. Run the day's program yourself, on your own copy, so nothing surprises you except the interesting things. Check where today's file will be saved. Put your own phone face down.

## The five steps

| Step | 11–12 | 13–14 | What happens |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1. **Unplug and Plan** | 5–10 min | 5–10 min | Paper first: pseudocode, a flowchart, a test table, or one unplugged card. |
| 2. **Predict** | 2–5 min | 2–5 min | A written prediction line in the Debug Log before anything runs. |
| 3. **Build and Test** | 15–25 min | 20–35 min | Small steps, run often, test against the plan. |
| 4. **Debug and Log** | 5–10 min | 5–15 min | Expected/Actual, read the error, one change at a time, one Debug Log line. |
| 5. **Save and Explain** | 3–5 min | 3–5 min | Save a new version in two places. Explain it, and say what helped. |

### 1. Unplug and Plan

The screen stays dark. Your student puts a plan on paper: a few lines of pseudocode (a plan for a program written in plain words), a flowchart, or a test table that lists inputs and the outputs they expect. Some days the whole step is one unplugged card from the chapter. Ask the talk-box opening here. A plan doesn't need to be right. It needs to exist, so there is something to test against.

### 2. Predict

Before anything runs, your student writes one line in the **Debug Log**, a dated notebook (paper or a plain file) that holds predictions, bugs, clues, and fixes. No run without a prediction line. The model for this step is PY-02 Predict the Print: a short program on paper, a written guess, then the run.

```python
score = 3
score = score + 2
print("Score:", score)
print(score * 2)
```

This prints `Score: 5` on one line and `10` on the next. A student who predicted `Score: 3` has just met the most useful idea in Chapter 2: a variable is a labeled box whose contents can change, and line 2 changed it. A wrong prediction is not a failure; it is a window into your student's thinking.

### 3. Build and Test

Your student types, runs, and tests in small steps, running after every few lines and testing against the plan. Most weeks follow the same arc: use a working program, change one part, then make something new.

Your hands stay off the keyboard. Your job is **first user**: when the program is ready, you try it the way a stranger would and report what happened, without fixing anything.

### 4. Debug and Log

A bug is a mistake in the instructions; debugging is finding and fixing it. When the program surprises them, your student writes an Expected/Actual line before touching the code (BUG-01): what I expected, what actually happened. Then they read the error message, if there is one, and change one thing at a time, running after each change. One short line goes in the Debug Log (BUG-08): the bug, the clue, the fix.

A Debug Log entry is evidence of good work, not a confession. Chapter 3 is all about debugging.

### 5. Save and Explain

Save a new version with a name a stranger could read, date first, such as `2026-10-12-guess-game-v2.py`, in two places. Screens off. Then your student says (at 11–12) or writes (at 13–14) the exit lines:

"I predicted… It did… Next I'd change…"

"Help I used: …"

The second line is said every time, even when the answer is "none." A tutorial, a sibling, the documentation, you, an AI tool: all of it counts, and none of it is shameful. Said every Hour, honesty becomes ordinary.

## Who runs it

**11–12.** You run Unplug and Plan for the first month: you bring the plan or the card and ask the opening. Your student runs Predict through Save and Explain from week one. By midyear your student writes the plan, and you check it.

**13–14.** Your student is the session lead. They write the plan, run the clock, keep the Debug Log, and call the save. You are the first user, the question-asker, and the keeper of the safety rules. Once a week, take five minutes to read the Debug Log together.

Place by skill, not birthday. A 14-year-old who is new to all of this may want a month with you running the plan. In the chapters, "ready 12" marks a 13–14 move some 12-year-olds can take.

## The rescue order

When your student is stuck, use the same order every time: **ask → wait → hint → short demo on your own copy → hand it back.**

Ask a real question. Wait. Offer a hint that points without solving: "Look at line 4." If a demo is truly needed, show it on your own paper or file, then close it and hand the turn back. Your hands stay off your student's keyboard. A program they fixed is theirs.

## The talk-box shape

Every teaching chapter has a talk box. The shape never changes.

**The opening question** is real. It asks about your student's own plan, prediction, evidence, or judgment, and it has no answer to recite. Each chapter gives its exact opening.

**Three to five follow-ups**, drawn from these:

- prediction: "What will it do?"
- evidence: "What did you see that makes you say that?"
- location: "Which line is the last one that did what you expected?"
- change: "What's the one thing you'd change?"
- revision: "You can change your prediction. Run it again."

**How to wait.** After you ask, count to five in your head. The habit comes from classroom studies of "wait time," used here as a pause, not a stopwatch.^[5](#note-5)^ When the question is about code on the screen, let them read as long as they need. Look at the code, not at your student.

**What a stuck silence usually means.** The question was vague. There was no wait. Your student fears being judged, which runs strong at this age, so keep every comment about the code. They're waiting for you to fix it, because you have before. There was no prediction to compare. Or the screen is louder than the question; lower the lid for a moment.

**One "don't say / say instead" line in every chapter.** For example: don't say "That's wrong"; say "Show me the line where it stops doing what you expected."

## The try-it shapes

Every named try-it gives you six things: **time**, **materials**, **safety and privacy**, **the fun**, **the skill**, and **the crumb** (what to save). Each heading carries a bank code, such as *In-home — PLAN-01 The Literal Sandwich Spec* or *Out-of-home — OUT-02 Library Computer Hour*. Each teaching chapter has at least one of each.

OUT-02 is worth knowing now. A whole Computing Hour can run on a public library computer, with you beside your student, the file saved to your own USB stick, and both of you logged out before you leave. A home with no computer can do about half of this book's activities on paper and the rest at the library.

## When the Hour goes wrong

| If the Hour looks like… | Try this |
|---|---|
| The computer won't start, or the internet is down | Thonny, the book's home Python editor, runs offline. If the machine itself won't start, make it a paper day: trace the program by hand. |
| An error nobody understands | Expected/Actual first. Read the error's last line aloud, then find the line number. Log it. Tomorrow is allowed. |
| "I'm deleting it and starting over" | "Save this as a new version first. Then tell me the one thing that's wrong." |
| Racing ahead and skipping Predict | No run without a written prediction line. Hands on the table until it's there. |
| Tears, or a slammed laptop | Stand up, get water, come back in five minutes. Stop while it's still fixable. |
| A sibling interrupts or takes over | Driver and navigator, swapping every ten minutes, or an unplugged card for the sibling. |
| You're itching to type | Hands in your lap. Point with words: "Look near line 6." Demo on your own copy if you must. |

## Family safety basics

These are ordinary and non-negotiable. Chapters point back here; Chapter 7 teaches them in depth.

- **You hold the keys.** At 11–12 you create and hold every login, and your student never signs up for anything alone. At 13–14 your student may hold passwords for tools the family has approved. You keep a sealed copy or a family password-manager entry, and two-step login (a password plus a second thing, such as a code) is turned on.
- **The 13 shift.** A federal law called COPPA requires sites and apps that collect personal information from children under 13 to get a parent's consent first, and photos, videos, and voice recordings count as personal information.^[4](#note-4)^ That protection ends at 13, a big reason so many services set 13 as their minimum age. Thirteen is a rule for companies, not a readiness test. Your family's rules sit on top.
- **Shared room, phone-free bedrooms.** The computer lives in a shared space. Phones and laptops stay out of bedrooms overnight, and screens go off an hour before bed.
- **Stop, Save, Tell.** If something online is scary, mean, or threatening: stop replying, save it instead of deleting it, and tell a parent. Nobody is in trouble for telling.
- **First names only.** No school, town, or face in anything shared beyond the family. Screenshots are cropped to the work.
- **Two places.** Every file lives in two places: the family drive (or a USB stick) and one backup. Not a shared bot computer, and not a chatbot.
- **Updates on, unknown downloads off.** At 11–12 you approve every new app. Publishing anything online is optional, and it's your call.
- **Old hardware.** Adults open old machines, unplugged. Nobody opens batteries, power supplies, or old tube screens.

## A note on screen time

The American Academy of Pediatrics replaced its older media guidance in 2026. Its policy statement puts the weight on content and on what screens push out: sleep, meals, movement, and homework. It suggests phone-free meals, bedrooms, the hour before bed, and homework; parent-set controls for privacy, downloads, content, purchases, and time; and a family-tailored range of roughly one to two hours or more a day of entertainment media for school-age children and teens, with school media counted separately.^[6](#note-6)^

The World Health Organization asks families to limit recreational screen time and to keep children and teens moving at least an hour a day. It gives no number for screens.^[7](#note-7)^ On sleep, the CDC points to the sleep-medicine recommendations: 9 to 12 hours in every 24 for children 6 to 12, and 8 to 10 hours for teens 13 to 18.^[8](#note-8)^ A late-night bug goes in the Debug Log and waits for morning.

These are guidelines for families, not a stopwatch rule, and they change as the research does. The Computing Hour is purposeful school media. About half its work is unplugged, and it ends with screens off.

## The AI box

**What AI is here for.** AI can explain, suggest, and check, sometimes well and sometimes confidently wrong. Your student does the planning, predicting, writing, testing, and debugging. Anything an AI produces is made by a tool, never your student's own work. Chapter 8 explains how it works.

**Ages 11–12: parent-operated.** Most consumer AI services set 13 as their minimum age, and at least one major one sets 18.^[9](#note-9),[10](#note-10)^ So at 11–12, AI runs on your account, on your device, with you at the keyboard or reading every exchange. Your student can dictate a question, read the answer beside you, and check it against a source. No student accounts on chatbots, AI tutors, or coding assistants, even inside a course that offers one. CodeAI (formerly Code.org), for example, labels its AI Tutor for students 13 and up, so in this book it stays off at 11–12.^[11](#note-11),[12](#note-12)^

**Ages 13–14: supervised, under the terms, with you.** Use only a service whose terms allow your student's age, and a teen or linked account where the service offers one. Sit nearby with the screen visible. Turn off training on your student's conversations, and turn off memory, where you can. Leave image generation off unless a lesson needs it. No companion, "friend," or role-play apps. Teen settings help; they are not a substitute for you in the room. Check the current terms before you start.

**You may, within the rule for your student's age:**

- have it explain an idea or an error message *after* your student has written Expected/Actual and a guess;
- ask for practice problems or a hint ladder, held back until your student has tried;
- at 13–14, get a code suggestion, then have your student explain every line before deciding whether to use any of it, with a "Help I used" line;
- check a claim it made against a real source.

**Please keep these off the table:**

- having it write your student's program, page, or project;
- using it during the AI-free checks: Cold Trace, Debug on the Spot, and Build From a Blank;
- filing generated code, pages, pictures, or text as your student's work, or as a portfolio piece;
- pasting private messages, photos, names, or your address into a chatbot;
- uploading photos of your children or family to any AI tool;
- companion, "friend," or role-play apps;
- an AI "detector" to judge your student's work, because "explain it to me" is the check;
- trusting a source it cites before you've opened it yourself.

**Whose work is it?** Your student's work is what they planned, wrote, tested, and can explain. If they can't explain a line, it isn't theirs yet. Every project carries a Whose Work Is It? card (AI-06): who or what helped, what was asked, what came back, what was kept, what was changed, and how it was tested.

**One caution from research, with a label.** In a study of high-school math students, those given an unrestricted chatbot did better on practice problems and worse on a later test taken without it; a version with guardrails avoided most of the harm.^[13](#note-13)^ That is high-school math, not 11–14 or code. The rule it supports is the one this book already uses: your student plans, predicts, and debugs first.

**The Grok Bot caution.** If you use Grok Bot, remember that all of your bots share one computer. Keep photos of your children and family off it, and keep your student's portfolio on a family drive.^[14](#note-14)^

## Dose honesty

There is no national table of how many minutes a middle-schooler should code. The standards many schools use imagine the equivalent of a yearlong course across grades 6–8, a school design rather than a home rule.^[15](#note-15)^ Two or three Computing Hours a week across a school year is a full course of experience. On other days, ten minutes is plenty: a Paper Parsons, a Cold Trace, a typing warm-up. Less is still real. Short and frequent beats long and rare.

## The life of the habit

Keep the Debug Log going all year. Do a Cold Trace (AS-01) once a month: a short program traced on paper, without help, dated. Finish a project every four to six weeks. Hold a demo day once a semester for family or a co-op. When the habit leaks out of the Hour, notice it and say so in a sentence.

## Five things to say

1. **Opening.** "Show me the plan. What happens first?" Then count to five in your head.
2. **Predict.** "Write the line: I predict it will… No run without it."
3. **First user.** "I'm going to try it like a stranger would." Then report: "It asked my name, I typed a number, and it crashed. Over to you."
4. **The bug moment.** "What did you expect, what happened instead, and what's your best clue?"
5. **Save and Explain.** "Save it as a new version, two places. Then tell me: 'I predicted… It did… Next I'd change…' and 'Help I used: …'"

## Ready to begin

You have the five steps, the rescue order, the talk-box and try-it shapes, Family safety basics, the screen-time note, and the AI box. Chapter 1 puts the focus line to work. Find bread, a jar, and a pencil.

\newpage

# Chapter 1

## What Computing Looks Like at 11–14: Plans, Algorithms, and Thinking It Through

![A desk with a hand-drawn flowchart of boxes, a diamond and arrows, a ruler, a small stack of index cards and a pencil — still life, no people, no logos, no readable text.](art/ch01-opener.jpg){width=100%}

## Why this matters

Here is a picture. It is an illustration, not a real family.

Eli is thirteen. His father has asked him to write instructions for making a peanut butter sandwich, and has promised to follow them exactly as written. Eli finds this beneath him. He writes four lines in about a minute: "Get the bread. Put peanut butter on it. Put the other slice on top. Done."

His father picks up the whole loaf, still in its bag, and sets it on the counter. Then he picks up the jar of peanut butter and places it, lid on, on top of the loaf. He looks up. "Done."

Eli laughs, and then he starts to argue. "You know what I meant." His father nods. "I do. A computer won't."

Twenty minutes later Eli has a page of instructions that open the bag, take out exactly two slices, twist the lid counterclockwise, and use the knife. His father asks one more question: "What if the jar is already open?" Eli adds a line that starts with IF.

That is computing at this age. Computing is telling a machine exactly what to do, and then finding out, calmly, why it did something else. A computer never guesses what you meant. It does what you said, in the order you said it, every time.

Eleven- to fourteen-year-olds are ready for this in a new way. They can hold a plan in their heads, write it down, test it, and defend it. They can imagine what might go wrong before it does. They are also ready to make the jump from *using* computers to *making* them do things. That jump is what this book is about.

It helps to know what computing is not. The organization that writes the computer science standards many American schools use says plainly that computer science is not "using technology tools like word processors, slide decks, or generative AI tools." It adds, about AI, "CS is what powers AI."^[16](#note-16)^ A student who can type a fast essay or get an answer from a chatbot is a skilled user. A student who can write the steps, predict what they'll do, and fix them when they go wrong is starting to do computing.

This chapter lays the foundation, and almost none of it needs a screen. Your student will write exact instructions, break big jobs into small ones, draw flowcharts, trace programs by hand, and compare two ways of doing a job. Every later chapter stands on these habits, and on the line the whole book runs on:

**Predict before you run; debug before you rewrite; explain it before you call it yours.**

## For the parent: understand it yourself

You don't need to know a programming language to teach this chapter. You need a handful of ideas in plain words, and the patience to play a very literal computer.

### The big ideas, in plain words

People sometimes call this whole set of habits computational thinking. In this book we'll just say what it is: breaking a job into parts, spotting patterns, writing exact steps, and checking them.

- **An algorithm** is a set of exact steps for doing a job. A recipe is an algorithm. So is the route you drive to the store. In computing, the steps have to be exact enough that something with no judgment at all could follow them.
- **Decomposition** means breaking a big job into smaller jobs. "Make dinner" is too big to program. "Boil water," "cook pasta," and "heat sauce" are getting closer.
- **Pseudocode** is a plan for a program written in plain words, with a few capitalized keywords such as SET, IF, and REPEAT. It isn't any real programming language, and that's the point: your student can think about the steps without worrying about brackets.
- **A flowchart** is the same plan as a picture. Ovals mark the start and end, rectangles are actions, and diamonds are decisions with a "yes" arrow and a "no" arrow.
- **A decision** (IF) lets a program do different things in different situations. **A loop** (REPEAT) does the same steps more than once.
- **A variable** is a labeled box that holds a value, such as a score, and the value can change as the program runs.
- **Tracing** means following a program by hand, line by line, and writing down what's in each box after every step.

The middle-school standards ask students to model an algorithm "with a flowchart or pseudocode" and to "verify the accuracy of an algorithm for given inputs."^[17](#note-17)^ In ordinary words: write the plan, then check that it works on real examples. That's this chapter.

### A program you can trace together

Here is a short program in pseudocode:

```
SET total TO 0
SET n TO 1
REPEAT 4 TIMES
    ADD n TO total
    ADD 1 TO n
SAY total
```

Trace it with your student on paper, in a table with one column for `total` and one for `n`. After each trip through the loop, write the new values. The first trip makes `total` 1 and `n` 2. Then 3 and 3, then 6 and 4, then 10 and 5. The program says **10**.

Plenty of adults guess wrong the first time. That's normal, and it's exactly why tracing matters. Your student will never trace a program perfectly by looking at it. Writing down each box after each step is how people who program for a living check their own thinking.

### Why paper first

Paper is not a lesser kind of computing. With the screen dark, your student has to hold the whole plan in mind instead of trying random changes to see what sticks. Paper is also where planning mistakes are cheapest. A wrong step in a sandwich spec costs a laugh. The same wrong step in a two-hundred-line program costs an evening.

And on paper, you can see your student's thinking. On a screen, you mostly see the result.

### The student you have

Young teens are building more capacity for complex thought and a stronger sense of right and wrong. They make more of their own choices and become more independent, although parents are still very important.^[18](#note-18)^ That's the practical case for this book's shape: your student writes more of the plan and runs more of the Hour, and you become the first user, the question-asker, and the keeper of the safety rules.

Young teens are also more sensitive to being judged by others than younger children or adults.^[19](#note-19)^ You'll see it when a bug feels like a verdict on the person. Keep every comment about the code: "Line 3 never runs when the jar is open" lands very differently from "You forgot again." Let your student choose what gets shown to anyone outside the family.

One more thing about the student you have. Children pick up the idea that computing is "for" some kids and not others as early as first grade.^[20](#note-20)^ Say plainly, and more than once, that computing is for anyone who likes making things work and figuring out why they don't.

### What the evidence says, honestly

Coding teaches coding. That much is solid. Whether it also helps with math, reading, or general problem-solving is a fair question, and the honest answer is "sometimes, a little, and less than people claim." A large review of studies found that learning to program helps most with skills close to programming, helps less with skills further away, and shows smaller effects when students are compared with others doing a different active task.^[21](#note-21)^ So treat any spillover as a welcome possibility, not a promise.

The good news about this age is solid too. In one carefully designed middle-school course, seventh and eighth graders learned real computer science concepts and carried some of them over to a text programming language, though loops and variables stayed hard for many.^[22](#note-22)^ This is a useful study, not a promise that every home will see the same result. It does match what you'll see at your table: this age can do real work, and some of it will take longer than you expect.

Fast thumbs are not the same thing as exact steps. Most 11- to 14-year-olds have never been asked to write a plan and test it, and that's the skill this book teaches.

### Why the third clause matters

The focus line ends with "explain it before you call it yours." That isn't a homeschool invention. The guidance for one widely taken high-school computer science course says students who use AI tools must be ready "to explain their code in detail."^[23](#note-23)^ At 11–14 this starts small and spoken: "Help I used: none." Chapter 8 goes further.

### Wrong answers you should be able to hear

| What you hear | What it usually means | What you can say |
|---|---|---|
| "You know what I meant." | The steps leave something to guessing. | "I do. The computer won't. Which line needs to be more exact?" |
| "It's obvious." | A hidden step nobody wrote down. | "Then it'll be quick to write. Show me where it goes." |
| "I'll just start over." | Overwhelm. | "Keep this page. Tell me the one thing that's wrong." |
| "It works, so it's right." | One example tested, no others. | "What's the strangest input you can think of? Let's try it." |
| "It says 4." (for the program above) | Guessing instead of tracing. | "Let's write the boxes after each trip around the loop." |

## How to teach it this week

This week's Hours run on the five steps from **The Computing Hour**. Here is how they fit the first activity:

1. **Unplug and Plan.** Your student writes the sandwich spec on paper. Ask the talk-box opening.
2. **Predict.** Your student writes one line: "I predict the sandwich will…"
3. **Build and Test.** You follow the spec exactly, one line at a time.
4. **Debug and Log.** Your student writes an Expected/Actual line (BUG-01), fixes one step, and you run the spec again from the top. Start the Debug Log (BUG-08) with the sandwich bugs: the bug, the clue, the fix.
5. **Save and Explain.** Photograph the marked-up spec. "I predicted… It did… Next I'd change…" and "Help I used: none."

### Exact words you can say

- Opening: "What are the steps — and where could they go wrong?"
- When a step is vague: "Computer does not understand. Which bread? How many slices?"
- After a funny result: "What did you expect? What happened instead?"
- At the fix: "Change one line. Then we start again from the top."
- An edge case: "What if the jar is already open?"
- At the end: "Save it. Then tell me: 'I predicted… It did… Next I'd change…' and 'Help I used: …'"

### In-home — PLAN-01 The Literal Sandwich Spec

- **Time:** about 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** bread, a spread (or a pretend version: a box for the loaf, a jar of anything), a butter knife, paper, and two colors of pen.
- **Safety and privacy:** use a butter knife, and check for food allergies in the room. Any photo is of the paper only.
- **The fun:** a parent who follows instructions with total, deadpan literalness.
- **The skill:** writing precise instructions, and thinking of the cases that could break them.
- **The crumb:** the marked-up spec, with the fixes in a second color.

**How it goes.** Your student writes the spec first, without you watching. Then you follow it line by line, exactly as written, and nothing more. "Put peanut butter on the bread" with no mention of the lid or the knife gets an unopened jar resting on the loaf. Keep a straight face. When it goes wrong, stop and let your student find the first line that failed, fix it in the second color, and run the whole spec again from the beginning.

The 11–14 twist is the edge case. Once the spec works, ask: "What if the jar is already open? What if there's only one slice left?" A good spec handles the ordinary case. A better one says what to do when things aren't ordinary.

**11–12:** your student can say the steps aloud first while you write them, then copy them out. You play the computer.

**13–14:** your student writes the spec alone, then plays the computer for a younger sibling's spec, or for yours. Try to write a spec they can't break. They will break it.

### In-home — PLAN-03 Pseudocode Recipe Card

- **Time:** about 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** an index card, a pencil, and a simple family recipe.
- **Safety and privacy:** paper only; no cooking needed.
- **The fun:** turning grandma's pancakes into something a robot could follow.
- **The skill:** plain-language pseudocode with IF and REPEAT.
- **The crumb:** the card.

**How it goes.** Pick a recipe and rewrite it in pseudocode, with at least one decision and one loop:

```
SET cups_of_flour TO 2
REPEAT cups_of_flour TIMES
    ADD 1 cup of flour TO bowl
ADD milk TO bowl
STIR
IF batter is lumpy THEN STIR again
```

Then swap: you read the card aloud as a literal computer, and your student listens for gaps. Where does the butter go? How long is "stir"?

**11–12:** one decision and one loop is plenty. **13–14:** add a decision with two outcomes (IF … ELSE), or a loop that repeats until something happens.

### In-home — PLAN-04 Flowchart the Morning

- **Time:** about 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** a large sheet of paper, a pencil, and an eraser.
- **Safety and privacy:** first names only if it's posted on the fridge.
- **The fun:** discovering how many decisions happen before breakfast.
- **The skill:** a flowchart with decision diamonds and a loop.
- **The crumb:** the flowchart.

**How it goes.** Your student draws the morning routine as a flowchart, from "alarm rings" to "out the door." Every decision is a diamond with exactly one question and two arrows, yes and no: "Is it a school day?" "Is the bathroom free?" At least one arrow should loop back: "Snooze pressed? Yes → back to alarm rings." Then you follow the flowchart with your finger, out loud, and your student checks whether it really describes their morning.

The usual trouble is a diamond with three questions in it. One question per diamond. If a diamond needs "and," it's two diamonds.

### In-home — ALG-01 Guess My Number

- **Time:** about 10 minutes.
- **Materials:** paper and a pencil for keeping count.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed. It works in the car.
- **The fun:** beating a parent at their own game, in very few guesses.
- **The skill:** searching by halving, and counting the steps.
- **The crumb:** the guess counts for 1–100 and for 1–1,000.

**How it goes.** You pick a secret number from 1 to 100. Your student guesses, and you say only "higher," "lower," or "yes." Count the guesses. Play a few rounds, then ask: "What's the best first guess? Why?"

The best plan is to guess the middle every time, which throws away half of the remaining numbers with each guess. Played that way, any number from 1 to 100 takes at most 7 guesses. Here's the surprise: 1 to 1,000 takes at most 10. Ten times as many numbers, only three more guesses. Let your student discover that by playing before you explain it. Chapter 2 turns this game into a real program.

**13–14:** ask your student to explain why each guess halves the problem, and to predict the most guesses needed for 1 to 2,000 before testing it.

### Talk box (this chapter)

**Opening:** "What are the steps — and where could they go wrong?"

**Follow-ups:**

1. Prediction: "What will happen at this step, exactly as it's written?"
2. Evidence: "What did you see that makes you say it worked?"
3. Location: "Which step is the last one that did what you expected?"
4. Change: "What's the one line you'd change?"
5. Edge case: "What's the strangest situation this plan might meet?"

**How to wait.** After you ask, count to five in your head. When the question is about code on the screen, let them read as long as they need. This chapter mostly lives on paper, so look at the page, not at your student. Your eyes on the plan say "we're examining the steps," not "I'm examining you."

**What a stuck silence usually means.** The question was too big ("Why didn't it work?"), so shrink it: "Was it this step or that one?" Your student may be bracing for a verdict, so name a bug of your own first. They may be waiting for you to fix it, so put the pencil down on your side of the table. Or there was no prediction, so there's nothing to compare. Go back and make one.

**Don't say / say instead.** Don't say "That's not how you make a sandwich." Say "I did exactly what line 2 says. Is that what you wanted?"

### How to fade help

Use the rescue order from **The Computing Hour**: ask → wait → hint → short demo on your own copy → hand it back.

- **First Hour:** you may point to the step that broke: "Look at line 2."
- **Second Hour:** ask only where it first went wrong.
- **By week's end:** ask the opening question and let your student run the fix.

When you notice good work, name it exactly. "You added a line for when the jar is already open, before I even asked" teaches more than "Great job."

### This week at a glance

| Day | Focus | Try-it |
|---|---|---|
| Hour 1 | Exact steps and edge cases | PLAN-01 The Literal Sandwich Spec; start the Debug Log |
| Hour 2 | Searching by halving | ALG-01 Guess My Number |
| Hour 3 | Plans on paper | PLAN-03 Pseudocode Recipe Card or PLAN-04 Flowchart the Morning |
| Other days | Ten minutes | PLAN-07 Paper Parsons; a trace table |
| Errand | Algorithms around you | OUT-01 Algorithms in the Wild |
| End of week | A baseline | AS-01 Cold Trace (see "done enough") |

## Practice that actually builds learning

Pick one or two of these per Hour over the next few weeks. Each is short, and most need nothing but paper.

### In-home — PLAN-02 Break It Down Tree

- **Time:** about 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** a large sheet of paper and a pencil.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** finding out how many tiny jobs hide inside one big one.
- **The skill:** decomposition: breaking a real job into parts a program could do.
- **The crumb:** the tree, dated.

**How it goes.** Write a big job at the top: "Host a family game night" or "Make tacos for six." Your student breaks it into three or four smaller jobs, then breaks each of those again, until every bottom box is something a person, or a program, could just do. "Choose a game" becomes "list the games we own," "count the players," and "cross off games for fewer players." Stop when the boxes are small enough to act on.

**13–14:** circle the boxes a computer could actually handle (counting, sorting, checking a list), and the ones that need a person.

### In-home — PLAN-05 Trace Table by Hand

- **Time:** about 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** paper ruled into columns, and a short program in pseudocode.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** being the computer, and catching your own wrong guess.
- **The skill:** tracing variable values line by line.
- **The crumb:** the trace table.

**How it goes.** Use the `total` and `n` program from "For the parent." Your student writes a prediction first, then traces it with one row per trip around the loop, and compares. Then change one line, such as REPEAT 5 TIMES, and predict again before tracing. The habit to build is predicting first, so a wrong guess becomes a clue instead of an embarrassment.

### In-home — PLAN-06 Two Algorithms, One Job

*For 13–14; ready 12.*

- **Time:** about 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** paper, a pencil, and a calculator for checking.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** finding a shortcut that does a hundred steps' work in one.
- **The skill:** comparing two algorithms by counting their steps.
- **The crumb:** the two step counts, side by side.

**How it goes.** The job: add up every whole number from 1 to 100. Algorithm A adds them one at a time: 1 + 2 = 3, 3 + 3 = 6, and so on. Count the additions: 99. Algorithm B pairs the numbers from the ends: 1 and 100 make 101, 2 and 99 make 101, all the way to 50 and 51. That's 50 pairs of 101, so the answer is 50 × 101 = 5,050, in one multiplication. Both get the same answer. One takes far fewer steps. That's what programmers mean by *efficient*.

Ask your student to predict how the step counts change for 1 to 1,000.

### In-home — PLAN-07 Paper Parsons

- **Time:** about 10 minutes.
- **Materials:** a short program printed or written out, cut into strips, one line per strip; tape.
- **Safety and privacy:** use scissors with care.
- **The fun:** a puzzle where every piece is a line of code.
- **The skill:** reading a program's order and structure.
- **The crumb:** the taped strip, in the right order.

**How it goes.** Cut up a short program, such as the pancake card or this one, mix the strips, and have your student put them in order:

```
SET coins TO 0
REPEAT 5 TIMES
    ADD 3 TO coins
IF coins > 10 THEN SAY "Enough for a snack"
```

Indented strips stay indented, so your student can see which lines belong inside the loop. Putting lines in order is a gentler start than writing from a blank page. One study, with university students, found that this kind of puzzle took less time than writing or fixing the same code, with similar learning.^[24](#note-24)^ It is not a study of 11- to 14-year-olds, but it explains why Parsons puzzles turn up again in later chapters.

### In-home — ALG-02 Card Sort Race

- **Time:** about 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** a deck of playing cards; paper for keeping a tally.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** a race where the slower method sometimes wins.
- **The skill:** two sorting methods, and counting comparisons.
- **The crumb:** the comparison tally.

**How it goes.** Deal eight cards face up in a row. Method one (selection sort): scan the whole row for the smallest card, move it to the front, then scan the rest for the next smallest, and so on. Method two (insertion sort): take cards one at a time from the left and slide each one back to its correct place among the cards already sorted. One player counts aloud every time two cards are compared.

Selection sort always takes 28 comparisons for eight cards, however they start. Insertion sort depends on the deal. Give it eight cards already in order, and it needs only 7. Ask your student to predict which method wins on an already-sorted row, then on a reversed one, and test both.

### In-home — ALG-03 Sorting Network on the Floor

- **Time:** about 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** masking tape, four index cards with numbers on them, and four people (or four stuffed animals moved by hand).
- **Safety and privacy:** clear the floor first. If you take a photo, take it of the tape, with no faces.
- **The fun:** a sorting machine you walk through.
- **The skill:** a sorting network: comparisons that happen side by side.
- **The crumb:** a photo of the floor network, with no faces.

**How it goes.** Tape four parallel lanes on the floor, with three rows of meeting squares. In row one, lane 1 meets lane 2, and lane 3 meets lane 4. In row two, lane 1 meets lane 3, and lane 2 meets lane 4. In row three, lane 2 meets lane 3. Each walker holds a number card and walks forward. At a meeting square, the two walkers compare cards: the smaller number takes the left lane out, the larger takes the right. Whatever order you start in, the cards come out sorted in three rows and five comparisons.

The idea comes from CS Unplugged, a free collection of computing activities without computers, which lists sorting networks for ages 5 to 14 and has a larger version with more lanes.^[25](#note-25)^ **13–14:** ask why two pairs can compare at the same time. Doing many comparisons at once is one way computers speed up big jobs.

### In-home — ALG-04 Battleships Search

- **Time:** about 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** two sheets of paper, each with a row of fifteen boxes; thirty small sticky notes; two pencils.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** a hidden-number duel.
- **The skill:** searching one by one versus searching by halving.
- **The crumb:** the score sheet with the number of looks for each round.

**How it goes.** Each player secretly writes fifteen numbers in their boxes and covers each with a sticky note. The other player names a target number and uncovers one box at a time to find it, counting each look. Round one: the numbers are in random order. The searcher may need all fifteen looks. Round two: the numbers are written in order from smallest to largest. Now the searcher can open the middle box and throw away half the row, just like Guess My Number. Fifteen sorted boxes take at most 4 looks. CS Unplugged has a printable version of this game.

**11–12:** play round one and two, and talk about the difference. **13–14:** explain why sorting first pays off only when you'll search more than once.

### Out-of-home — OUT-01 Algorithms in the Wild

- **Time:** about 20 minutes during an errand or walk.
- **Materials:** a notebook and a pencil.
- **Safety and privacy:** stand well back from the curb when watching traffic lights. Watch machines; leave other people out of notes and photos. At a self-checkout, watch without using someone else's lane. See Family safety basics in **The Computing Hour**.
- **The fun:** spotting the hidden program in something you see every day.
- **The skill:** writing pseudocode for a real machine.
- **The crumb:** the pseudocode page.

**How it goes.** Pick one machine: a traffic light, an elevator, or a self-checkout at the store. Watch it for a few minutes and talk about what it seems to do and when. Then sit on a bench and write its algorithm in pseudocode:

```
REPEAT forever
    SHOW green FOR some seconds
    SHOW yellow FOR a few seconds
    SHOW red
    IF a car is waiting on the side road THEN let it go
```

Then hunt for edge cases. What does the elevator do if people on two floors press the button at once? What if someone scans the same item twice? Your student won't know the real program, and that's fine. The point is a plausible one, exact enough to argue about.

**13–14:** write a second version that handles one edge case, and say which is better and why.

### Blocked, then mixed

**Blocked practice for the new move.** For the first week or two, every Hour includes the same core move: write exact steps, predict, and test them. Sandwich on Monday, pancakes on Wednesday, the morning on Friday.

**Mixed practice.** After that, mix it up. One Hour might hold a Paper Parsons, a trace table, and a round of Guess My Number. Mixing helps your student learn *when* to use each move, not just how.

### One incorrect example to diagnose

Here is an illustration, not a real family. A twelve-year-old writes a flowchart for "get ready for bed." One diamond reads: "Teeth brushed and pajamas on and homework done?" The "no" arrow points to "do the missing thing." The parent follows it and stops: "Which missing thing?"

**Diagnosis.** Three questions in one diamond, so the "no" answer can't say what to do next.

**Rescue.** One question per diamond. Split it into three diamonds, each with its own "no" arrow to a specific action. Then follow the chart again with your finger.

## For the student

This chapter is about something programmers do all day that most people never see: thinking it through before touching the keyboard.

You might picture a programmer typing fast, code flying across the screen. Real programming looks more like this: read the problem, plan, write a little, test it, find out why it didn't work, fix it, test again. A lot of the time goes into planning, reading, testing, and fixing. The typing is often the quick part.

That's why this chapter happens mostly on paper. It might feel like the baby version of coding. It isn't. Paper is where you can see the whole plan at once, and where mistakes are cheap.

**A computer does what you say, not what you mean.** That's the most important idea in this book. If you write "put peanut butter on the bread" and forget to say "open the jar," a computer won't open the jar. It isn't being stubborn. It has no idea what a jar is. It follows the steps, exactly, in order.

So your job is to write steps so exact that something with zero judgment could follow them. Steps like that are called an **algorithm**. Writing them in plain words with a few keywords (SET, IF, REPEAT) is called **pseudocode**, which means "pretend code." It's the plan before the program.

**A tiny example.** Here's a plan in pseudocode:

```
SET lives TO 3
REPEAT 2 TIMES
    SUBTRACT 1 FROM lives
IF lives > 0 THEN SAY "Keep going" ELSE SAY "Game over"
```

What will it say? Write your prediction down before you read on.

Start with 3 lives. Lose one: 2. Lose another: 1. Is 1 more than 0? Yes. So it says "Keep going." If you predicted "Game over," find the step where your thinking went a different way. Maybe you counted three trips through the loop instead of two. That's a bug in your trace, and finding it is exactly the skill.

**Your Debug Log.** Starting this week, you keep a Debug Log: a dated notebook (paper or a plain file) that holds predictions, bugs, clues, and fixes. Before you run anything, write a prediction. When something surprises you, write two things before you change anything: what you expected, and what actually happened. Then write the clue and the fix. It takes thirty seconds. In a month, it'll show you how much you've learned, and it'll save you from fixing the same bug twice.

**The line this book runs on.** You'll see it everywhere: *Predict before you run; debug before you rewrite; explain it before you call it yours.* In your own words, it means:

- Guess first, so you'll know if you're right.
- When it breaks, find the broken part. Don't throw the whole thing away.
- If you can't explain it, it isn't yours yet. And always say what help you used, even if the answer is "none."

**This part is hard for everyone.** Writing steps exact enough for a computer is harder than it looks, for adults too. People who program for a living still write plans that miss a case. The difference is that they expect it, look for it, and fix it without drama.

**Try this today.** Write the instructions for something you do every day: making toast, tying a shoe, logging in to a game. Then hand them to someone and ask them to follow them exactly, with no guessing. Watch where it goes wrong. Fix one line at a time.

**Explain it back.** Tell someone in two or three sentences: why does a computer need more exact steps than a person does? Use your own example.

**If you're 13 or older:** write a spec that someone else, maybe a younger sibling, has to follow, and then try to break theirs. Look for the edge cases: the jar that's already open, the last slice of bread, the elevator button pressed twice.

**Challenge.** Play Guess My Number with 1 to 100 until you can always win in 7 guesses or fewer. Then predict: how many guesses for 1 to 1,000? Test it. Can you explain why the answer is so small?

**What to save.** Your sandwich spec with the fixes, your first Debug Log page, and your Guess My Number counts. Date them all.

## If it isn't clicking

**The steps are too vague.** Play the literal computer again, and be truly literal. "Put it on the plate" gets "Put what? Which plate?" Then have your student read their own spec aloud, slowly, as if they'd never seen a sandwich.

**Flowchart spaghetti.** Arrows cross everywhere and diamonds hold three questions. One decision per diamond. Start again on a fresh sheet with just three boxes, then add one at a time.

**"This is boring. I want to code."** Fair enough. Show PY-02 Predict the Print on paper today: a four-line Python program, a written prediction, and a check. It's real code, and it uses exactly the same habit. Install Thonny tomorrow (Chapter 2). Then come back to planning, and point out that the prediction they just made was a trace.

**Tracing goes wrong every time.** Slow down to one line at a time, and write the boxes after every line, not just at the end of each loop. Use sticky notes as boxes and physically replace the number each time.

**Your student is far ahead of you.** Wonderful. Your job doesn't change: ask for the prediction, be the first user, and ask for the explanation. Have your student teach you the trace.

**A thirteen-year-old new to all of this.** Start here anyway. The 13–14 moves go fast.

## Tools, including AI

**What you need:** a pencil, an eraser, paper, index cards, a deck of playing cards, and masking tape. That's the whole list.

**Optional:** CS Unplugged (free, printable) for the sorting network and searching games.^[25](#note-25)^

**AI:** none needed for this chapter. Planning, predicting, and tracing are the student's own work, and they're the muscles everything else depends on. For the rules on AI at each age, see the AI box in **The Computing Hour**.

## What "done enough" looks like

Your student is ready to move on when they can:

- write pseudocode for an everyday job that includes at least one decision (IF) and one loop (REPEAT);
- trace a five-line program on paper and predict what it will say;
- explain, in their own words, why guessing the middle finds a number so fast;
- write an Expected/Actual line before fixing something;
- say the exit lines without prompting: "I predicted… It did… Next I'd change…" and "Help I used: …"

**11–12:** spoken explanations are fine; the pseudocode can be short. **13–14:** written explanations, and at least one comparison of two algorithms by counting steps.

### AS-01 Cold Trace (baseline)

At the end of this chapter, do a first Cold Trace. It's a ten-minute check your student does alone: no help from you, no screen, and no AI. Hand over this program and ask for a prediction of what it says, with a trace table:

```
SET coins TO 4
REPEAT 3 TIMES
    ADD 2 TO coins
IF coins > 8 THEN SAY "Piggy bank full" ELSE SAY "Keep saving"
```

(The answer: `coins` goes 6, 8, 10, so it says "Piggy bank full.")

Date the sheet and file it. Right or wrong, this is a baseline, not a grade. You'll do a Cold Trace once a month, and by spring this first sheet will show your student how far they've come.

If most of the list is in place, move on to Chapter 2, where these plans become real Python. Planning never goes away. It just gets a keyboard.

\newpage

# Chapter 2

## From Blocks to Text: First Programs in Python

![An open laptop whose dark screen shows rows of indented colored bars, the shape of code, beside an index card and a pencil — still life, no people, no logos, no readable text.](art/ch02-opener.jpg){width=100%}

## Why this matters

Many students arrive at this chapter with years of block coding behind them. They've snapped together Scratch games with scores, timers, and sprites that bounce off walls. That was real programming. The ideas in those blocks (steps in order, decisions, loops, variables) are the same ideas inside every program on every computer.

Text code is the next step. Most software is written by typing lines in a programming language, and this chapter starts your student doing exactly that, in Python.

Text brings three new powers. It's precise: every character means something. It's fast: once your fingers know the way, typing a line is quicker than dragging a block. And it's readable by others: your student can read code that other people wrote, and the documentation that explains how a language works.

Text also brings a new kind of trouble. In blocks, pieces only fit where they belong. In text, a missing bracket stops everything, a capital letter in the wrong place is a different word, and the computer replies in red. This chapter is about making that move with no drama and no loss of confidence. Red text is information, not judgment. Your student will learn to read it, find the line it names, change one thing, and run it again.

The habit that makes it all work is the first clause of the focus line, now with real code: predict before you run.

## For the parent: understand it yourself

You can teach this chapter without being a programmer. You need to know what a handful of Python lines do, well enough to read them aloud and ask good questions. Here they are.

### Why Python

Python is a programming language that reads more like English than most. Its makers describe it as "friendly & easy to learn" and open, and it's used for web development, science, education, and games.^[26](#note-26)^ It's free. It's also the language of the home editor this book recommends. Your student won't stay with Python forever, and doesn't need to. The habits learned here move to any language.

### Five ideas, one at a time

**Printing and running.** `print()` shows something on the screen. Running a program means telling the computer to follow its lines from top to bottom.

```python
print("Hello, world!")
```

This prints `Hello, world!` The quotation marks tell Python it's text, not an instruction.

**Variables.** A variable is a labeled box whose contents can be replaced. The equals sign in Python doesn't mean "is equal to forever." It means "put this value in this box now."

```python
snack = "apple"
count = 2
count = count + 1
snack = "pretzel"
print(count, snack)
```

This prints `3 pretzel`. The box called `count` held 2, then 3. The box called `snack` held "apple," then "pretzel." The old contents are gone.

**Types: text versus numbers.** Python treats `"12"` (text, in quotes) and `12` (a number) as different things. You can do arithmetic with a number, not with text. This matters most with `input()`, which asks the person at the keyboard to type something. Python's documentation says it "reads a line from input, converts it to a string," which is programmer talk for text, "and returns that."^[27](#note-27)^ So a typed age arrives as text and has to be turned into a number with `int()`:

```python
name = input("What is your first name? ")
age = input("How old are you? ")
next_year = int(age) + 1
print("Hi,", name)
print("Next year you will be", next_year)
```

If your student types Sam and 12, this prints `Hi, Sam` and `Next year you will be 13`. Leave out `int()` and write `age + 1`, and Python stops with a `TypeError`: it can't add a number to text. That error is one of the most common in the first month, and now you'll recognize it.

**Decisions: if, elif, else.** `if` checks whether something is true. `elif` (short for "else if") checks another possibility. `else` catches everything left over.

```python
answer = input("Left or right? ")
if answer == "left":
    print("You find a quiet library.")
elif answer == "right":
    print("You find a noisy market.")
else:
    print("You stand still. Nothing happens.")
```

Two equals signs (`==`) mean "is this equal?" One equals sign means "put in the box." Type `left` and you find the library. Type `Left`, with a capital, and you stand still, because to Python those are different words. The indented lines, four spaces in, belong to the line above them. In Python, indentation is part of the meaning.

**Loops: for and while.** A `for` loop repeats a set number of times:

```python
for number in range(1, 6):
    print(number * 3)
```

This prints 3, 6, 9, 12, and 15, each on its own line. `range(1, 6)` counts from 1 and stops *before* 6, a detail that trips up nearly everyone at first. A `while` loop repeats as long as something stays true, which is perfect for games that go on until the player wins. You'll see one in the guess-the-number game below.

### Blocks to text: what the research says

Studies of block and text programming have mostly been done with high-school students, so read them as a picture of older beginners. In one, students who started with blocks learned more in the first weeks and were more interested in further computing courses, while students who started with text felt their work was closer to what professional programmers do. A follow-up found the difference had disappeared after the students spent ten weeks in a professional text language.^[28](#note-28),[29](#note-29)^

The practical lesson: blocks were never babyish, and starting text now leaves nobody behind. Scratch is designed especially for ages 8 to 16, so it's fine to keep it around for quick sketches and fun.^[30](#note-30)^ This chapter uses Scratch on purpose in the first weeks, side by side with Python, so your student can see that the ideas they already know have new clothes.

### How to teach code: read before you write

The method this book uses is called PRIMM: **P**redict, **R**un, **I**nvestigate, **M**odify, **M**ake. Your student reads a short working program first and predicts what it will do. Then they run it and compare. Then they investigate ("What happens if I change this number?"), modify it to do something a little different, and finally make something of their own. A large study in schools found that students aged about 11 to 14 who learned this way did a little better on a programming test than students who didn't.^[31](#note-31)^ It is a small edge, not a miracle, and it matches the Hour's shape.

The weekly arc is the same idea in three words: **Use, Modify, Create.** Use a working program, change one part, then build your own. It grew out of programs for middle- and high-school students.^[32](#note-32)^ It also takes the terror out of the blank page.

The standards for this age ask students to use variables of different types, to analyze how loops, decisions, and variables work in a piece of code, and to "use reference documentation in program development."^[17](#note-17)^ PRIMM and Use-Modify-Create cover all three.

### Installing Thonny

Thonny is the book's home editor for Python. It's free, it comes with Python built in, it needs no account, and it runs offline once installed. It has a step-by-step mode that runs a program one piece at a time, and a Variables view that shows what's in every box.^[2](#note-2)^ Install it yourself before your student does, so you've seen the screens. The top half is where you write the program. The bottom half, called the Shell, is where the output and any red error messages appear.

### Wrong answers you should be able to hear

| What you hear | What it usually means | What you can say |
|---|---|---|
| "The computer is broken." | Expected and actual differ, and there's no clue yet. | "It did exactly what we told it. Which line is the last one that did what you expected?" |
| "x = 5 means x equals 5 forever." | Mixing up "put in the box" with "is equal." | "It's a box. What's in the box after line 4?" |
| "I typed it exactly right." | A capital letter, a missing bracket, or a missing colon. | "Read it to me one character at a time." |
| "It says error, so it's all wrong." | One error stops the run, but most lines are fine. | "Which line does it name? Let's start there." |
| "I'll just start over." | Overwhelm. | "Save this as a new version first. Then tell me the one thing that's wrong." |

## How to teach it this week

This week's Hours run on the five steps from **The Computing Hour**, with Python on the screen. Here's how a typical Hour fits:

1. **Unplug and Plan.** Read the day's program on paper, or translate a Scratch script on paper. Ask the talk-box opening.
2. **Predict.** Your student writes a prediction line in the Debug Log for every program before it runs.
3. **Build and Test.** Your student types and runs the program in Thonny, then changes one thing at a time.
4. **Debug and Log.** When it surprises them: an Expected/Actual line first (BUG-01), then read the error's last line and the line number it names, change one thing, and run again. One line in the Debug Log (BUG-08).
5. **Save and Explain.** A new version, date-first name, in two places. "I predicted… It did… Next I'd change…" and "Help I used: …" From this chapter on, "Help I used" is a habit, not an occasional question.

### Exact words you can say

- Opening: "What do you predict this line will do — and why?"
- When the screen goes red: "Read me the last line. What line number does it name?"
- When your student wants to change three things: "Pick one. Run it. Then we'll see."
- When it works: "Before we move on, what did line 3 do?"
- At the end: "Save it, two places. Then tell me: 'I predicted… It did… Next I'd change…' and 'Help I used: …'"

### In-home — BLK-01 Scratch Side-by-Side

- **Time:** about 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** Scratch open on the screen (no account needed to make a project), paper, and Thonny.
- **Safety and privacy:** no sharing projects online; that's a family decision for later.
- **The fun:** discovering that you already know Python's ideas.
- **The skill:** matching each block to a line of Python.
- **The crumb:** a two-column sheet: blocks on the left, Python on the right.

**How it goes.** Build this tiny script in Scratch: *when green flag clicked*, *set score to 0*, *repeat 3*, *change score by 1*, and after the loop, *say score*. Run it. The sprite says 3. Now write the Python beside it:

```python
score = 0
for i in range(3):
    score = score + 1
print(score)
```

Predict, then run it in Thonny. It prints `3`. Draw a line from each block to its Python line. Notice what Python needs that Scratch didn't: a colon at the end of the `for` line, and four spaces before the line inside the loop. Those are the jobs the C-shaped block was doing.

**11–12:** do this twice more with your student's own small scripts. **13–14:** one is enough; move on to BLK-03.

### In-home — PY-01 Hello, Editor

- **Time:** about 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** a computer with Thonny installed.
- **Safety and privacy:** files go in a family folder, with a first name only in any file name.
- **The fun:** making the computer say something you chose.
- **The skill:** opening the editor, `print()`, running, and saving with a name a stranger could read.
- **The crumb:** the first file, named date first.

**How it goes.** If you did this in your first week (How to Use This Book This Week), do it again with a twist. Your student types `print("Hello, world!")`, predicts, and presses Run. Then three more `print` lines, saying anything they like. Save it with the date first, such as `2026-10-12-hello.py`, in two places. Then make a deliberate mistake: delete the closing bracket and run it. Read the red text together, find the line number, and fix it. Your student has now caused, read, and fixed an error on purpose, on day one.

### In-home — PY-02 Predict the Print

- **Time:** about 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** a short program on paper; the Debug Log; Thonny.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** betting against the computer, and sometimes winning.
- **The skill:** predicting the output of a short program, running it, and investigating any surprise.
- **The crumb:** the prediction line and the result line.

**How it goes.** Use the four-line program from **The Computing Hour** (score starts at 3, then line 2 adds 2) or any short program from "For the parent." Your student writes a prediction for every line of output, then types and runs the program, and writes what actually happened beside the prediction. If they differ, investigate: "Which line did something you didn't expect?" Then change one number and predict again.

### In-home — PY-03 Variable Boxes

- **Time:** about 10 minutes.
- **Materials:** sticky notes and a marker.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** being the computer's memory.
- **The skill:** seeing that a new value replaces the old one, and that text and numbers are different.
- **The crumb:** a photo of the boxes after the trace.

**How it goes.** Take the `snack` and `count` program from "For the parent." Each variable gets a sticky note with its name on top. Your student reads the program aloud, line by line.
