Each time a box gets a new value, they cross out the old one, or peel off the note and write a new one. At the end, read what's in each box. Then ask: "What would happen if `count` were `"2"`, in quotes?" Text in quotes can't be added to a number. Writing a word like "TEXT" or "NUMBER" in the corner of each note helps.

### Talk box (this chapter)

**Opening:** "What do you predict this line will do — and why?"

**Follow-ups:**

1. Prediction: "And the line after that?"
2. Evidence: "What in the code makes you think so?"
3. Location: "Which line is the last one that did what you expected?"
4. Change: "What's the one change you'd try first?"
5. Revision: "You can change your prediction. Run it again."

**How to wait.** After you ask, count to five in your head. When the question is about code on the screen, let them read as long as they need. Reading code is slow, even for experienced programmers. Look at the screen, not at your student.

**What a stuck silence usually means.** The program is too long to hold in mind, so cover all but the first two lines with a sheet of paper. Your student doesn't know what one piece means, such as `range` or `==`, and is embarrassed to say so; ask, "Which word on this line is the least familiar?" They're waiting for you to fix it, so put your hands in your lap. Or the red text feels like a verdict. Read the last line aloud yourself, calmly, as if it were a weather report.

**Don't say / say instead.** Don't say "You typed it wrong." Say "Read me line 3, one character at a time."

### This week at a glance

| Day | Focus | Try-it |
|---|---|---|
| Hour 1 | Same ideas, new clothes | BLK-01 Scratch Side-by-Side |
| Hour 2 | The editor, and a first error on purpose | PY-01 Hello, Editor; PY-02 Predict the Print |
| Hour 3 | Boxes that change | PY-03 Variable Boxes, then type and run the program |
| Other days | Ten minutes | PLAN-07 Paper Parsons with Python lines; a typing warm-up |
| Errand | A full Hour away from home | OUT-02 Library Computer Hour |

## Practice that actually builds learning

Over the next four to six weeks, work through these roughly in order. Each new idea gets a week or so of practice before the next arrives.

### In-home — BLK-03 Block-to-Line Translator

- **Time:** about 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** two or three Scratch scripts drawn or printed on paper, and a pencil.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** cracking a code you already half know.
- **The skill:** translating blocks into Python on paper.
- **The crumb:** the translated lines.

**How it goes.** Give your student a Scratch script on paper, such as *ask "What's your name?" and wait*, then *say (join "Hi, " (answer))*. Their job is to write the Python without a computer: `name = input("What's your name? ")` and `print("Hi,", name)`. Then try one with *if … then … else*. Type the translation into Thonny afterward to check. **13–14:** translate a script with a loop inside a decision, and get the indentation right on paper first.

### In-home — BLK-02 Remix and Explain

*For 11–12.*

- **Time:** about 25 minutes.
- **Materials:** a working Scratch project, opened from your family's files or the Scratch site, with you present.
- **Safety and privacy:** no sharing, comments, or account sign-ups during this activity. If you open a project someone else made, it stays a private remix.
- **The fun:** changing one rule in a game and seeing what breaks.
- **The skill:** Use, Modify, Create: changing one part of a working program and explaining the change.
- **The crumb:** the saved remix, with a credit line naming the original maker.

**How it goes.** Your student plays the project, then changes exactly one rule: the speed, the score for a hit, the number of lives. Before running it, they predict what will be different. Afterward, they explain the change in one sentence and write a credit line: "Original by ___. I changed ___." That credit line is the start of a habit the whole book cares about.

### In-home — PY-04 Ask and Answer

- **Time:** about 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** Thonny.
- **Safety and privacy:** use a first name, or a made-up one, in any program that asks a name.
- **The fun:** a program that talks back.
- **The skill:** `input()`, text versus numbers, and `int()`.
- **The crumb:** the program file.

**How it goes.** Start with the name-and-age program from "For the parent." Predict, run, and type Sam and 12 (or any first name). Then plant the bug on purpose: change `int(age) + 1` to `age + 1` and predict what will happen. Run it, and read the `TypeError` together. The last line of the error names the problem. Fix it. Then modify: ask for a favorite number and print double it.

### In-home — PY-05 Choose Your Path

- **Time:** about 30 minutes.
- **Materials:** paper for a path map; Thonny.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** a tiny text adventure where the player's choices matter.
- **The skill:** `if`, `elif`, and `else`.
- **The crumb:** the program file plus the path map.

**How it goes.** Start with the left-or-right program from "For the parent." Before typing, your student draws a path map on paper: a box for each choice and an arrow for each answer. Then they predict what happens for "left," "right," "Left," and "banana," and run all four. The "Left" surprise is the lesson: to Python, a capital letter makes a different word. Then modify: add a third path, or a second question inside one path. **13–14:** make the program accept "Left" too, and explain how.

### In-home — PY-06 Loop It

- **Time:** about 25 minutes.
- **Materials:** Thonny; the Debug Log.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** making the computer do a hundred things with two lines.
- **The skill:** `for` loops with `range()`, and counting patterns.
- **The crumb:** the program file.

**How it goes.** Start with the three-times-table loop from "For the parent." Predict all five lines of output before running. Then change `range(1, 6)` to `range(1, 11)` and predict the last number. Many first predictions include the stopping number. That's the lesson: `range` stops just before it. Then modify: print the five times table, or count down from 10.

### In-home — PY-07 Turtle Patterns

- **Time:** about 30 minutes.
- **Materials:** Thonny.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** a program that draws.
- **The skill:** loops that draw shapes, using Python's turtle module.
- **The crumb:** a screenshot of the pattern.

**How it goes.** Python's turtle module is a little on-screen pen that moves and turns when told. It comes from the drawing tools of Logo, an educational language from 1967.^[33](#note-33)^

```python
import turtle

pen = turtle.Turtle()
for side in range(4):
    pen.forward(100)
    pen.right(90)
turtle.done()
```

This opens a window and draws a square, 100 steps on each side. Before running it, your student predicts the shape. Then: what would make a triangle? (Three sides, turning 120 each time.) A hexagon? A star? Let them predict the angle before each run. **13–14:** draw a square inside a loop that turns a little after each square, and predict the pattern before running it.

### In-home — PY-08 Guess-the-Number Game

- **Time:** about 40 minutes, across two weeks.
- **Materials:** Thonny; the Debug Log.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** a real game, written by your student, that a sibling can play.
- **The skill:** a `while` loop, random numbers, and decisions, in a Use-Modify-Create project.
- **The crumb:** the version 1 and version 2 files.

**How it goes.** This is Chapter 1's Guess My Number, as a program. Week one is Use: your student types this, predicts what will happen, and plays it.

```python
import random

secret = random.randint(1, 20)
guess = 0
while guess != secret:
    guess = int(input("Guess a number from 1 to 20: "))
    if guess < secret:
        print("Too low.")
    elif guess > secret:
        print("Too high.")
print("You got it!")
```

`random.randint(1, 20)` picks a whole number from 1 to 20, including both ends.^[34](#note-34)^ `!=` means "is not equal to." The loop keeps asking until the guess matches. Ask: "Using the halving trick, what's the most guesses you should ever need?" (Five.)

Week two is Modify and Create. Save the first version, then save a copy as version 2 and change it: a range of 1 to 100, a different message for each try, or a limit on guesses. **11–12:** one change, predicted and tested. **13–14:** add a guess counter that reports "You got it in 4 guesses," and a question at the end that offers to play again. Chapter 3 comes back to this game to debug it.

### PLAN-05 and PLAN-07, now in Python

Chapter 1's trace tables and Paper Parsons work just as well with Python. For a trace table (PLAN-05), use the `snack` and `count` program, with a column for each variable. For a Paper Parsons (PLAN-07), cut the guess-the-number game into strips, one line each, mix them, and have your student put them back in order, keeping each strip's indentation. Both make good ten-minute practice on days without a full Hour.

### Out-of-home — OUT-02 Library Computer Hour

- **Time:** about 45 minutes.
- **Materials:** a public library computer, your own USB stick, a notebook, and the day's program on paper.
- **Safety and privacy:** you sit with your student the whole time. Save only to your own USB stick, not to the library computer. Sign in to nothing personal, and log out and close the browser before you leave. See Family safety basics in **The Computing Hour**.
- **The fun:** a change of scene, and proof that your skills travel.
- **The skill:** running a full Computing Hour on a computer that isn't yours, and saving to your own drive.
- **The crumb:** the file, saved in two places once you're home.

**How it goes.** Before you go, check what the library computers allow: some have Python installed, some allow a browser editor, and some allow neither. Run the five steps as usual: plan on paper, predict in the notebook, build and test, debug and log, and save to your USB stick. At home, copy the file to its second place. For a family with no computer at home, this is how the screen half of this book happens.

### Blocked, then mixed

**Blocked practice for the new move.** For a week, every Hour practices the newest idea: a week of variables, then a week of decisions, then a week of loops.

**Mixed practice.** Once loops arrive, mix. A good Hour might hold a Predict the Print with a decision in it, a short trace table, and fifteen minutes on the game. Mixing teaches your student to recognize which tool a problem needs.

### One incorrect example to diagnose

Here is an illustration, not a real family. A thirteen-year-old's folder holds `game.py`, `game2.py`, `final.py`, `final_final.py`, and `final_final2.py`. The version that worked last Tuesday is in one of them. Nobody knows which.

**Diagnosis.** No date in the names, no way to tell what changed, and nothing saved in a second place.

**Rescue.** Date first, then a short description, then a version number: `2026-10-19-guess-game-v2.py`. One line in the Debug Log says what changed. Every save goes to two places.

## For the student

You've probably made things with blocks. Now you're going to type.

Python is a real programming language, used by people who build websites, run science experiments, and make games. It reads a lot like English. But typing code is fussier than snapping blocks together, and the computer is completely literal about it. Here's what you need to know.

**Capitals matter.** `print` works. `Print` doesn't. To Python, they're two different words, and it only knows one of them. If you type `Print("hi")`, you'll get an error that says `name 'Print' is not defined`. Python is telling you it's never heard of `Print`. Recent versions even suggest the fix.

**Brackets come in pairs.** Every `(` needs a `)`. Every opening quotation mark needs a closing one. When one is missing, Python stops and tells you which line the problem is on.

**Indentation means something.** In Scratch, the C-shaped blocks showed which steps were inside a loop. In Python, the indentation does that job. Lines that belong inside a loop or an `if` start four spaces in. Forget them, and you'll see an `IndentationError` whose message starts `expected an indented block`.

**A tiny example.** Read this, and predict exactly what it prints before you read on:

```python
lives = 3
for turn in range(2):
    lives = lives - 1
print("Lives left:", lives)
```

It prints `Lives left: 1`. The loop runs twice, and each time it takes one away. If you predicted 0, check `range(2)`: it runs two times, not three. If you predicted the message would print twice, look at the indentation: `print` isn't indented, so it's outside the loop and runs once, at the end.

**Red text is information, not judgment.** Everyone who programs sees error messages every day. Here's how to read one. Look at the last line first; it says what kind of problem it is. Then find the line number. Go to that line, and also check the line just above it, because sometimes the real mistake is a little earlier. Change one thing. Run it again.

**Reading the docs.** Python has official documentation that explains every built-in function. When you meet a new one, look it up and read just the first sentence or two. For `input()`, the docs say it reads a line and returns it as a string, which means text. That one sentence explains why you need `int()` to do math with what someone types. You don't have to understand the whole page. Looking things up is what programmers do.

**This part is hard for everyone.** The first weeks of typing code are slow and full of small errors. Missing colons, missing brackets, a capital where it shouldn't be. Every programmer has been there, and the errors get rarer surprisingly fast.

**Help I used.** From now on, at the end of every Hour, you'll say (or write, if you're 13 or older) what help you used: a book, the docs, a parent, a sibling, a tutorial. "None" is a fine answer when it's true. Saying it every time is how you keep your work honestly yours.

**Try this today.** Write a program that asks someone's favorite animal and then prints a sentence using it. Predict what it'll print before you run it. Then break it on purpose: delete a bracket, run it, and read the error. Fix it.

**Explain it back.** Show someone your program and explain each line in your own words. If there's a line you can't explain, that's the line to look at next.

**If you're 13 or older:** look up one function you haven't used yet in the Python documentation, such as `len()` or `round()`. Read the first sentence, predict what it does in a small program, and test it.

**Challenge.** Change the guess-the-number game so it tells you how many guesses you took. Predict where in the program the counting line has to go before you try it.

**Save like someone will need to find it.** Name every file with the date first, then a few words, then a version number: `2026-10-19-guess-game-v2.py`. When you're about to make a big change, save a copy as the next version first. Then if the change goes badly, the version that worked is still there, and you can compare the two to find exactly what broke. A file called `final_final2.py` helps nobody, including you next month.

**What to save.** Every program, with a date-first name, in two places. Your Debug Log entries. Your version 1 and version 2 of the game.

## If it isn't clicking

**Typing is slow and painful.** That's normal for the first month. Do five-minute typing warm-ups on other days, and keep programs short, under ten lines. Typing speeds up faster than you'd think.

**Indentation errors keep coming.** Count the spaces with your finger: four per level. If your editor can show spaces as dots, turn that on. Read the line above the error; it often ends in a colon that wants indented lines below it.

**"I liked Scratch better."** Keep a Scratch project going alongside Python, for fun. Blocks aren't the enemy. Do one more Scratch Side-by-Side so the connection stays visible.

**Racing ahead and skipping Predict.** No run without a written prediction line. Hands on the table until it's there. The prediction is the part that builds understanding; the run just checks it.

**Error messages cause panic.** Read the last line aloud yourself, calmly. Then ask only, "What line number does it name?" That's the whole first job.

**Your student is far ahead.** Let them read the docs for a new function and teach it to you. Your role is the same: ask for the prediction, be the first user, and ask for the explanation.

## Tools, including AI

**Thonny** is the home default: free, with Python built in, no account, and offline once installed.^[2](#note-2)^

**Raspberry Pi Code Editor** runs Python in a web browser and can be used without creating an account. Saving projects online needs an account, and for creators under 13, a parent or guardian sets it up with their own email address. These rules have been changing, so check the current terms before you start.^[35](#note-35)^ It's a good choice for a library computer or a borrowed laptop.

**CodeAI (formerly Code.org) Python Lab** runs Python in the browser and is designed for students aged 13 and up.^[36](#note-36)^ Use it at 13–14 only, with you present.

**Not Trinket.** It shut down on 31 August 2026, and older guides still point to it.^[37](#note-37)^

**No Replit or GitHub accounts at 11–12.** Both require users to be at least 13, and Replit asks for a parent's permission under 18.^[38](#note-38),[39](#note-39)^ Neither is needed for this chapter.

**AI: none in this chapter, at either age.** At 11–12, there are no coding assistants or AI tutors anyway. At 13–14, keep the first text weeks AI-free too, so your student builds the reading muscle that everything else depends on: reading a line, predicting it, and reading the error. AI tools come back, supervised, in later chapters. The AS checks below are always AI-free. For the rules at each age, see the AI box in **The Computing Hour**.

## What "done enough" looks like

Your student is ready to move on when they can:

- write and run a program that asks a question, stores the answer, decides with `if`/`elif`/`else`, and repeats with a loop;
- predict the output of a short program before running it;
- read an error message's last line and find the line it names;
- save files with date-first names a stranger could read, in two places;
- say "Help I used: …" at the end of every Hour without being asked.

**11–12:** programs of five to ten lines; reading code aloud together is fine. **13–14:** a `while` loop and random numbers; reads the documentation for one new function alone; the guess-the-number game with a counter and replay.

**The checks (always AI-free).** Keep the monthly Cold Trace (AS-01), now with a short Python program. Add a Debug on the Spot (AS-02): plant one bug, such as a missing `int()` or a capital `Print`, and have your student fix it while explaining aloud. Then an Explain Your Code (AS-03): your student explains their own game, line by line, to you. If they can explain every line, it's theirs. Write a one-line note on each, dated, for the portfolio.

If most of the list is in place, move on to Chapter 3, where debugging becomes a discipline of its own.

\newpage

# Chapter 3

## Debugging as a Discipline: Errors, Tests, and the Debug Log

![An open Debug Log notebook with dated scribble lines and a boxed check mark, a red mug, a magnifying glass and a pencil — still life, no people, no logos, no readable text.](art/ch03-opener.jpg){width=100%}

## Why this matters

Here is an illustration, not a real family. Picture a twelve-year-old who has spent forty minutes on a guessing game. She presses Run. The screen fills with red text. She pushes her chair back, says "It's broken," and reaches for the key that deletes everything. This is one of the most important moments in learning to program, and it looks like failure. It isn't. It's the start of the real work.

A bug is a mistake in the instructions. Debugging is finding and fixing it. Every program bigger than a few lines has bugs on the way to working, and professionals plan for that. Two experienced programmers put it plainly in a well-known book for working developers: no one writes perfect software, so debugging will take up a big part of the day.^[40](#note-40)^ That isn't a confession. It's a job description.

This chapter is the middle of the book's focus line: **Predict before you run; debug before you rewrite; explain it before you call it yours.** Chapter 2 taught your student to predict what a line will do. This chapter teaches what to do when the prediction and the screen disagree. The national computer science standards for ages 11–14 describe the same skill in plain words: use systematic ways to test a program, improve it, and write down the changes.^[17](#note-17)^ "Systematic" is the key word. Debugging is a method, not a mood.

The word has a story your student will enjoy. People called small faults in machines "bugs" long before computers; Thomas Edison talked about bugs in electrical circuits in the 1870s. In 1947, engineers working on the Mark II computer at Harvard found a moth stuck in one of its parts, taped it into their logbook, and labeled it "first actual case of bug being found." The Smithsonian, which now holds that logbook, says it was probably not Grace Hopper's own, but she and the rest of the team helped make "bug" and "debug" ordinary words.^[41](#note-41)^ The engineers did something worth copying: they wrote it down. By the end of this chapter, your student will keep a Debug Log of their own: a dated notebook (paper or a plain file) that holds predictions, bugs, clues, and fixes.

This chapter also carries the feelings that come with mistakes. Students this age care a great deal about how they look to others, and red text can feel like a grade on them rather than a note about the code.^[19](#note-19)^ You can change that. The way you react to the first error message of the week teaches more than anything on this page.

## For the parent: understand it yourself

You don't need to be a programmer to teach debugging. You need a calm voice, a few good questions, and the habit of looking at the code instead of the clock.

**The everyday picture.** A cake comes out flat. You don't throw away the kitchen. You ask what you expected (a tall cake), what happened (a flat one), and what the best clue is (the baking powder tin is still sealed). Then you change one thing next time. Debugging is that, written down.

**The precise picture.** Python can go wrong in three ways, and it helps to name them:

- A **syntax error** means the computer can't read it. A colon or a parenthesis is missing, so Python stops before running anything.
- A **runtime error** means it crashes while running. The program starts, reaches a line it can't carry out, and stops with a message.
- A **logic error** means it runs and does the wrong thing. No red text at all. The answer is simply wrong.

Here is a two-line program with a syntax error:

```python
score = 0
if score > 10
    print("Big score")
```

Python stops and reports `SyntaxError: expected ':'`, with a small arrow under the end of line 2. Add the colon and it runs.

Here is a runtime error:

```python
score = 5
print("Score: " + score)
```

The program starts and then stops on line 2 with `TypeError: can only concatenate str (not "int") to str`. In plain words: you tried to glue words to a number. Writing `str(score)` turns the number into text, and the line works.

And here is a logic error:

```python
average = 3 + 6 + 9 / 3
print(average)
```

It prints `12.0`. The student expected `6.0`. Python did the division first, the way math rules say it should, so only the 9 was divided. Parentheses fix it: `(3 + 6 + 9) / 3` prints `6.0`. Nothing turned red. Only a prediction caught it, which is why the book keeps asking for one.

**How to read the red text.** The Python documentation gives the two habits that matter most. The last line of an error message says what happened. The message also names the line where Python noticed the problem, but that is not always the place that needs fixing; a missing parenthesis on line 4 can be reported on line 5.^[42](#note-42)^ So the order is: read the last line first, then the line number, then look at that line and the one above it. Error wording can differ slightly between Python versions. Thonny, the free beginner's editor this book uses, comes with its own recent Python, so your screen may not match these examples word for word.^[2](#note-2)^

**The method.** Professional debugging comes down to a short loop, and your student can learn every step:

1. Write the Expected/Actual line: "I expected… It did…"
2. Read the clue: the error message, or the wrong output.
3. Make one guess about the cause.
4. Change one thing.
5. Run it again and look.
6. Write it in the Debug Log.

The "one thing" matters. The same developers' book notes that if you changed only one thing and the program stopped working, that one thing is probably responsible.^[40](#note-40)^ Change five things and you learn nothing, even if it starts working.

**Tests before trouble.** A test table lists inputs and the outputs you expect before you run anything. The best rows are the awkward ones: zero, a negative number, an empty answer, a very large number. Programmers call these edge cases, and the developers' book advises testing boundary conditions hard.^[40](#note-40)^ A program that works for 5 and breaks for 0 isn't finished.

**Tools for seeing inside.** Two help most. A *print detective* adds temporary `print()` lines that show what a variable holds at each step, then deletes them when the bug is found. Thonny also has a step-through debugger. Press Ctrl+F5 instead of F5 and the program runs one step at a time; F6 takes a big step and F7 a small one. Choosing View, then Variables, shows every labeled box and its contents as the program runs.^[2](#note-2)^

**The rubber duck.** Many programmers explain their code, line by line, out loud, to a rubber duck. This is a habit from working practice, not a research finding. The developers' book tells of a colleague who kept a small rubber duck on his computer while he coded, and says that the simple act of explaining what the code is supposed to do "often causes the problem to leap off the screen and announce itself." If no person is around, they add, "a rubber duck, or teddy bear, or potted plant will do."^[40](#note-40)^ It works because saying each step forces you to notice the step you skipped.

**Saving versions.** A student who saves `2026-10-19-guess-game-v1.py`, then `2026-10-20-guess-game-v2.py`, then `v3`, date first, with one line in a changelog for each and every file saved in two places, can always go back. Nobody has to fear trying a change.

**The feelings.** Expect some perfectionism, some "I'm bad at this," and at least one slammed laptop lid. Keep your feedback about the code: "Line 7 is missing something," not "You always forget." Praise the find, not only the fix: "You spotted that the arrow was one line late. That's real debugging."

### Wrong answers you should be able to hear

- **"The computer is broken."** It almost never is. Reply: "Maybe. What did you expect, and what did it do?" The computer did exactly what it was told. That's the problem, and also the good news.
- **"I'll just start over."** A fresh file throws away the clue along with the bug. Reply: "Deleting the file is not a strategy. Let's read the last line of the message first."
- **"It works, so it's right."** It worked for one input. Reply: "What happens with zero? With a negative number? Let's write three tests."
- **"I fixed it."** But the student can't say what changed. Reply: "Great. Which line, and why did that fix it? Put it in the log."
- **"Python is stupid."** Frustration talking. Reply: "It's very literal. Let's find the line where it stopped doing what you meant."

### Your five-minute warm-up

Tomorrow, before the student arrives, type the three short programs above into Thonny and run each one. Read the last line of each message aloud. Then practice this chapter's opening question once, out loud, and practice saying nothing afterward while you count to five in your head. That's the whole preparation.

## How to teach it this week

Run the week on the usual five steps of the Computing Hour. Here is how each step leans toward debugging.

- **Unplug and Plan (5–10 minutes).** On paper, write the Expected/Actual line format and the Debug Log headings: date, prediction, bug, clue, fix, what I learned.
- **Predict (2–5 minutes).** Before running, the student says what will appear on screen, including "it will crash on line 3" if they suspect it.
- **Build and Test.** The student runs the program, compares it with the prediction, and fills in a test table.
- **Debug and Log.** One bug, one guess, one change, one log line.
- **Save and Explain.** The student saves a new version and says: "I predicted… It did… Next I'd change…" and "Help I used: …" (even if it's "none").

### In-home — BUG-01 Expected/Actual Line

- **Time:** 5 minutes, every time something goes wrong.
- **Materials:** the Debug Log (a notebook or a single document) and a pencil.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** it turns "ugh" into a sentence, which calms everyone down.
- **The skill:** writing what you expected and what happened before touching the code.
- **The crumb:** one Debug Log line.

Try this exact sentence the first time the screen surprises your student: "Before you change anything, finish this: 'I expected… It did…'" Write it on a card and prop it against the screen for the first two weeks. The rule is simple: no keyboard until the line is written.

**11–12:** You can write it while they say it. **13–14:** They write it themselves, and you only check that it exists.

### In-home — BUG-02 Read the Red Text

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** Thonny, the three error programs from the parent section, and an index card.
- **Safety and privacy:** offline; nothing to sign up for.
- **The fun:** decoding the message like a spy reading a note.
- **The skill:** finding the error type and the line number, and knowing which kind of error it is.
- **The crumb:** an error decoder card.

Run each broken program. For each one, the student copies the last line onto the card and writes a plain translation beside it. Add a fourth: the `IndentationError` that appears when the line under an `if` isn't pushed in. (Python 3.13 reports `expected an indented block after 'if' statement on line 2`; other versions word it slightly differently.) Add a fifth: misspell `score` as `scroe` and get a `NameError`. Recent versions even suggest `Did you mean: 'score'?` A finished card might read:

- `SyntaxError` = it can't read this line. Look for a missing colon, parenthesis, or quote.
- `IndentationError` = the spacing is wrong. Lines inside an `if` or loop move in four spaces.
- `NameError` = I used a name Python doesn't know. Check spelling and capitals.
- `TypeError` = I mixed things that don't mix, like words and numbers.

Thonny highlights unclosed quotes and parentheses, which it calls the most common beginner syntax errors.^[2](#note-2)^ Point that out; it's a free clue.

### In-home — BUG-08 The Debug Log

- **Time:** 5 minutes at the end of each Hour.
- **Materials:** a dated notebook (paper or a plain file) that holds predictions, bugs, clues, and fixes, kept beside the computer or in the student's project folder.
- **Safety and privacy:** first names only if it's ever shown to anyone.
- **The fun:** watching the list grow is satisfying, and old entries are funny a month later.
- **The skill:** writing down the bug, the clue, the fix, and what was learned, with the date.
- **The crumb:** the monthly log page.

A good entry is short:

> 6 Oct. Bug: game said "Too high" when I was too low. Clue: both messages said "Too high." Fix: line 11 now says "Too low." Learned: copy-paste can copy the mistake too.

Tell your student, more than once, that a full Debug Log is evidence of good work. A blank one usually means the bugs got fixed in silence, or not at all.

### How to fade your help

Follow the rescue order: **ask → wait → hint → short demo on your own copy → hand it back.** Ask the opening question. Wait. If silence goes on, give a hint that points to a place, not an answer: "Look at line 7 and the line above it." If that fails, fix a *similar* bug in your own copy while they watch, then hand the keyboard back. The student's file stays the student's.

**11–12:** Expect to reach "hint" often in the first week. Co-write test tables together. Plant two bugs, not three, for the planted-bug game.

**13–14:** The student leads the Hour and you are **first user**: you try the program like a stranger, report what happened, and fix nothing. Plant three bugs, including one logic bug. Then swap: the student plants bugs for you.

**Ready 12:** A twelve-year-old who reads error messages without being asked is ready for the logic-bug round.

### A worked Hour (illustration)

Here is an illustration, not a real family. Picture a thirteen-year-old and his father on a Wednesday. In Unplug and Plan, the son copies the Debug Log headings into his notebook. In Predict, his father points to a guessing game the son wrote last week and asks, "What will happen when you type 5?" The son says, "Too low or too high, then another guess." He runs it. It crashes on line 10 with `TypeError: '<' not supported between instances of 'str' and 'int'`.

His father asks the opening question and counts to five in his head. The son reads the last line twice. "It says I'm comparing a string and a number. The guess is a string." He remembers from Chapter 2 that `input()` always gives text. He changes one thing: `guess = int(input("Guess 1 to 10: "))`. He runs it. It works. He writes the log entry, saves `2026-10-21-guess-game-v2.py`, and says, "I predicted it would say too low or too high. It crashed. Next I'd change the message so it tells you how many tries are left. Help I used: none." Twenty minutes, one bug, one clear win.

## Practice that actually builds learning

### In-home — BUG-03 Planted Bugs

- **Time:** 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** a working program saved under a new name, Thonny, and a sheet for the bug list.
- **Safety and privacy:** offline.
- **The fun:** a treasure hunt where you made the treasure.
- **The skill:** finding three bugs a parent planted in working code.
- **The crumb:** a bug list with line numbers.

Start from a program that works. You plant the bugs in a copy. (At 13–14, the student plants some for you, too.) Here is a guessing game with three planted bugs:

```python
import random

secret = random.randint(1, 10)
guess = 0
tries = 0

while guess != secret
    guess = int(input("Guess 1 to 10: "))
    tries = tries + 1
    if guess < secret:
        print("Too high")
    elif guess > secret:
        print("Too high")

print("Got it in", trys, "tries")
```

Python first stops at line 7 with `SyntaxError: expected ':'`. Fix that and the game runs, but it says "Too high" for a low guess (line 11, a logic bug). When the player finally guesses right, it crashes on line 15 with `NameError: name 'trys' is not defined`. One bug of each kind. The student writes each one on the list: line, kind, fix.

**11–12:** Plant two bugs, one syntax and one runtime. Tell them how many there are. **13–14:** Plant three, including a logic bug, and don't say which line. Then the student plants bugs in a working program for you, and you narrate your hunt. Watching a parent read an error message calmly is a lesson in itself.

### In-home — BUG-04 Rubber Duck Explain

- **Time:** 10 minutes.
- **Materials:** a rubber duck, a stuffed animal, or a houseplant, plus the stuck program.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** it's silly, and it works anyway.
- **The skill:** explaining code line by line out loud.
- **The crumb:** one sentence: the line where I found it.

Say once, plainly, that this is a working programmer's habit, not a classroom game. The student reads each line to the duck and says what it does: "Line 9 adds one to tries. Line 10 checks if the guess is too small…" The rule is every line, no skipping the "obvious" ones, because the bug usually lives in an obvious one. Your job is to leave the room or stay silent.

### In-home — BUG-05 One Change at a Time

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** a buggy program and a change log (three columns: change, result, keep or undo).
- **Safety and privacy:** offline.
- **The fun:** a controlled experiment, like a science lab.
- **The skill:** change one thing, run it again, write it down.
- **The crumb:** the change log.

Give the student a program with one stubborn logic bug. The rule is one edit per run, logged. If a change doesn't help, undo it before trying the next. Students who change five things at once sometimes get lucky, but they can't explain why it works, and the next bug beats them.

### In-home — BUG-06 Test Table First

- **Time:** 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** paper, a pencil, and the program about to be written.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** trying to break your own program before anyone else can.
- **The skill:** writing inputs and expected outputs before coding, including edge cases (zero, negative, empty, very large).
- **The crumb:** a test table with pass/fail marks.

Before writing a small program that gives a reaction to a score, the student fills in a table:

| Input | Expected | Actual | Pass? |
|---|---|---|---|
| 12 | Big score | | |
| 10 | Keep going | | |
| 0 | Keep going | | |
| -3 | That can't be right | | |
| (nothing typed) | Asks again | | |

The row for 10 is the interesting one: is 10 a big score or not? Writing the table forces the decision before the code hides it. **11–12:** Write the table together. **13–14:** The student writes it alone, with at least two edge cases you didn't suggest.

### In-home — BUG-07 Print Detective

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** Thonny and a program that gives a wrong number.
- **Safety and privacy:** offline.
- **The fun:** spying on your own variables.
- **The skill:** adding temporary `print()` lines to watch values change.
- **The crumb:** a before/after snippet.

This program is meant to add 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 + 5:

```python
total = 0
for number in range(1, 5):
    print("number is", number, "total is", total)
    total = total + number
print(total)
```

The student expected 15. It prints 10. The detective line (line 3) shows `number` going 1, 2, 3, 4 and then stopping. The clue is in plain sight: `range(1, 5)` stops before 5. Changing it to `range(1, 6)` prints 15. Then the detective line comes out. Save the before and after.

Show the same bug with Thonny's step-through (Ctrl+F5, then F7 for small steps, with View, then Variables, open). Some students like the printed trail; others like watching the boxes change. Both are real tools.

### In-home — PROJ-02 Version Saves and Changelog

- **Time:** 10 minutes.
- **Materials:** the project folder and a plain text file called `changelog.txt`.
- **Safety and privacy:** save in two places (see Family safety basics in **The Computing Hour**).
- **The fun:** a time machine for code.
- **The skill:** saving v1, v2, v3 with a one-line changelog.
- **The crumb:** the changelog file.

Before any big change, the student saves a copy with today's date first and the next version number, like `2026-10-20-guess-game-v2.py`, and writes one line in the changelog: `2026-10-20 v2: guess is now a number; fixed crash on line 10.` If v3 goes badly, v2 is waiting. Both live in two places.

### In-home — PY-08 Guess-the-Number Game

- **Time:** 40 minutes over two Hours.
- **Materials:** Thonny and the guessing game from Chapter 2.
- **Safety and privacy:** offline.
- **The fun:** a game the family will actually play.
- **The skill:** a `while` loop, random numbers, and `if` checks, now debugged and improved.
- **The crumb:** version 1 and version 2 files.

Use the game as this week's lab. The student makes one improvement per version (a tries counter, a limit of five guesses, a "play again?" question) and logs every bug met along the way. The family plays as **first user**, one at a time. Siblings are excellent at typing "banana" when asked for a number, which is a test case worth having.

### In-home — AS-02 Debug on the Spot

- **Time:** 15 minutes, about once a month.
- **Materials:** a short program with one planted bug the student hasn't seen.
- **Safety and privacy:** offline; no AI, no notes beyond the error decoder card.
- **The fun:** a calm show of skill.
- **The skill:** fixing a planted bug while explaining aloud.
- **The crumb:** your one-line note.

The student reads the program aloud, predicts, runs it, and fixes the bug while saying what they're doing. You write one line: "Found the missing colon in under two minutes; read the last line first without being asked." This check is always AI-free.

### Out-of-home — OUT-01 Algorithms in the Wild

- **Time:** 20 minutes on a walk or an errand.
- **Materials:** a pocket notebook.
- **Safety and privacy:** road rules; stay together in stores and parking lots; no photos of strangers or staff.
- **The fun:** catching machines making mistakes.
- **The skill:** writing pseudocode (a plan for a program written in plain words) for a machine you meet, then finding its bug.
- **The crumb:** a pseudocode page with the bug circled.

This week the walk becomes a "bug in the world" hunt. Look for systems that follow rules and sometimes get them wrong: a self-checkout that says "unexpected item" when nothing is there, a crosswalk button that seems to do nothing, an automatic door that opens for a passing leaf. The student writes the likely rule ("IF something moves near the sensor THEN open") and then the Expected/Actual line for the mistake. At home, they suggest one change to the rule. Keep it about machines, not people, and keep guesses labeled as guesses.

### Talk box (this chapter)

**Opening:** "What did you expect, what happened instead, and what's your best clue?"

**Follow-ups:**
- Prediction: "If you make that change, what do you think it will do?"
- Evidence: "What did you see that makes you say that?"
- Location: "Which line is the last one that did what you expected?"
- Change: "What's the one thing you'd change first?"
- Revision: "You can change your prediction. Run it again and see."

**How to wait.** After asking, count to five in your head. When the question is about code on the screen, let them read as long as they need. Look at the code, not at the student. That simple shift tells them you're both working on the program, not watching them.^[5](#note-5)^

**What a stuck silence usually means.** The question was too vague ("What's wrong?"), so make it smaller: "Read me the last line of the message." There may have been no wait time. They may fear looking foolish, which is strong at this age, so keep every comment about the code. They may be waiting for you to fix it, because you have before. There may have been no prediction, so nothing to compare. Or the screen is louder than the question; lower the lid for a moment and talk on paper.

**Don't say / say instead.** Don't say "That's wrong." Say "Show me the line where it stops doing what you expected."

### Blocked, then mixed

For the first week, practice one error type at a time: a day of syntax errors, a day of runtime errors, a day of logic bugs. Blocked practice builds the decoder card. In the second week, mix them. Hand over three programs without saying which kind of error each has. Naming the kind is half the skill, and only mixed practice teaches it.

### One incorrect example to diagnose

Here is an illustration, not a real family. Show your student this folder, from an imaginary student:

`game.py`, `game2.py`, `game_final.py`, `game_final_final2.py`, `game_NEW.py`

Ask: "Which one works? Which one has the fix for the crash? What would you do differently?" Let them find the problem (no way to tell what changed, or which is newest) and propose the fix: numbered versions and a changelog line for each. Students who laugh at this folder rarely build one.

### Siblings and co-ops

Planted bugs work beautifully across ages. An older sibling plants, a younger one hunts, and the planter must explain each bug afterward. In a co-op, pairs swap programs and fill in each other's test tables. The rule for everyone: talk about the code, not the coder.

## For the student

Here's something true: every programmer you have ever heard of has seen thousands of red error messages. They didn't get good by avoiding errors. They got good at reading them.

A bug is a mistake in the instructions. Debugging is finding and fixing it. Bugs come in three kinds:

- A **syntax error** means Python can't read your code. It stops before running anything. Usually a missing colon, parenthesis, or quote.
- A **runtime error** means the program starts, then crashes on a line it can't do.
- A **logic error** means the program runs fine and gives the wrong answer. These are the sneaky ones, because nothing turns red.

**How to read red text.** Start at the bottom. The last line names the error and says what happened. Then find the line number. Look at that line, and the line above it, because Python sometimes notices a mistake one line late.

Here's a worked example. You run this:

```python
score = 5
print("Score: " + score)
```

The last line of the message says `TypeError: can only concatenate str (not "int") to str`. Translate it: "You tried to stick words and a number together." Line 2 is where it happened. Your guess: the number needs to become text. Change one thing: `print("Score: " + str(score))`. Run it. It prints `Score: 5`. Write it in your Debug Log:

> Date. Bug: crash on line 2. Clue: TypeError, words plus number. Fix: str(score). Learned: + only joins text to text.

That's the whole method: **Expected/Actual, clue, guess, one change, run, log.**

**One change at a time.** When something's wrong, it's tempting to change five things at once. If it starts working, you won't know which change did it. If it gets worse, you won't know which change broke it. One change, one run, every time.

**Test your program before someone else does.** Before you write a program, write a test table: some inputs and what you expect. Include the awkward ones. What if someone types 0? A negative number? Nothing at all? A huge number? Programs usually work for the easy cases. The edges are where bugs live.

**Spy on your variables.** If a number comes out wrong, add a temporary line like `print("total is", total)` inside your loop and watch it change. Or press Ctrl+F5 in Thonny and step through one line at a time with F7. Delete the spy lines when you're done.

**Talk to the duck.** Explain your code out loud, line by line, to a rubber duck or a stuffed animal. Professional programmers really do this. Saying every step forces you to notice the one you skipped.

**Keep the log.** A Debug Log is a dated notebook (paper or a plain file) that holds predictions, bugs, clues, and fixes. Add one line on what you learned. It takes two minutes. Months from now, you'll flip back and see bugs that once took you an hour and now take you ten seconds. That's what getting better looks like from the inside.

### Try this today

Ask your parent to plant two bugs in a program that already works. Find them. For each one, write the line number, the kind of error, and your fix.

### Explain it back

Tell your parent, without looking at this page, the difference between a syntax error and a logic error. Then give an example of each from your own code.

### This part is hard for everyone

Logic errors are hard. There's no message to read, only an answer that's wrong, and your brain keeps reading what you *meant* instead of what you *wrote*. That isn't a sign you're bad at this. It's why programmers use test tables, print lines, and ducks.

**The ten-minute rule.** If you've been stuck for ten minutes: stand up. Explain it to the duck. Write the Expected/Actual line. *Then* ask for help. And one more thing: deleting the file is not a strategy. The clue is in there.

### Challenge

Write a short program that works. Save a copy. Plant three bugs in the copy: one syntax, one runtime, and one logic. Give it to your parent and watch them hunt. You'll learn a surprising amount about bugs by hiding them well.

**If you're 13 or older:** make your logic bug an off-by-one, like `range(1, 5)` where you meant 1 to 5. Then write a test table that would catch it.

### What to save

- Your error decoder card.
- Your Debug Log, dated.
- Your test table with pass/fail marks.
- Each version of your program, named date first (like `2026-10-19-guess-game-v2.py`), and your changelog.

Every entry in that log is proof you solved something. Keep it in two places.

## If it isn't clicking

**They change five things at once.** Slow down to BUG-05. Sit beside them with the change log and say "one" out loud before each run. After a week it becomes habit.

**They won't read the error messages.** Many students glance at red text and look away, as if it were shouting. Go back to the error decoder card. Cover everything except the last line with an index card, and ask only, "What does this line say, in your words?"

**Frustration takes over.** The ten-minute rule exists for this. Stand up, take a short walk around the house, and come back to paper. If a lid gets slammed, stay calm and keep it about the code: "That bug is being stubborn. Let's write down what we know before we look again." End the Hour early if you need to, on a small win.

**They blame the computer.** "It's broken" usually means "I don't know where to look." Hand them a location question: "Which line is the last one that did what you expected?" Then follow the rescue order: ask → wait → hint → short demo on your own copy → hand it back.

If two weeks pass and every bug still needs you, step back to paper. Trace a five-line program by hand, writing each variable's value as it changes. Debugging on screen gets easier once tracing on paper feels natural.

## Tools, including AI

**Thonny**, a free Python editor that works offline, is the main tool here. It highlights unclosed quotes and parentheses, and its step-through debugger and Variables view (described above) show what the program is really doing.^[2](#note-2)^ Nothing else is required. A rubber duck helps.

**AI and error messages.** For the rules, see the AI box in **The Computing Hour**. Here is what's different for debugging. The student's own Expected/Actual line and best guess always come first.

**11–12:** Rarely, after your student has written Expected/Actual and a guess, you may ask a chatbot on your own account to explain an error message and read the explanation aloud. Then check it against the code together. The AI explains; the student fixes.

**13–14:** Under the service's terms, with you nearby and the screen visible, your student may ask an AI to *explain* an error message, never to fix the code. Add a line to the Debug Log: "Help I used: asked AI what `IndentationError` means after my guess."

A preview of something that comes up again in Chapter 8: for 13–14, any code an AI suggests goes through **AI-07 Explain Every Line**. The student explains every line before deciding whether to use any of it, and anything kept goes on a Whose Work card as made by a tool. Debugging is also a good honesty check. A student who can find and fix a bug in their own program wrote that program. AS checks like Debug on the Spot are always AI-free.

Services change their rules often, so check the current terms before anyone uses one.

## What "done enough" looks like

Your student has met this chapter's goals when they can:

- name the three kinds of error and give an example of each;
- read an error message from the last line, then the line number, without being reminded;
- find a planted bug while explaining aloud (AS-02 Debug on the Spot);
- show a dated Debug Log with real entries, including what they learned;
- write a test table, with edge cases, before writing a small program;
- save numbered versions with a changelog.

**Placement by skill.** A twelve-year-old who reads errors calmly and finds logic bugs is ready for the 13–14 moves. A fourteen-year-old who still deletes files when stuck needs more weeks of BUG-01 and BUG-05, and that's fine.

**The life of the habit.** The Debug Log doesn't end with this chapter. Every program from here on gets Expected/Actual lines and log entries. Once a month, read back through the log together. Your student will see bugs they once found hard and now spot in seconds. That's the best kind of progress report, and they wrote it themselves.

\newpage

# Chapter 4

## Functions, Lists, and Data: Bigger Programs and Spreadsheets That Answer Questions

![Index cards ruled into small data tables with colored cells, a bar-chart card and a printed table page — still life, no people, no logos, no readable text.](art/ch04-opener.jpg){width=100%}

## Why this matters

By now your student can write a program of ten or twenty lines. Somewhere around thirty lines, something changes. The same five lines show up three times. A program needs to remember twelve quiz questions, not one. A question comes up that a single number can't answer. This chapter gives your student the three tools that carry programs past that point: functions, lists, and data.

A **function** is a named chunk of code you can use again. A **list** is items in order, numbered from 0. **Data** is information collected to answer a question. Together they let a student build a quiz game the family will actually play, keep a packing list that updates itself, and turn two weeks of bird-feeder tallies into a chart that tells the truth.

The national computer science standards for ages 11–14 name these skills directly: use a procedure (a function) to organize code for clarity and reuse; use variables to store and change data; and use a computing tool to sort, filter, group, and summarize data. The same standards ask students to see how design choices in a chart change what people conclude, and how decisions about collecting data can lead to misleading results.^[17](#note-17)^ That last part matters as much as the code. A student who can ask "Who's missing from this data?" is learning something many adults never do.

There's a satisfying idea underneath all of this. A spreadsheet formula like `SUM` is a function, with inputs and an output, just like the ones your student will write in Python. Once they see that, a spreadsheet stops being a mysterious office program and becomes another place to compute.

The focus line still holds: predict before you run; debug before you rewrite; explain it before you call it yours. Functions add one question to the Predict step: what goes in, and what comes out?

## For the parent: understand it yourself

### Functions

**The everyday picture.** A recipe card that says "Make the pancake batter" is a function. You write the steps once, give the card a name, and from then on you just say "make the batter." If the card says "for ___ people," that blank is an input.

**The precise picture.** In Python, `def` defines a function. The name in the parentheses is a **parameter**, an input the function needs:

```python
def draw_box(size):
    for row in range(size):
        print("#" * size)

draw_box(3)
draw_box(5)
```

This prints a 3-by-3 square of `#` marks, then a 5-by-5 square. The four indented lines run only when the function is called. Recall from earlier chapters: a variable is a labeled box whose contents can change, and a loop means do it again. Here `size` is a variable that gets a new value each time the function is called.

**Return versus print.** This is the idea that trips up nearly everyone. `print` shows a value on the screen and then forgets it. `return` hands the value back so the program can keep using it.

```python
def c_to_f_print(c):
    print(c * 9 / 5 + 32)

def c_to_f(c):
    return c * 9 / 5 + 32

answer = c_to_f_print(100)
print("Saved:", answer)
answer = c_to_f(100)
print("Saved:", answer)
```

The output is `212.0`, then `Saved: None`, then `Saved: 212.0`. The first function showed the answer and kept nothing; Python's word for "nothing" is `None`. The second handed the answer back. A function that returns can be tested: `c_to_f(0)` gives `32.0`, and `c_to_f(-40)` gives `-40.0`, which is a pleasing edge case.

### Lists

**The everyday picture.** A row of numbered cubbies, where the first cubby is number 0.

**The precise picture.** A list holds items in order. The Python documentation describes positions as "zero-based": the first item is at position 0.^[43](#note-43),[44](#note-44)^ You can add to the end with `append`, take out an item with `remove`, count items with `len`, and walk through every item with a `for` loop:

```python
packing_list = ["socks", "toothbrush", "book"]
packing_list.append("flashlight")
packing_list.remove("book")
print(len(packing_list))
for item in packing_list:
    print("Pack the", item)
print(packing_list[0])
```

This prints `3`, then three "Pack the…" lines (socks, toothbrush, flashlight), then `socks`. If the student types `packing_list[3]`, Python stops with `IndexError: list index out of range`. Three items live at positions 0, 1, and 2. That off-by-one surprise is worth meeting on purpose.

### Data and spreadsheets

**Question first.** Good data work starts with a question, not a pile of numbers: "Which bird visits our feeder most in the morning?" The question decides what to count, when, and for how long.

**Spreadsheets.** A spreadsheet is a grid. Each row is one observation (one morning, one bird). Each column is one kind of information (date, time, bird, how many). Each box is a cell, named by its column letter and row number, like `B4`. Clean data means one kind of thing per column and the same spelling every time: "sparrow," not "Sparrow" in one row and "sparow" in the next.

**Formulas are functions.** `=SUM(D2:D31)` takes a range of cells as input and returns their total. `AVERAGE`, `MAX`, and `COUNTIF` work the same way. `=COUNTIF(C2:C31, "sparrow")` counts how many cells in column C say "sparrow." Spreadsheet programs differ in small ways (some want a semicolon between the parts instead of a comma), so if a formula won't take, check the program's own help page for its exact form. Any spreadsheet program your family already has will do.

**Sort, filter, group.** Sorting puts rows in order. Filtering hides the rows you don't need right now. Grouping gathers rows that share something (all the cardinals) so you can total each group.

**Charts that tell the truth.** The same numbers can tell different stories. If 41 sparrows and 37 cardinals are drawn as bars on an axis that starts at 35, the sparrow bar is three times as tall as the cardinal bar (6 units against 2). Start the axis at zero and the bars look nearly equal, which is the honest picture. The standards ask students to see exactly this: design choices change how people read the same data.^[17](#note-17)^

**Who's missing?** If you only count birds at 8 a.m., you'll miss the ones that come at dusk. If you only ask your friends, you'll miss everyone who isn't your friend. Every dataset leaves someone or something out. Saying so is part of an honest answer. Chapter 8 returns to this idea when it explains how AI learns from data.

**Data about data.** A phone photo carries more than the picture. Phones and cameras automatically record details about when, where, and how a photo was taken, and that hidden information can travel with the photo when it's shared. Phones let you turn off the camera's access to location.^[45](#note-45)^ This hidden information is called metadata. It makes a good data lesson here, and Chapter 7 covers what it means for privacy. Before anyone shares a photo, see Family safety basics in **The Computing Hour**.

### Wrong answers you should be able to hear

- **"The list starts at 1."** Natural, and wrong in Python. Reply: "Let's test it. Print `packing_list[0]` and see what comes out."
- **"Print and return are the same thing."** They look the same on screen. Reply: "Store the answer in a variable and print the variable. What do you get?"
- **"My chart proves it."** A chart shows; it rarely proves. Reply: "What does the axis start at? Who's missing from the data?"
- **"The average tells you everything."** Reply: "What's the biggest number? The smallest? Would one strange day change the average a lot?"
- **"It works, so I'm done."** Reply: "What does your test table say about zero, a negative number, and an empty list?"

### Your five-minute warm-up

Type the `c_to_f` example into Thonny and run it. Say out loud why the first "Saved" line shows `None`. Then open any spreadsheet, type five numbers in a column, and try `=SUM` and `=AVERAGE` on them. Practice this chapter's opening question once, and then count to five in your head.

## How to teach it this week

This chapter is two or three weeks of work, not one. A good order: functions and lists first, then data, then a small project that uses all three.

Use the Hour's steps as usual:

- **Unplug and Plan.** Function machines on paper; index cards for lists; a question card for data.
- **Predict.** For every function call, the student says what goes in and what will come out. For every list line, which position and which item.
- **Build and Test.** Write the function or list program, run the test table.
- **Debug and Log.** Expected/Actual for every surprise; one Debug Log line.
- **Save and Explain.** "I predicted… It did… Next I'd change…" and "Help I used: …"

### In-home — FN-01 Function Machine

- **Time:** 10 minutes.
- **Materials:** a cardboard box or a drawn box, small slips of paper, rule cards.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** guessing the secret rule.
- **The skill:** input, rule, output.
- **The crumb:** rule cards.

You hold a secret rule card ("double it and add one"). The student feeds in a number on a slip; you pass back the output. After three or four tries, they guess the rule and test it on a number they haven't tried. Then swap roles. Finish by writing the rule as Python: `def machine(n): return n * 2 + 1`.

### In-home — LIST-01 Index Cards in a Row

- **Time:** 10 minutes.
- **Materials:** index cards, a marker, a table.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** acting out a program with your hands.
- **The skill:** positions starting at 0; `append` and `remove`.
- **The crumb:** a photo of the row (no faces).

Write a small number above each card position, starting at 0. Lay out three items. Call out commands: "append flashlight" (add to the end), "remove book" (slide the rest left), "what's at position 2?" The student moves cards and answers. The slide-left after a removal is the moment the numbering clicks.

### How to fade your help

Follow the rescue order: **ask → wait → hint → short demo on your own copy → hand it back.** For functions, the best hint is a question about inputs: "What does this function need to know to do its job?"

**11–12:** Start with functions that print and take one parameter. Use one list at a time. In spreadsheets, stick to `SUM` and `AVERAGE` at first.

**13–14:** Add `return` and test tables for every function. Use two lists side by side, or pairs. Add `COUNTIF` and grouping. Write a short investigation that names its limits. The student leads; you're **first user**, trying each program like a stranger and reporting without fixing.

**Ready 12:** A twelve-year-old who explains why the first "Saved" line shows `None` is ready for FN-03.

### A worked Hour (illustration)

Here is an illustration, not a real family. Picture a twelve-year-old and her mother on a Tuesday. In Unplug and Plan, the girl lays out four index cards and numbers them 0 to 3. Her mother asks, "What goes in, what comes out — and how would you test it?" about a list program the girl has sketched. In Predict, the girl says `print(packing_list[3])` will show "flashlight." She runs it: `IndexError`. Her mother waits. The girl looks at her cards. "Oh. I removed the book. There are only three now. The last one's at 2." She changes one thing, runs it, and writes the log line. "I predicted flashlight. It crashed. Next I'd print the length first. Help I used: my cards."

## Practice that actually builds learning

### In-home — FN-02 Define Your Own

- **Time:** 25 minutes.
- **Materials:** Thonny.
- **Safety and privacy:** offline.
- **The fun:** inventing a command Python didn't have.
- **The skill:** `def` with parameters.
- **The crumb:** the program file.

Start with `draw_box(size)` from the parent section, then let the student design their own: `draw_triangle(height)`, or `greet(name, times)`. Students who did turtle drawings in Chapter 2 (PY-07 Turtle Patterns) can write `draw_square(size)` with the turtle and call it at four sizes to make a pattern. Before writing any function, they write the paper version first: name, inputs, what it does.

### In-home — FN-03 Return to Sender

*For 13–14; ready 12.*

- **Time:** 25 minutes.
- **Materials:** Thonny, paper.
- **Safety and privacy:** offline.
- **The fun:** a converter you can trust.
- **The skill:** `return` versus `print`; a unit converter with a test table.
- **The crumb:** the program file plus the test table.

The student writes `c_to_f(c)` and fills in a test table before running it (this is BUG-06 Test Table First, back from Chapter 3): 0 → 32, 100 → 212, -40 → -40, 37 → 98.6. Then they write the opposite converter and check that converting there and back gives the starting number.

### In-home — FN-04 Refactor the Repeats

*For 13–14.*

- **Time:** 25 minutes.
- **Materials:** a program the student wrote earlier with repeated code.
- **Safety and privacy:** offline.
- **The fun:** making a program shorter and better at the same time.
- **The skill:** finding repeated code and replacing it with a function.
- **The crumb:** before and after files.

Look through an old program for lines that appear twice or more with only a small difference. That difference becomes the parameter. The program must give exactly the same output before and after, which is a test in itself. Save both versions.

### In-home — LIST-02 Packing List Program

- **Time:** 25 minutes.
- **Materials:** Thonny.
- **Safety and privacy:** offline.
- **The fun:** a tool for the next family trip.
- **The skill:** `append`, `remove`, `len`, and a loop over a list.
- **The crumb:** the program file.

Start from the packing list example. Add an `input()` loop so the user can type items to add, and "done" to stop. Print the final list with numbers. A good test: what happens if you `remove` something that isn't in the list? (Python raises a `ValueError`.^[43](#note-43),[44](#note-44)^) Can the student make the program check first with `if item in packing_list:`?

### In-home — LIST-03 Quiz Machine

- **Time:** 40 minutes over two Hours.
- **Materials:** Thonny, a friend or family member to take the quiz.
- **Safety and privacy:** offline; quiz-takers' first names only.
- **The fun:** a game other people play.
- **The skill:** two lists side by side, a score variable, and feedback.
- **The crumb:** the program file plus a friend's score.

```python
questions = ["7 x 8?", "Capital of France?", "Bits in a byte?"]
answers = ["56", "paris", "8"]
score = 0
for i in range(len(questions)):
    reply = input(questions[i] + " ")
    if reply.strip().lower() == answers[i]:
        score = score + 1
        print("Right!")
    else:
        print("The answer was", answers[i])
print("Score:", score, "out of", len(questions))
```

If someone answers 56, Paris, and 7, it prints "Right!" twice, then "The answer was 8," then `Score: 2 out of 3`. Ask your student to explain `.strip().lower()`: it tidies spaces and capitals so " Paris" still counts. **11–12:** Five questions on a topic they love. **13–14:** Ten questions, and a message that changes with the score.

### In-home — LIST-04 Find the Biggest

- **Time:** 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** a deck of cards, then Thonny.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** beating a famous algorithm (exact steps to reach a goal) at its own game.
- **The skill:** the maximum algorithm by hand, then in code.
- **The crumb:** a trace table and the file.

Deal cards face down in a row. The student turns them over one at a time, keeping only the biggest so far in hand. Then the same in code:

```python
temps = [14, 19, 11, 23, 17]
biggest = temps[0]
for t in temps:
    if t > biggest:
        biggest = t
print(biggest)
```

It prints `23`. Then compare with Python's built-in `max(temps)`, which gives the same answer. This is also a good place to revisit ALG-04 Battleships Search from Chapter 1: finding the biggest in an unsorted list means checking every item, just as a search through unsorted numbers does.^[46](#note-46)^

### In-home — DATA-01 Question First

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** an index card.
- **Safety and privacy:** pick questions that don't need other people's private information.
- **The fun:** choosing a question you actually care about.
- **The skill:** writing a question and deciding what data would answer it.
- **The crumb:** the question card.

On the card: the question, what to count, where, when, for how long, and one thing that might make the answer wrong. "Which bird visits our feeder most? Count each visit, 8:00–8:15 a.m., for 14 days. Might be wrong because: we're only watching mornings."

### In-home — DATA-02 Spreadsheet Basics

- **Time:** 25 minutes.
- **Materials:** any spreadsheet program the family already has.
- **Safety and privacy:** keep the file on the family drive.
- **The fun:** seeing a messy notebook become a tidy table.
- **The skill:** rows, columns, cells; entering clean data.
- **The crumb:** the sheet file.

Headings in row 1: Date, Time, Bird, Count. One observation per row. Agree on spellings before typing. Then the student finds three "dirty" entries you slipped in (a typo, a capital, a number typed as words) and cleans them.

### In-home — DATA-03 Formulas Are Functions

- **Time:** 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** the sheet.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** making the spreadsheet do the arithmetic.
- **The skill:** `SUM`, `AVERAGE`, `MAX`, and `COUNTIF` as functions with inputs and outputs.
- **The crumb:** the sheet with formulas shown.

For each formula the student says, before pressing Enter, what goes in (the range) and what will come out. **11–12:** `SUM` and `AVERAGE`. **13–14:** add `MAX` and `COUNTIF`. Most spreadsheets have a setting to show formulas instead of results; turn it on for the saved copy.

### In-home — DATA-04 Sort, Filter, Group

- **Time:** 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** the sheet.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** asking the data new questions.
- **The skill:** sorting, filtering, grouping, and summarizing.
- **The crumb:** a summary table.

Sort by bird, then by count. Filter to show only one bird. Then build a small summary table: each bird and its total, using `COUNTIF` or the spreadsheet's grouping tool. **13–14:** Compare two groups (weekdays and weekends) and write one sentence about the difference and one about why it might not mean much.

### In-home — DATA-05 A Chart That Tells the Truth

- **Time:** 25 minutes.
- **Materials:** the summary table.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** making a chart lie, then making it honest.
- **The skill:** choosing a chart; spotting a misleading axis.
- **The crumb:** the chart plus a one-line caption.

Make a bar chart of bird totals. Then change the axis so it starts just below the smallest bar and watch the differences balloon. Change it back to zero. The student writes one honest caption: "Sparrows came most often (41 visits), but cardinals were close (37)."

### In-home — DATA-06 Who Is Missing?

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** the question card and the data.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** catching your own blind spot.
- **The skill:** spotting a biased sample: what or who the data leaves out.
- **The crumb:** a two-sentence note.

Ask: "What didn't we count? When weren't we watching? What would change the answer?" The note: "We only watched mornings, so birds that come in the evening are missing. Our answer is about morning visitors, not all visitors."

### Out-of-home — OUT-03 Data Walk

- **Time:** 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** a clipboard, a tally sheet, a pencil.
- **Safety and privacy:** road rules; stay together; count things, not people's faces; no photos of strangers.
- **The fun:** fieldwork.
- **The skill:** counting something outdoors and bringing the data home.
- **The crumb:** the tally sheet.

Pick something countable from a safe spot: cars by color at a corner, dogs on a park loop, kinds of trees on one block. Agree on categories before you start, including "other." At home, the tally goes into the spreadsheet for DATA-02 through DATA-06. The student who owns a micro:bit can log light or temperature instead; see the optional PHYS-02 Sensor Logger in Chapter 6.

### In-home — PROJ-01 Project One-Pager

- **Time:** 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** one sheet of paper.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** planning something real.
- **The skill:** problem, user, features, test plan, and schedule on one page.
- **The crumb:** the one-pager.

Before a bigger project (the quiz machine with ten questions, or a bird report with a chart), the student writes one page: what problem it solves, who will use it, three features, how it will be tested, and what gets done in which Hour. PLAN-07 Paper Parsons, from Chapter 1, makes a good warm-up: cut a finished function into strips, mix them up, and put them back in order.

### Talk box (this chapter)

**Opening:** "What goes in, what comes out — and how would you test it?"

**Follow-ups:**
- Prediction: "If you call it with 0, what will it give back?"
- Evidence: "What in the output makes you say it's working?"
- Location: "Which position in the list is the last one that's right?"
- Change: "What's the one thing you'd change to make it work for an empty list?"
- Revision: "Want to change your prediction? Run it again."

**How to wait.** Count to five in your head. When the question is about code on the screen, let them read as long as they need. Look at the code or the spreadsheet, not at the student.^[5](#note-5)^

**What a stuck silence usually means.** The question may be too big; shrink it to one input. They may not know what "returns" means yet; go back to the function machine. They may be afraid the answer is obvious and they're missing it. There may be no test table to compare against. Or the spreadsheet is full of colors and charts, and the question got lost; hide the chart for a minute.

**Don't say / say instead.** Don't say "No, lists start at zero." Say "Let's ask Python. Print position 0 and see."

### Blocked, then mixed

Spend a few days on functions alone, then lists alone, then spreadsheets alone. Then mix: one Hour might ask for a function that takes a list and returns its biggest number. That mixed task is where students discover the ideas belong together.

### One incorrect example to diagnose

Here is an illustration, not a real family. Show your student this function, from an imaginary student who says it finds the biggest temperature:

```python
temps = [-5, -2, -9]
biggest = 0
for t in temps:
    if t > biggest:
        biggest = t
print(biggest)
```

It prints `0`. Zero isn't even in the list. Ask: "What did this student assume?" (That temperatures are never below zero.) "What test would have caught it?" (A list of all negative numbers.) "What's the fix?" (Start with `biggest = temps[0]`.) A test table with edge cases would have found it in a minute.

### A week without a screen

Everything in this chapter has a paper version, which is handy for travel weeks or a broken laptop. Function machines need only slips of paper. Index cards act out every list command. A tally sheet and a pencil can do the work of `COUNTIF`: count the marks, group them by bird, add the groups. Charts can be drawn on graph paper, which is the best way to feel how much an axis choice matters, because you have to decide where zero goes with your own pencil. A student can even "run" a function by hand, writing a trace table with one column for each variable and one row for each step. When the laptop comes back, the code feels familiar instead of new.

### Encouragement that means something

Praise the move, not the student. Try these:

- "You wrote the test table before the code. That's what careful programmers do."
- "You noticed the axis didn't start at zero. Plenty of adults miss that."
- "You turned three copies of the same lines into one function. The program's easier to read now."
- "You wrote down who was missing from your data. That makes your answer more honest, not less."

Specific praise tells your student exactly what to do again. "Great job" tells them only that you're pleased.

### Siblings and co-ops

The quiz machine is made for groups: each student writes five questions on a shared topic, then everyone takes everyone's quiz. Data walks work well with two counters on two different corners, then a comparison at home. Charts make a good co-op show-and-tell, especially the "lying" version beside the honest one.

## For the student

If you've copied and pasted the same five lines three times, you've found a function.

A **function** is a named chunk of code you can use again. You write the steps once, give them a name, and call the name whenever you need them. Inputs go in the parentheses after the name. Those inputs are called parameters.

```python
def draw_box(size):
    for row in range(size):
        print("#" * size)

draw_box(3)
```

This prints three rows of `###`. Call `draw_box(5)` and you get a bigger square, with no new code.

**Print or return?** `print` shows something on the screen. `return` hands an answer back so your program can keep using it. If you write `answer = some_function(100)` and then `print(answer)` shows `None`, your function printed instead of returning. It showed you the answer and then threw it away.

A **list** is items in order, numbered from 0. That "from 0" part catches everyone at first:

```python
pets = ["cat", "dog", "fish"]
print(pets[0])
print(len(pets))
```

This prints `cat`, then `3`. Three items, at positions 0, 1, and 2. Ask for `pets[3]` and Python says `IndexError: list index out of range`. Now you'll know why. You can add to the end with `pets.append("hamster")`, take out an item with `pets.remove("dog")`, and visit every item with `for pet in pets:`.

**Data** is information you collect to answer a question. Always start with the question. "Which bird comes to our feeder most?" tells you what to count, when, and how. A spreadsheet holds the data in rows and columns. And here's the neat part: spreadsheet formulas are functions. `=SUM(D2:D31)` takes a range of cells in and gives a total out. Same idea as Python.

### A worked example

Say your question is "Which bird visits most in the morning?" You count for two weeks and type it into a sheet. `=COUNTIF(C2:C40, "sparrow")` tells you how many sparrow visits there were. You do the same for each bird and make a bar chart. Sparrows: 41. Cardinals: 37. Your caption says: "Sparrows came most often, but cardinals were close.
