We only watched in the mornings, so evening birds are missing."

That last sentence is what makes it honest.

### Functions and lists together

The real power shows up when you combine them. A function can take a whole list as its input:

```python
def total(numbers):
    result = 0
    for n in numbers:
        result = result + n
    return result

print(total([3, 2, 1]))
```

This prints `6`. Now you can total any list you like with one short call. That's how bigger programs stay readable: small functions with clear names, each doing one job, each tested on its own.

A tip for when things go wrong: when a list program gives a strange answer, add `print(my_list)` and `print(len(my_list))` just before the line that misbehaves. Seeing what's really in the list solves most list bugs in under a minute. Then take those lines out again.

### Try this today

Write a function called `double(n)` that returns `n * 2`. Before you run anything, fill in a test table: what should `double(0)`, `double(-3)`, and `double(1000)` give? Then run it and check.

### Explain it back

Tell your parent the difference between `print` and `return`, using a function you wrote. Show them what happens when you store the result in a variable.

### This part is hard for everyone

Return versus print confuses almost every new programmer, because both put something on the screen when you test them. The difference only shows up when you try to *use* the answer. If it feels slippery, that's normal. Store the answer in a variable and print the variable. That test always tells you which one you wrote.

Lists starting at 0 also feel wrong for a while. Keep index cards with the numbers written above them nearby until it doesn't.

### Challenge

Build a quiz machine with two lists: one for questions, one for answers. Keep score. Give it to two people, and write down their scores (first names only). Then improve one thing based on what confused them.

**If you're 13 or older:** write a function `biggest(numbers)` that returns the largest number in a list without using `max()`. Test it with an empty list. What happens? Decide what it *should* do, and make it do that.

### What to save

- Your function files, with a test table for each.
- Your quiz machine and a friend's score.
- Your spreadsheet, with formulas shown.
- Your chart, with a one-line honest caption.
- Your question card and your "who's missing?" note.
- Your Debug Log entries.

Save everything in two places.

## If it isn't clicking

**Functions feel pointless.** That usually means the programs are still small enough not to need them. Go back to FN-01 Function Machine, then find a real repeat in an old program. The point of a function shows up the moment you'd otherwise copy and paste.

**Return makes no sense.** Step back to paper. The function machine passes a slip back through the slot; that's `return`. A machine that shouts the answer and keeps the slip is `print`. Then run the "Saved:" example again.

**Off-by-one errors keep happening.** Bring back the index cards with positions written above them. Before each list line, the student points to the card. Add `print(len(my_list))` lines as print detectives.

**The spreadsheet feels like busywork.** The question isn't theirs. Let them pick one they care about, even if it's "Which of my games do I actually play most?" (Track it on paper for a week; no app needed.)

If none of this works, slow down. Use the rescue order: ask → wait → hint → short demo on your own copy → hand it back. It's fine to spend an extra week on lists.

## Tools, including AI

**Thonny**, the free offline Python editor from earlier chapters, is all the programming needs.^[2](#note-2)^ Its Variables view (View, then Variables) is especially good for lists, because you can watch items appear and disappear.

**Spreadsheets.** Use whatever spreadsheet program the family already has, on a computer in a shared room. Keep files on the family drive.

**Optional data source.** A student with a micro:bit can collect light or temperature readings and bring them into a spreadsheet; see the optional strand in Chapter 6.

**AI.** For the rules, see the AI box in **The Computing Hour**. What's different here:

**11–12:** AI is parent-operated only. If you use it at all, it might suggest practice problems (answers hidden until your student tries).

**13–14:** After your student has chosen a chart type and said why, they may ask an AI, with you present, what kind of chart it would suggest and compare the reasons. If an AI suggests a formula or a function, it goes through AI-07 Explain Every Line before anything is used, with a "Help I used" line and a Whose Work card marking it as made by a tool. Check the current terms of any service first.

## What "done enough" looks like

Your student is ready to move on when they can:

- write a function with a parameter and explain what goes in and what comes out;
- at 13–14, explain `return` versus `print` and test a function with a table that includes edge cases;
- use a list with `append`, `remove`, `len`, and a loop, and explain why the first item is at 0;
- start a data project with a written question;
- use `SUM` and `AVERAGE` (and at 13–14, `COUNTIF` and grouping) and say what each formula takes in and gives back;
- make an honest chart and name who or what is missing from the data.

**AS checks.** Once this month, run AS-01 Cold Trace on a short list program (predict the output without running it), AS-02 Debug on the Spot with a planted off-by-one, and AS-03 Explain Your Code on the quiz machine. All three are AI-free.

**Placement by skill.** A twelve-year-old who handles `return` comfortably can take on FN-03. A fourteen-year-old still unsure about positions needs more time with index cards, and that's a fine use of a week.

**The life of the habit.** From now on, every program bigger than twenty lines should have at least one function, and every question about the world should start with a question card. Keep the bird sheet. It makes a good portfolio piece next to the chart that tells the truth.

\newpage

# Chapter 5

## Building for the Web: HTML, CSS, and a First Taste of JavaScript

![Two hand-drawn web page wireframes with a header bar, an image box, lines for text and a button, beside three color swatches — still life, no people, no logos, no readable text.](art/ch05-opener.jpg){width=100%}

## Why this matters

Every site your student has ever visited started as a blank file. Somebody typed a few lines of text, saved it, and opened it in a browser. That is still how it works, and your student can do it this week with tools your computer already has.

Building a web page teaches something Python alone doesn't: that a program has an audience. A page is made to be read by someone else, on someone else's screen, possibly with their eyes or possibly with a screen reader that speaks the page aloud. That turns every choice (what goes first, what a link says, what a picture's description says) into a question about another person. It's a gentle way to teach that good code is considerate code.

A web page has three layers, and this chapter takes them in order:

- **HTML** gives the page its structure: headings, paragraphs, lists, links, pictures.
- **CSS** gives it style: colors, fonts, spacing.
- **JavaScript** gives it behavior: a button that does something when clicked.

The national computer science standards for ages 11–14 ask students to use reference documentation while they program and to improve their work based on user feedback, making it easier to use and more accessible.^[17](#note-17)^ Web pages are a natural place for both. Your student will look things up in MDN Web Docs, a free reference and course for web developers run by Mozilla, and test pages on real people at a small family "demo day."^[47](#note-47)^

One more thing makes the web a good fit at this age: no account is needed. A page saved on your own computer opens in your own browser, and nobody else sees it. Publishing a page online is a separate decision, made by you, later or never. This chapter keeps the work local.

## For the parent: understand it yourself

**The everyday picture.** Think of a house. HTML is the frame and the rooms: this is the kitchen, this is a hallway. CSS is the paint, the furniture, and how far apart things sit. JavaScript is the light switches: press one and something happens.

**The precise picture.** An HTML file is plain text with **tags** in angle brackets. Most tags come in pairs: `<h1>` opens a top-level heading and `</h1>` closes it. Here is a small but complete page, saved as `index.html`:

```html
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
<head>
  <meta charset="utf-8">
  <title>Birds at Our Feeder</title>
  <link rel="stylesheet" href="style.css">
</head>
<body>
  <h1>Birds at Our Feeder</h1>
  <p>I counted birds every morning for two weeks.</p>
  <h2>What I saw most</h2>
  <ul>
    <li>Sparrows</li>
    <li>Cardinals</li>
  </ul>
</body>
</html>
```

Double-click the file and it opens in the browser: a big heading, a sentence, a smaller heading, and a bulleted list. The `<head>` holds information about the page (its title on the browser tab, and a link to the style file). The `<body>` holds what you see.

**CSS** lives in a second file, `style.css`, and says how things should look:

```css
body {
  font-family: sans-serif;
  background-color: rgb(250 248 240);
  margin: 2em;
}
h1 {
  color: rgb(20 80 140);
}
```

Each block starts with a **selector** (which things to style: `body`, `h1`) and lists **properties** and values inside curly braces. A color written as `rgb(20 80 140)` gives amounts of red, green, and blue, each from 0 to 255.^[48](#note-48)^ Chapter 6 explains why it's those three numbers, and why 255.

**The box model.** Every element on a page is a box. From the inside out: the **content**, then **padding** (space inside the edge), then a **border**, then a **margin** (space outside, pushing other boxes away). Most layout puzzles in CSS are box puzzles.

**JavaScript** is a programming language that runs in the browser. Students who know Python will recognize variables, `if`, and functions with different punctuation. This chapter uses it for one small job: when a button is clicked, change some text.

**Accessibility as good manners.** Some people use a screen reader, which reads a page aloud. Many screen readers let a person jump from heading to heading, so headings work like a table of contents.^[49](#note-49)^ That's why a page should use real headings in order (`h1`, then `h2`) instead of just making text big. MDN's accessibility guide adds three habits a student can learn this week:^[49](#note-49)^

- Every picture gets **alt text**, a short description in the `alt` attribute that gives a direct representation of what the picture shows. A purely decorative picture gets an empty one: `alt=""`.
- **Link text makes sense on its own.** "Read about how web pages work" is good; "click here" tells a screen-reader user nothing.
- **Text stands out from its background.** The guide asks for a contrast of at least 4.5 to 1 between text and background color. Pale gray on white usually fails.

**Reading the documentation.** Programmers look things up all day; it's part of the job, not a sign of weakness. MDN is the reference this book uses for HTML, CSS, and JavaScript.^[47](#note-47)^ Teach your student to look up a tag on MDN before asking anyone else.

**Developer tools.** Every major browser has built-in tools for looking at a page's code. You can usually open them by right-clicking part of a page and choosing "Inspect," or with Ctrl+Shift+I (or F12) on Windows and Cmd+Option+I on a Mac. Safari keeps them turned off until you enable them in its settings. The **Console** tab lists errors the browser meets while running JavaScript, which is where a silent button usually explains itself.^[50](#note-50)^

**Your computer or the web?** When your student double-clicks `index.html`, the browser reads a file from your own drive. Nothing travels anywhere. When you visit a site online, your browser asks a faraway computer, called a server, for files much like these, and they arrive in small pieces over the internet. MDN draws a useful line here: the internet is the network that connects computers, and the web is one service that runs on top of it.^[51](#note-51),[52](#note-52)^ Chapter 6 follows those pieces on their journey. For now, it's enough to know that the page your student builds is made of exactly the same stuff as the pages they visit.

**Publishing and privacy.** A page on your own computer is private. If your family ever decides to put a page online, that's your call, and it comes with the usual rules: first names only; no faces, school, or town; no photos of other people. Pictures on the page should be made by your student, or credited and used with permission. See Family safety basics in **The Computing Hour**.

### Wrong answers you should be able to hear

- **"I changed it, but nothing happened."** Usually the file wasn't saved, or the browser wasn't refreshed, or the wrong file is open. Reply: "Save, then refresh. Which file is the browser showing?"
- **"The page is broken."** Browsers try hard to show *something*, so a missing closing tag can make half the page bold instead of showing an error. Reply: "Which part looks wrong? Let's find the tag just before it."
- **"Alt text is optional."** Reply: "Close your eyes. I'll read you the page. What do you want me to say when we get to the picture?"
- **"It was online, so I can use it."** Reply: "Who made it? Did they say we could? Let's draw our own or credit it."
- **"The AI made the page, but I get it."** A generated page is made by a tool, not your student's work. Reply: "Great, then build one like it from a blank file and explain every line."

### Your five-minute warm-up

Type the small HTML page above into a plain text editor, save it as `index.html`, and open it in your browser. Change one word, save, refresh, and watch it change. Then practice this chapter's opening question once, aloud, and count to five in your head.

## How to teach it this week

Plan on two to three weeks: structure first, then style, then (for students who are ready) one button. Each Hour follows the usual steps:

- **Unplug and Plan.** A paper wireframe, labeled with tag names.
- **Predict.** "When you refresh, what will change, and where?"
- **Build and Test.** Type, save, refresh, look. Then test on a **first user**.
- **Debug and Log.** Expected/Actual for every surprise, written in the Debug Log.
- **Save and Explain.** "I predicted… It did… Next I'd change…" and "Help I used: …"

The tools: a plain text editor (the one that came with the computer is fine, but not a word processor, which adds hidden formatting) and a web browser. Make one folder for the project. Keep `index.html`, `style.css`, and any pictures together in it.

### In-home — WEB-01 Paper Wireframe

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** paper, pencil, a ruler if you like.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** designing before building, like an architect.
- **The skill:** sketching a page layout and labeling the parts as tags.
- **The crumb:** the sketch.

The student draws boxes for each part of the page (title, introduction, list, picture, link) and labels each with its tag: `h1`, `p`, `ul`, `img`, `a`. Then you ask the opening question: "Who is this page for — and what will they see first?" The answer often reorders the sketch.

### In-home — WEB-02 My First Page

- **Time:** 30 minutes.
- **Materials:** a plain text editor, a browser, the wireframe.
- **Safety and privacy:** saved locally; no account; first names only on the page.
- **The fun:** your words, in a real browser.
- **The skill:** HTML headings, paragraphs, lists, and links.
- **The crumb:** `index.html`.

The student types the page from the wireframe, saving and refreshing after every few lines. That rhythm (small change, save, refresh, look) is the web version of Predict and Build and Test. Add one link: `<a href="https://developer.mozilla.org/">Read how web pages are built on MDN</a>`. Notice the link text says where it goes.

### How to fade your help

Use the rescue order: **ask → wait → hint → short demo on your own copy → hand it back.** For HTML, the best hints point to a pair: "Every opening tag in this part, can you find its closing tag?"

**11–12:** One page plus a CSS file. You might type the first `<!DOCTYPE html>` skeleton together, then hand over the keyboard for everything inside `<body>`.

**13–14:** Three pages linked to each other, sharing one stylesheet, plus a JavaScript button and a round of feedback from first users. The student leads; you're the **first user**, trying the page like a stranger and reporting what you see without fixing anything.

**Ready 12:** A twelve-year-old who finishes a styled page and asks "Can I make it *do* something?" is ready for WEB-06.

### A worked Hour (illustration)

Here is an illustration, not a real family. Picture a fourteen-year-old and her grandmother on a Thursday afternoon. The girl has three pages about the birds at the feeder. In Predict, she says the heading will turn dark blue when she adds an `h1` rule to `style.css`. She saves, refreshes. Nothing changes. Her grandmother asks the opening question, then, after a pause, "What did you expect, and what happened?" The girl writes Expected/Actual. She opens the HTML and reads the `<link>` line out loud: `href="styles.css"`. She looks at the folder. The file is called `style.css`. One letter. She fixes it, refreshes, and the heading turns blue. Her log entry: "Bug: CSS didn't load. Clue: no styles at all, not just one. Fix: file name in link line. Learned: if *nothing* is styled, check the link line first." That last sentence is the kind of thing that turns into skill.

## Practice that actually builds learning

### In-home — WEB-03 Style It

- **Time:** 30 minutes.
- **Materials:** the page from WEB-02, a new file called `style.css`.
- **Safety and privacy:** local only.
- **The fun:** making it look like yours.
- **The skill:** CSS selectors, color, fonts, and spacing.
- **The crumb:** `style.css`.

Add the `<link rel="stylesheet" href="style.css">` line in the `<head>`. Write one rule at a time and predict each change before refreshing. The student should look up at least one property on MDN by themselves (`font-family`, `text-align`, `border`). **11–12:** Three rules. **13–14:** A shared stylesheet used by all three pages, so one change updates the whole site.

### In-home — WEB-04 Paper Box Model

- **Time:** 10 minutes.
- **Materials:** four sheets of colored paper, scissors.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** nesting rectangles like a picture frame.
- **The skill:** margin, border, padding, and content.
- **The crumb:** a labeled paper model.

Cut a small rectangle (content: write a word on it). Glue it on a slightly bigger one (padding). Then a thin frame (border). Then the biggest sheet (margin). Label each layer. Now write the CSS: `p { padding: 1em; border: 2px solid gray; margin: 1em; }`, refresh, and match the screen to the paper.

### In-home — WEB-05 Alt Text and Access Check

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** the student's page and a printed checklist.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** seeing your page the way someone else does.
- **The skill:** alt text, heading order, contrast, and link text.
- **The crumb:** the checklist.

The checklist has four lines: every picture has alt text that says what it shows (or `alt=""` if it's decoration); headings go in order with no skipped levels; text is easy to read against its background; every link says where it goes. Then you read the page aloud with your eyes closed, the way a screen reader might, reading alt text when you reach a picture. Students hear the difference immediately. A picture might get: `<img src="feeder-drawing.png" alt="Pencil drawing of a wooden bird feeder with two sparrows on the perch">`.

### In-home — WEB-06 A Button That Answers

*For 13–14; ready 12.*

- **Time:** 30 minutes.
- **Materials:** the page, a new file called `script.js`.
- **Safety and privacy:** local only.
- **The fun:** a page that responds.
- **The skill:** a JavaScript click event that changes text on the page.
- **The crumb:** `script.js`.

Add to the HTML, just before `</body>`:

```html
<button id="fact-button">Which bird came most?</button>
<p id="fact"></p>
<script src="script.js"></script>
```

Then, in `script.js`:

```javascript
const button = document.querySelector("#fact-button");
const answer = document.querySelector("#fact");

button.addEventListener("click", function () {
  answer.textContent = "Sparrows: 41 visits in two weeks.";
});
```

Click the button and the empty paragraph fills in. In plain words: find the button, find the paragraph, and when the button is clicked, run a small function that sets the paragraph's text.^[53](#note-53)^ The script line goes at the end of the body so the button already exists when the script looks for it. Using a real `<button>` (rather than a styled box) means keyboards and screen readers know it's a button.^[49](#note-49)^

If nothing happens, open the Console with a parent (right-click, Inspect, then the Console tab) and read the error. It's the red text from Chapter 3, in a new place.

### In-home — WEB-07 View-Source Detective

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** a browser and a simple page a parent picks.
- **Safety and privacy:** parent chooses the page and sits alongside; no logging in anywhere.
- **The fun:** peeking behind the curtain.
- **The skill:** reading a simple page's HTML, and asking who made it and how.
- **The crumb:** three tags found and what each does.

Right-click a plain page (a simple MDN example page works well) and choose "View page source." The student finds three tags they know and writes what each does. Big commercial pages are a jungle of code; choose something small. **13–14:** Use "Inspect" to change a heading's text on screen, then refresh and watch it go back. Nothing you change in your own browser touches the real site, which is a useful thing to know.

### In-home — PROJ-01 Project One-Pager

- **Time:** 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** one sheet of paper.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** planning a real mini-site.
- **The skill:** problem, user, features, test plan, and schedule on one page.
- **The crumb:** the one-pager.

For a 13–14 three-page site, the student writes who the site is for, what each page does, how they'll test it (the access checklist and two first users), and which Hour each part gets done.

### In-home — PROJ-03 Demo Day

- **Time:** 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** the finished page or site on the family computer, a feedback sheet.
- **Safety and privacy:** family or co-op only; the page stays local.
- **The fun:** an audience.
- **The skill:** showing a project and collecting user feedback.
- **The crumb:** feedback notes.

Two or three family members try the page while the student watches without explaining. Each visitor answers two questions on the sheet: "What did you look at first?" and "What was confusing?" The student picks one change to make, makes it, and says why. That last step is the standard's "improve based on user feedback" in action.^[17](#note-17)^

### Out-of-home — OUT-01 Algorithms in the Wild

- **Time:** 20 minutes on an errand or walk.
- **Materials:** a pocket notebook.
- **Safety and privacy:** road rules; stay together in stores and parking lots; no photos of strangers or staff.
- **The fun:** reviewing the world's "pages."
- **The skill:** reading a real design and writing its rules in plain words.
- **The crumb:** a page of notes with one redesign sketch.

This week the walk becomes "who made this sign?" Signs, posters, menus, and screens are all designed for an audience, just like web pages. The student picks three and asks: Who is this for? What do you see first? What does it want you to do? Is anything hard to read? Then they write the sign's "rules" as pseudocode (a plan for a program in plain words): "IF a person walks in THEN they see the hours first." Back home, they sketch one improved version and label it with HTML tags. A self-checkout screen or a bus-stop timetable makes a great subject.

### Talk box (this chapter)

**Opening:** "Who is this page for — and what will they see first?"

**Follow-ups:**
- Prediction: "When you refresh, what will change?"
- Evidence: "What did you see on the screen that tells you that?"
- Location: "Which tag is the last one that's showing the way you expected?"
- Change: "What's the one thing you'd change so your visitor finds that sooner?"
- Revision: "Want to change your prediction? Save, refresh, and see."

**How to wait.** Count to five in your head. When the question is about code on the screen, let them read as long as they need. Look at the page or the code, not at the student.^[5](#note-5)^

**What a stuck silence usually means.** The question was too broad ("Is it good?"); ask about one part. They may not have thought about the visitor at all yet, which is normal; offer a specific visitor ("your grandmother on her phone"). They may be worried their design looks childish; keep comments about what a visitor can find and read. Or the page is so busy with colors that the question got lost; look at the wireframe instead.

**Don't say / say instead.** Don't say "That color's ugly." Say "Can you read this paragraph easily from across the table? Let's check."

### Blocked, then mixed

Spend a few Hours on HTML alone until opening and closing tags feel automatic. Then CSS alone, using a page that's already finished. Then mix: a mystery page with one HTML mistake and one CSS mistake, and the student has to say which layer each lives in. Knowing "is this structure, style, or behavior?" is half of web debugging.

### One incorrect example to diagnose

Here is an illustration, not a real family. Show your student this setup from an imaginary student. The folder holds `index.html` and `style.css`. The HTML has:

```html
<link rel="stylesheet" href="styles.css">
```

and the CSS has:

```css
H1 {
  colour: blue;
}
```

The student says, "None of my styles work." Ask: "How many problems can you find?" There are two that matter. The link line names `styles.css`, which doesn't exist, so no styles load at all. And `colour` is spelled the British way; CSS only understands `color`, so even with the right file name, that line would be skipped. (The capital `H1` happens to work, but lowercase is the habit.) A student who finds both has learned the two most common CSS bugs in one go.

### A week without a screen

Web design is unusually good on paper. Wireframes are paper by nature. For HTML, write tags on index cards (`<h1>`, `</h1>`, `<p>`, `</p>`) and have the student build a page on the table, matching every opener with its closer. Then a sibling or parent plays "browser": they read the cards and draw what the page would look like, following the tags literally. If a closing card is missing, the "browser" keeps the whole rest of the page bold, just as a real one might. The paper box model (WEB-04) and the "who made this sign?" walk need no screen either. A student who has played browser on paper reads real HTML much faster.

### Encouragement that means something

Name the move you saw:

- "You checked the link line before changing anything else. That saved you ten minutes."
- "Your alt text tells me exactly what's in the picture. Someone using a screen reader would know."
- "You watched your grandmother use the page without explaining it. That's hard, and it's what designers do."
- "You looked that property up on MDN yourself."

Avoid praising the page as "cool" or "professional-looking" and stopping there. The student can't repeat "cool" on purpose. They can repeat checking the link line.

### Siblings and co-ops

A family or co-op "web day" works well: each student builds one page on a shared theme (a hobby, a book, a local bird), and the pages link to each other through a shared index page on one computer. Everyone tests everyone else's page with the access checklist. Feedback rules: about the page, specific, and invited.

## For the student

Every site you've ever visited started as a blank file. Somebody typed some text, saved it, and opened it in a browser. You can do the same thing today, with tools already on your computer.

A web page has three layers:

- **HTML** is the structure: headings, paragraphs, lists, links, pictures.
- **CSS** is the style: colors, fonts, spacing.
- **JavaScript** is the behavior: things that happen when you click.

HTML uses **tags** in angle brackets. Most come in pairs, an opening tag and a closing tag with a slash:

```html
<h1>My Bird Report</h1>
<p>I counted birds for two weeks.</p>
```

`h1` is the main heading. `p` is a paragraph. Save this inside a full page skeleton as `index.html`, double-click it, and your browser shows it. That's a real web page. It's on your computer, not the internet, and nobody else can see it unless your family decides otherwise.

### A worked example

You want your heading to be dark blue. You make a file called `style.css` in the same folder and write:

```css
h1 {
  color: rgb(20 80 140);
}
```

You add `<link rel="stylesheet" href="style.css">` to the `<head>` of your page. Predict: the heading turns blue. Save both files, refresh the browser, and look. If it worked, great. If nothing changed, write Expected/Actual, then check the link line: does the file name match exactly? One wrong letter stops all your styles from loading.

### Make it work for everyone

Some people can't see the screen and use a screen reader, which reads the page aloud. Help them by:

- giving every picture alt text that says what it shows: `alt="Pencil drawing of a bird feeder with two sparrows"`;
- using headings in order (`h1`, then `h2`) instead of just making text bigger;
- writing links that say where they go, not "click here";
- choosing text colors that stand out clearly from the background.

This isn't extra credit. It's good manners, in code.

### Save, refresh, look

Web building has a rhythm. Type a few lines. Save. Switch to the browser. Refresh. Look. Predict before you refresh: what should change, and where? If you wait until you've typed fifty lines before looking, and something's wrong, you'll have fifty places to search. If you look after every three lines, you'll have three.

### Look it up like a pro

Professional web developers don't memorize every tag and property. They look things up constantly. When you forget how to make a link or center a heading, open MDN Web Docs with your parent and search for it. Read the short example at the top of the page, try it in your own file, and change one thing to see what happens. That's how you learn the tags you actually need, faster than any list.

You can also learn by reading other people's pages. Right-click a simple page your parent picks and choose "View page source." You'll see the same tags you've been writing. Every page on the web is built from them.

### Try this today

Draw a wireframe of a one-page site about something you know well. Label each box with its tag. Then build it: headings, one paragraph, one list, one link. Save and refresh after every few lines.

### Explain it back

Tell your parent what HTML, CSS, and JavaScript each do, using your own page as the example. Point to one line of each.

### This part is hard for everyone

The hardest part of web pages at first isn't the code. It's the files. Saving in the wrong folder, forgetting to save, a file secretly named `index.html.txt`, a link line with one wrong letter. Every web developer has stared at a page that wouldn't change and then realized they were editing a different copy. When nothing happens, check the file before you check the code.

### Challenge

Make a second page and link the two pages to each other. Use the same `style.css` for both, so changing one color changes it on both pages.

**If you're 13 or older:** add a button with JavaScript that changes some text when clicked (WEB-06). Then run a demo day: watch two people use your site without explaining it, write down what confused them, and fix one thing.

### What to save

- Your wireframe sketch.
- `index.html`, `style.css`, and (if you made one) `script.js`, all in one project folder.
- Your access checklist.
- Your demo-day feedback notes.
- Your Debug Log entries.

Keep the whole folder in two places. Pictures on your page should be ones you made, or ones you've credited and have permission to use. A page or picture made by a tool isn't your work, even if you asked for it.

## If it isn't clicking

**"Nothing changes when I refresh."** Check the file first: Is it saved? Is it the file the browser is showing (look at the address bar)? Is it really called `index.html`? If the computer hides file endings, a file can end up named `index.html.txt` without anyone noticing. Turning on "show file extensions" in the computer's settings is worth doing once, with a parent.

**"My CSS doesn't work."** If *nothing* is styled, check the link line and the file name. If *one* rule fails, check the selector's spelling and that every `{` has a matching `}` and every line ends with `;`. Look the property up on MDN to check its spelling.

**"My button does nothing."** JavaScript often fails silently on the page. Open the Console with a parent (right-click, Inspect, Console) and read the red text: last line first, then the line number. Check that the `id` in the HTML matches the name in the script exactly, including the `#`.

**"I don't know what to make."** That's a planning problem, not a coding one. Go back to WEB-01 and ask: who is this for? A page for a younger sibling, a grandparent, or a pet's fan club is easier to start than a page for "everyone."

Use the rescue order throughout: ask → wait → hint → short demo on your own copy → hand it back. If a week of HTML is still frustrating, step back to paper wireframes and tag labels until the structure feels natural.

## Tools, including AI

**A plain text editor and a browser** are all this chapter needs, with no installs and no accounts. Pages stay on your computer.

**MDN Web Docs** is free and is the reference for HTML, CSS, and JavaScript.^[47](#note-47)^ Its "Learn web development" section is a full beginner course if your student wants more.

**The Raspberry Pi Code Editor** is a free browser-based editor from the Raspberry Pi Foundation that includes HTML projects. It can be used without an account; saving work there needs one, and for children under 13 a parent sets up the account with the parent's email.^[35](#note-35)^ The rules have been changing, so check the current rules before using it.

**CodeAI (formerly Code.org)** offers Web Lab inside its free middle-school courses, CS Discoveries and the newer AI Discoveries. It's an optional course path, not a requirement. A free CodeAI account is required. At 11–12, a parent creates and holds the account. CodeAI describes its AI Tutor as for students 13 and up, and its newer Web Lab includes AI-assisted features, so keep any AI helpers inside the course off at 11–12.^[54](#note-54),[55](#note-55),[11](#note-11)^

**AI.** For the rules, see the AI box in **The Computing Hour**. What's different for web work:

- Pages made by an AI are made by a tool and don't go in the portfolio as the student's work.
- **13–14:** After looking a CSS property up on MDN first, your student may ask an AI, with you nearby, to explain it in other words, and then check that explanation against MDN. Any code an AI suggests goes through AI-07 Explain Every Line, with a "Help I used" line and a Whose Work card.
- **11–12:** Parent-operated only, as always.

Check the current terms of any service before anyone uses it.

## What "done enough" looks like

Your student has met this chapter's goals when they can:

- build a page from a blank file with headings, paragraphs, a list, and a link;
- style it with a separate CSS file and explain what a selector is;
- label the four layers of the box model;
- check a page for alt text, heading order, readable contrast, and clear links;
- find and fix a broken link line or a misspelled property;
- at 13–14, add a button with JavaScript and improve the site after a demo day.

**AS checks.** Try AS-01 Cold Trace with a short HTML snippet ("What will this look like?"), AS-02 Debug on the Spot with a page that has one planted bug, and AS-03 Explain Your Code on the student's own page. All three are AI-free.

**Placement by skill.** A twelve-year-old who styles a page confidently can try WEB-06. A fourteen-year-old who still loses track of files needs more time with folders and file names (Chapter 6 helps), and that's worth the time.

**The life of the habit.** A small personal page is a lovely thing to keep updating over a year: new sections, better styling, a button or two. Every version is a record of growth. Keep the old versions in a dated folder.

\newpage

# Chapter 6

## How Computers and Networks Really Work — and Hardware You Can Program If You Like

![Binary cards with 16, 4 and 1 dots face up and two face down, beside an opened old computer case, a memory stick, a cable and an adult's screwdriver tray — still life, no people, no logos, no readable text.](art/ch06-opener.jpg){width=100%}

## Why this matters

Everything on a computer is a number, including this sentence. Every letter, every pixel of every photo, every note of every song is stored as a pattern of two states: on or off, written 1 or 0. This chapter shows your student how that works, and it's one of the most satisfying ideas in computing, because it turns the machine from magic into something they can explain.

Few people of any age can say what happens between tapping a link and seeing a page. By the end of this chapter, your student can. They'll count to 255 with eight paper cards, draw a picture from a grid of digits, act out a message crossing the internet in pieces, and scramble a secret with a paper wheel.

The national computer science standards for ages 11–14 cover all of this: how data is represented and how precise it needs to be; what devices do and how their parts work together; how information is broken into packets, sent, and put back together; why the internet keeps working when parts of it fail; and why keeping data private, accurate, and available matters.^[17](#note-17)^ In plain words: what is the computer actually doing, and where does the information go next?

An optional strand uses a small programmable board called a micro:bit, with buttons, lights, and sensors, programmed in Python. You don't need to buy anything; a free browser simulator does nearly everything the board does.

Understanding the machine also makes your student safer: a student who knows what the little lock in the browser does and doesn't mean is harder to fool.

## For the parent: understand it yourself

### Numbers made of two digits

**The everyday picture.** A row of light switches. Each is either on or off. With one switch you can show two things. With two switches, four (off-off, off-on, on-off, on-on). Every switch you add doubles the count.

**The precise picture.** Each switch is a **bit**, a 1 or a 0. Eight bits make a **byte**. The bits stand for place values that double from right to left: 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 128. Add up the places that are "on." So `00001101` is 8 + 4 + 1 = 13. All eight on, `11111111`, is 128 + 64 + 32 + 16 + 8 + 4 + 2 + 1 = 255. That's why so many computer numbers stop at 255: it's the biggest number one byte can hold, and with zero there are 256 possible values.

A pattern of bits means something because everyone agreed what it means. The same eight bits can stand for a number, a letter, or a shade of red.

### Text, pictures, and color

**Text.** Each character has an agreed number, called a character code. Python shows it with `ord()`: `ord("H")` gives 72, and `chr(72)` turns it back into `"H"`.^[44](#note-44)^

```python
for letter in "Hi!":
    code = ord(letter)
    print(letter, code, format(code, "08b"))
```

This prints `H 72 01001000`, then `i 105 01101001`, then `! 33 00100001`. Each letter is a number, and each number is eight bits.

**Pictures.** A screen is a grid of tiny squares called **pixels** (short for "picture elements"). In a black-and-white picture, each pixel can be one bit: 1 for black, 0 for white. In a color picture, each pixel usually gets three numbers, for red, green, and blue, each from 0 to 255.^[48](#note-48)^ Your student met those numbers in Chapter 5's CSS: `rgb(20 80 140)` is a little red, more green, and a lot of blue.

**Squeezing it.** Pictures take a lot of numbers, so computers compress them, storing the same picture in less space. One simple method is run-length encoding: instead of "white, white, white, white, black, black, white, white," you write "4, 2, 2," always starting with white. Fax machines relied heavily on this idea, and a form of it still works behind the scenes in photo formats.^[56](#note-56)^ It works well for long runs of one color and badly when colors change every pixel.

### The parts of a computer

- The **processor** follows instructions and does calculations, one tiny step after another, very fast.
- **Memory** is fast, short-term space for whatever is happening right now.
- **Storage** (a drive) keeps files for later, even after you switch off, but works more slowly. Unsaved work lives only in short-term memory; saving writes it to storage.
- The **operating system** is the manager. It handles the keyboard and mouse, draws on the screen, moves things around in memory, and decides which program gets which part of the machine.^[57](#note-57)^

Some computers also have **graphics chips**: processors with thousands of small parts that do huge numbers of simple sums at the same time. They were built to draw pixels quickly, and the same trick makes them good at training AI.^[58](#note-58)^ Big data centers full of those chips use a lot of electricity; the International Energy Agency estimated that data centers used about 1.5 percent of the world's electricity in 2024.^[59](#note-59)^ Chapter 8 returns to this.

**Files and folders.** Storage is organized as a tree of folders. A file's full address, its **path**, lists the folders from the top down. The ending after the dot, the **extension** (`.py`, `.html`, `.csv`), tells the computer what kind of file it is. Good names help: `2026-10-06-quiz-v2.py` sorts itself by date and says what it is. And every important file lives in two places.

### How the internet moves information

**The everyday picture.** Mailing a long letter as numbered postcards. Each postcard carries the address, its number, and how many there are in total. They may travel by different trucks and arrive out of order. The receiver sorts them by number and asks again for any that went missing.

**The precise picture.** That's almost exactly how it works. Data travels in small chunks called **packets**. Each one carries the sending and receiving addresses and its place in the order. Packets can take different paths and arrive out of order; the receiving computer puts them back together and asks for any that are missing.^[52](#note-52)^ **Routers** pass packets along, using **IP addresses**, which work much like street addresses. Your home "router" is often three devices in one box: a switch, a router, and a modem that connects to your internet provider.^[51](#note-51)^

People don't remember number addresses, so the internet has an address book. The **Domain Name System (DNS)** turns a name like `developer.mozilla.org` into the IP address of the right computer.^[52](#note-52)^ The internet is the network itself; the web is one service that runs on top of it.^[51](#note-51)^

Why doesn't the internet stop when one cable breaks? There are many routes; if one fails, packets find another. MDN describes the internet's design as making sure that, whatever happens, computers find a way to stay connected.^[51](#note-51)^

### Keeping secrets

**Encryption** means scrambling a message so only the right person can unscramble it. The oldest classroom example is the **Caesar cipher**: shift every letter three places, so HELLO becomes KHOOR. It's fun and very weak. In English there are only 25 useful shifts, so anyone can try them all in a few minutes.

Modern encryption uses an idea that sounds impossible at first: **public-key encryption**. Think of a padlock. You can hand out open padlocks to anyone, but only you have the key. Anyone can snap your padlock shut on a box, but only you can open it. In the real system, keys come in pairs; one is public, one is kept private. Anyone can encrypt a message to the owner of the private key, but only that owner can decrypt it. Real systems commonly use this padlock idea to share a secret key safely, then use that faster shared key for the messages themselves.^[60](#note-60)^ Longer keys are stronger for the same reason more switches make more patterns: every extra bit doubles the number of possible keys.

**The lock in the browser.** A web address starting with `https` means the connection is encrypted, which keeps others from reading the data as it travels.^[61](#note-61)^ It does *not* mean the site is honest. The FBI has warned that scammers use these locks too, and advised: "Do not trust a website just because it has a lock icon or 'https' in the browser address bar."^[62](#note-62)^ A private connection to a fake site is still a fake site. Chapter 7 teaches how to spot one.

### Wrong answers you should be able to hear

- **"Binary is just 0 and 1."** True but incomplete. Reply: "Right, so how does 01001000 become the letter H?" The meaning comes from place value and agreement.
- **"The lock icon means the site is safe."** Reply: "It means nobody can read what we send on the way. Does it tell us who's on the other end?"
- **"Memory and storage are the same thing."** Reply: "What happens to a document you never saved when the power goes out?"
- **"The internet is in the air."** Wi-Fi is the last few feet. Reply: "Where does the signal go after the router? Let's go look for the cables."
- **"My Caesar message is uncrackable."** Reply: "How many different shifts are there? How long would it take to try them all?"

### Your five-minute warm-up

Make the eight binary cards described below and show yourself 13 and 255. Then run the `ord()` loop above in Thonny with your own first name. Practice the opening question aloud once, and count to five in your head.

## How to teach it this week

This chapter is three to four weeks of work, best taken in three parts: data as numbers, the machine, and networks and secrets. The optional micro:bit strand can run alongside any of them.

Many of these Hours are unplugged, and that's not a lesser version: paper is the clearest way to see what the machine does. Use the usual steps:

- **Unplug and Plan.** Cards, grids, wheels, and role cards.
- **Predict.** "What number will these cards make?" "Which packet will arrive first?"
- **Build and Test.** Do the activity, then check it with a short Python line or by decoding.
- **Debug and Log.** Expected/Actual for every surprise.
- **Save and Explain.** "I predicted… It did… Next I'd change…" and "Help I used: …"

### In-home — BIN-01 Binary Cards to 255

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** eight index cards and a marker.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** counting higher than you'd think with only eight cards.
- **The skill:** five, then eight, binary cards; bits and bytes.
- **The crumb:** a card chart.

On the cards, draw dots: 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 128. Lay them in a row with 128 on the left. A card face up is a 1; face down is a 0. Ask for 13: the student turns up 8, 4, and 1, and reads `00001101`. Then: "What's the biggest number you can make?" (All up: 255.) "What happens if we add a ninth card? What would it be worth?"

**11–12:** Start with five cards (1 to 16) and count from 0 to 31 in order, turning cards like an odometer. Move to eight once the pattern clicks. **13–14:** Go straight to eight, then write numbers in binary on paper and check with Python's `format(13, "08b")`.

### How to fade your help

Use the rescue order: **ask → wait → hint → short demo on your own copy → hand it back.** For number work, the best hint is a smaller example: "Show me 3. Now show me 6. What did you notice?"

**11–12:** Five-then-eight-bit cards, black-and-white pixel grids, and the Caesar wheel. Skip compression and the padlock exchange unless the student asks.

**13–14:** Add color numbers, compression, the padlock box exchange, and why longer keys are stronger. If you have a micro:bit or want to use the simulator, add sensor projects. The student leads; you're **first user** and safety-keeper.

**Ready 12:** A twelve-year-old who explains why eight bits stop at 255 is ready for BIN-04 and NET-04.

### A worked Hour (illustration)

Here is an illustration, not a real family. Picture a thirteen-year-old and his mother at the kitchen table. In Unplug and Plan, he cuts a message into six numbered strips. His mother asks the opening question: "What is the computer actually doing — and where does the information go next?" He says, "Breaking it into pieces. They go to the router." She counts to five in her head. "And then?" "Different routers. They might go different ways." In Predict, he guesses the pieces will arrive in order. They play NET-01 with his little sister as a second router. Piece 4 arrives first; piece 2 "gets lost" behind the couch. He has to ask for it again. In Save and Explain he says: "I predicted they'd come in order. They didn't. Next I'd add a 'total number of pieces' to each strip so you know when you have them all. Help I used: none." That's the real packet design, discovered at a kitchen table.

## Practice that actually builds learning

### In-home — BIN-02 Your Name in Numbers

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** a printed character-code chart for capital and lowercase letters (or Thonny to look them up), paper.
- **Safety and privacy:** first names only.
- **The fun:** a secret-looking name tag.
- **The skill:** text as numbers.
- **The crumb:** the encoded name.

The student writes their first name as character codes (H = 72, i = 105), then as eight-bit binary using the cards. A sibling decodes it. **13–14:** Write the `ord()` loop from the parent section and check the paper work against it.

### In-home — BIN-03 Pixel Grid Picture

- **Time:** 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** graph paper, pencils.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** a picture that's secretly a list of numbers.
- **The skill:** images as grids of numbers; color as three numbers.
- **The crumb:** the grid sheet.

On an 8-by-8 grid, the student draws a simple shape (a heart, a house, a cat face). Then they write each row as 1s and 0s. A parent decodes the rows onto a blank grid without seeing the original. **11–12:** Black and white only. **13–14:** Pick three colors and write each as red, green, and blue numbers, then try them in a CSS file from Chapter 5.

### In-home — BIN-04 Squeeze It

*For 13–14; ready 12.*

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** the pixel grid from BIN-03.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** making a picture smaller without losing anything.
- **The skill:** run-length compression.
- **The crumb:** before and after counts.

Rewrite each row of the grid as run lengths, starting with white: `WWWWBBWW` becomes 4, 2, 2. If a row starts with black, it begins with 0. Count how many numbers the original used (64) and how many the squeezed version uses. Then design a grid that compresses *badly* (a checkerboard). In Python:

```python
row = "WWWWBBWW"
runs = []
count = 1
for i in range(1, len(row)):
    if row[i] == row[i - 1]:
        count = count + 1
    else:
        runs.append(count)
        count = 1
runs.append(count)
print(runs)
```

It prints `[4, 2, 2]`. Challenge: what does it print for `"BBWW"`, and how would you fix it to start with 0?

### In-home — SYS-01 Input-Process-Output Hunt

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** a clipboard.
- **Safety and privacy:** look, don't open or unplug anything.
- **The fun:** finding computers hiding in ordinary things.
- **The skill:** inputs, processing, outputs, and storage in household devices.
- **The crumb:** a labeled list.

For each device in the house (microwave, thermostat, washing machine), the student writes its inputs, what it processes, its outputs, and anything it stores (a saved time, a favorite setting).

### In-home — SYS-02 The Operating System Plays Manager

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** role cards: Manager, three Programs, Memory (a small tray), Screen (a whiteboard).
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** a play where the boss is the operating system.
- **The skill:** how the operating system shares memory, time, and files among programs.
- **The crumb:** the role cards.

The student plays the operating system. Siblings or parents are programs asking for turns: "I need the screen!" "I need space in memory!" The manager gives each a turn and a space on the tray, and says no when the tray is full.

### In-home — SYS-03 Folder Tree and File Names

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** the family computer, with a parent.
- **Safety and privacy:** student's own folders only; save in two places (see Family safety basics in **The Computing Hour**).
- **The fun:** taming a messy desktop.
- **The skill:** folders, extensions, date-first names, and two places.
- **The crumb:** a screenshot of the folder tree, cropped to the folders.

Draw the student's project folders as a tree on paper first. Then build it: one folder per chapter or project, files named with the date first. Turn on "show file extensions" together if it's off. Finally, copy the whole folder to a second place the family controls, like a USB drive or the family drive.

### In-home — SYS-04 Inside an Old Computer

- **Time:** 30 minutes.
- **Materials:** an old desktop computer, a screwdriver, a clear table.
- **Safety and privacy:** an adult opens it, and it stays unplugged the whole time. Leave batteries, the power supply box, and any old tube-style screen alone; those stay closed. Wash hands afterward.
- **The fun:** seeing the real parts.
- **The skill:** naming the processor, memory, and storage.
- **The crumb:** a labeled sketch.

With the case open, the student finds and sketches the processor (often under a fan or metal fins), the memory sticks, and the storage drive, with arrows for how information moves. No old computer? A library book with clear photos works fine.

### In-home — NET-01 Packet Relay

- **Time:** 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** a short message written on paper, scissors, two or three helpers.
- **Safety and privacy:** clear floor space.
- **The fun:** a relay race where the message breaks apart.
- **The skill:** messages split into numbered packets that take different routes.
- **The crumb:** the reassembled message.

Cut a message into six numbered strips marked "to" and "from." Helpers (siblings work well) stand around the house as routers and pass strips along by different paths. The receiver reassembles them in order. Hide one strip; the receiver must notice it's missing and ask for it again.

### In-home — NET-02 The Internet's Address Book

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** index cards.
- **Safety and privacy:** make up addresses; no real ones.
- **The fun:** playing the internet's phone book.
- **The skill:** IP addresses and DNS as a lookup.
- **The crumb:** a lookup chain sketch.

Write made-up site names on cards ("birdfacts"). A parent holds the "address book" matching each name to a made-up number address (like `10.0.0.7`). The student looks up a name, gets a number, and delivers a message to the "computer" (a family member) with that number. Then sketch the chain: name, address book, number, computer.

### In-home — NET-03 Caesar Wheel

- **Time:** 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** two paper circles (one smaller), a brad fastener, a marker.
- **Safety and privacy:** practice messages only, never real passwords.
- **The fun:** secret messages.
- **The skill:** encrypting and decrypting; why 25 tries break it.
- **The crumb:** the paper wheel and a solved message.

Write the alphabet around both circles, turn the inner one three places, and encrypt a message. Then the real lesson: break a message *without* being told the shift, trying shifts one at a time. How long did it take? That's why nobody uses this for real secrets.

**13–14:** Write the Caesar shift in Python:

```python
message = "HELLO"
shift = 3
secret = ""
for letter in message:
    number = (ord(letter) - ord("A") + shift) % 26
    secret = secret + chr(number + ord("A"))
print(secret)
```

It prints `KHOOR`. Then write a loop that tries all 25 shifts on a secret message and prints each one. The right answer jumps out.

### In-home — NET-04 Padlock Box Exchange

*For 13–14; ready 12.*

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** a small box, a padlock with a key, and a note.
- **Safety and privacy:** none needed.
- **The fun:** solving a puzzle that sounds impossible.
- **The skill:** the public-key idea with a padlock and a box.
- **The crumb:** a diagram.

The puzzle: you want a secret note, but you can't meet the sender to give them a key. Solution: send your *open* padlock and keep the key. They lock the note in the box with your padlock and send it back. Only you can open it. The open padlock is the public key; your key is the private key. The student draws the exchange as a diagram with arrows.

### In-home — NET-05 Look for the Lock

- **Time:** 10 minutes.
- **Materials:** a parent's device and browser.
- **Safety and privacy:** parent drives; visit only sites the parent chooses; no logging in.
- **The fun:** finding out what the little lock really means.
- **The skill:** what HTTPS does and doesn't mean.
- **The crumb:** a one-sentence note.

On a site you trust, find the lock and the `https`. Ask: "Could a fake site have a lock too?" (Yes.) The student writes one sentence: "The lock means the connection is private; it doesn't tell you the site is honest." Chapter 7 picks this up.

### Out-of-home — OUT-04 Network Spotting

- **Time:** 20 minutes on a walk.
- **Materials:** a pocket notebook.
- **Safety and privacy:** road rules; stay on sidewalks; look, don't touch or climb; no photos of strangers.
- **The fun:** finding the internet, which turns out to be very physical.
- **The skill:** spotting routers, cables, and towers.
- **The crumb:** a spotting list.

Find the router and the cable that leaves the house. Outside, look for cables on utility poles, gray boxes at the curb, and cell towers on the skyline. Back home, draw a map from your router to "the internet," with question marks where you're not sure.

### The optional strand: a computer you can hold

A **micro:bit** is a small programmable board with two buttons (A and B), 25 lights in a 5-by-5 grid that also act as a light sensor, a temperature sensor inside its processor, a motion sensor, a compass, and a radio. The newer version (V2) adds a touch-sensitive logo, a microphone, and a speaker. It runs on two AAA batteries or a USB cable.^[63](#note-63)^ Students write real Python for it in the free micro:bit Python Editor, which runs in the browser and needs no logins or passwords.^[64](#note-64)^

Everything below has a simulator path. No hardware is required. The programs follow the micro:bit's official MicroPython documentation but weren't tested on a physical board, so treat any surprise as a debugging job.

### In-home — PHYS-04 Simulator First

- **Time:** 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** a computer with a browser; a parent opens the micro:bit Python Editor.
- **Safety and privacy:** no account needed; save the file to the family drive.
- **The fun:** programming a gadget you don't own yet.
- **The skill:** programming a micro:bit in the browser simulator.
- **The crumb:** the program file.

The editor's simulator shows a micro:bit on screen with clickable buttons and sliders for light, temperature, and other sensors, and it keeps working without internet once loaded.^[64](#note-64)^ The simulator shows the V2 board, so a few programs may not run on an older V1. Try PHYS-01 here first.

### In-home — PHYS-01 Name Badge That Scrolls

- **Time:** 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** a micro:bit and USB cable, or the simulator.
- **Safety and privacy:** a first name or a nickname only.
- **The fun:** a badge that talks back.
- **The skill:** a micro:bit program with buttons as events.
- **The crumb:** the program file.

```python
from microbit import *

while True:
    if button_a.is_pressed():
        display.scroll("HELLO")
    if button_b.is_pressed():
        display.show(Image.HAPPY)
    sleep(100)
```

Press A and HELLO scrolls across the lights. Press B and a smiley face appears.^[65](#note-65)^ Change "HELLO" to a first name. Predict first: what happens if you press both buttons?

### In-home — PHYS-02 Sensor Logger

- **Time:** 25 minutes.
- **Materials:** a micro:bit V2 (data logging is a V2 feature), or the simulator.
- **Safety and privacy:** logging stays on the device; nothing is sent anywhere.
- **The fun:** a gadget that collects data while you're away.
- **The skill:** reading a light or temperature sensor and logging data for a spreadsheet.
- **The crumb:** the data file.

```python
from microbit import *
import log

log.set_labels("temp", "light")

while True:
    if button_a.is_pressed():
        log.add(temp=temperature(), light=display.read_light_level())
        display.show(Image.YES)
        sleep(500)
        display.clear()
```

Each press of A records a temperature (in degrees Celsius) and a light level. On a real V2 board, the data is saved in a file called `MY_DATA` on the micro:bit's drive, which opens as a table in a browser and can be copied into a spreadsheet.^[66](#note-66)^ Then the spreadsheet skills from Chapter 4 take over: "Is the kitchen windowsill warmer than the hallway?"

### In-home — PHYS-03 Reaction Game

*For 13–14; ready 12.*

- **Time:** 30 minutes.
- **Materials:** a micro:bit or the simulator.
- **Safety and privacy:** first names only on any score list.
- **The fun:** a game the whole family will want to beat.
- **The skill:** variables, random timing, and buttons.
- **The crumb:** the program file plus the best time.

```python
from microbit import *
import random

display.show("?")
sleep(random.randint(1000, 4000))
display.show(Image.HEART)
start = running_time()
while not button_a.is_pressed():
    sleep(1)
reaction = running_time() - start
display.scroll(str(reaction))
```

After a random wait, a heart appears; press A as fast as you can, and the board scrolls your time in milliseconds.^[65](#note-65)^ Then debug it like Chapter 3: what happens if someone holds A down before the heart appears? (They get a suspiciously perfect time.) How would you catch a cheater?

### Talk box (this chapter)

**Opening:** "What is the computer actually doing — and where does the information go next?"

**Follow-ups:**
- Prediction: "If you add one more card, what's the biggest number now?"
- Evidence: "What did you see that makes you think the packets took different paths?"
- Location: "Where exactly is the information right now: memory, storage, or on its way somewhere?"
- Change: "What's one thing you'd change to make the secret harder to break?"
- Revision: "Want to change your prediction? Let's try it again."

**How to wait.** Count to five in your head. When the question is about code on the screen, let them read as long as they need. Look at the cards or the code, not at the student.^[5](#note-5)^

**What a stuck silence usually means.** The question was too abstract; put a physical object in their hands (a card, a packet strip). They may be guessing what you want to hear; say "There's no wrong guess here, only a guess we test." They may not have a picture in their head yet; draw one together. Or the screen is louder than the question; close it and go back to paper.

**Don't say / say instead.** Don't say "No, the internet isn't wireless." Say "Let's follow the signal. Where does it go after the router?"

### Blocked, then mixed

Spend a few days on each part alone. Then mix them in one puzzle: "A photo you send to Grandma: where is it stored, how is it written as numbers, how does it travel, and who could read it on the way?" A student who can answer that has put the whole chapter together.

### One incorrect example to diagnose

Here is an illustration, not a real family. An imaginary student writes: "I invented an unbreakable code. I shift every letter by 7 instead of 3, so nobody will guess it." Ask your student: "Is it unbreakable? How would you break it?" (Try all 25 shifts; shift 7 is just one of them.) "What would make it stronger?" (More possible keys, so trying them all takes too long.) Real encryption uses keys with so many possibilities that trying them all isn't practical.

## For the student

Everything on a computer is a number, including this sentence.

**Bits and bytes.** A computer stores everything as switches that are on or off, written 1 and 0. Each one is a **bit**. Eight bits make a **byte**. The places in a byte are worth 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, and 128, doubling each time from the right. To read a byte, add up the places that are 1:

`00001101` = 8 + 4 + 1 = **13**

All eight on is 255. That's why you see 255 everywhere in computing, like in color numbers.

**Letters are numbers too.** Each character has an agreed code. In Python, `ord("H")` gives 72. So "Hi" is really 72 and 105, which in binary is `01001000 01101001`.

**Pictures are grids of numbers.** Each tiny square on a screen is a pixel. In a color picture, each pixel has three numbers: how much red, green, and blue, each from 0 to 255.

### A worked example

Let's follow a message you send to a friend. You type it; the keyboard is an **input**. The **processor** turns each letter into numbers. The message sits in **memory** while you write. When you press Send, the computer breaks it into **packets**, small numbered pieces, each with an address. Your **router** passes them on, and other routers pass them along, maybe by different paths. Your friend's device collects the packets, puts them in order by number, asks again for any that went missing, and shows the message. If the connection uses **encryption**, scrambling the message so only the right person can unscramble it, nobody along the way can read it.

**Why this helps you.** Knowing how things work makes you harder to fool. When you understand that a message travels through many machines, you see why encryption matters. When you know the browser lock only means "private connection," you won't trust a fake site just because it has one.

**A computer you can hold.** If your family uses the micro:bit simulator or a real board, you'll program buttons, lights, and sensors in Python. It's the same idea as everything above: inputs come in, the processor follows your instructions, and outputs come out.

### Try this today

Make eight binary cards (1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 128). Show your age in binary. Then your birthday month and day. Then the biggest number you can make.

### Explain it back

Explain to your parent why the lock in a browser's address bar doesn't prove a website is honest. Use the word "encrypted" correctly.

### This part is hard for everyone

The padlock idea, where you can give away the way to lock something but keep the way to open it, feels wrong at first. Nearly everyone has to act it out with a real box and padlock before it makes sense. That's fine. That's what the activity is for.

Memory versus storage confuses people too. Here's the short version: memory is your desk (fast, for what you're working on now); storage is the filing cabinet (slower, but it keeps things). Saving moves your work from the desk to the cabinet. That's why a power outage can lose the work you hadn't saved yet, but not the files you had.

### Challenge

Encrypt a message with a Caesar wheel and give it to someone *without* telling them the shift. Time how long it takes them to break it. Then explain why that proves Caesar ciphers aren't safe for real secrets.

**If you're 13 or older:** a code with 8-bit keys has 256 possible keys. How many does a 9-bit key have? A 16-bit key? Work out the pattern, and explain why longer keys are stronger. If you have a micro:bit or the simulator, build the reaction game and then find a way to stop people cheating.

### What to save

- Your binary card chart and your name in numbers.
- Your pixel grid and its rows written as numbers.
- Your labeled sketch of a computer's parts (or a device's inputs and outputs).
- Your packet relay message and your network spotting list.
- Your Caesar wheel and one solved message.
- Any micro:bit programs.
- Your Debug Log entries.

Save your files in two places, in folders with clear, date-first names.

## If it isn't clicking

**Binary doesn't make sense.** Go back to five cards and count from 0 to 31 out loud, turning cards over like a car's odometer. The pattern of flips is the lesson. Skip conversion on paper until the cards feel easy.

**Networks feel invisible.** That's because they mostly are. Do NET-01 and OUT-04 before any explanation. Seeing the router and the cable leaving the house makes the rest concrete.

**The padlock idea won't land.** Act it out with a real padlock and box, twice, swapping roles. Then draw it. Then, and only then, use the words "public key" and "private key."

**The micro:bit is frustrating.** Go back to the simulator. Check that the program starts with `from microbit import *`, and read any error last line first, as in Chapter 3.

Use the rescue order: ask → wait → hint → short demo on your own copy → hand it back. Binary often clicks suddenly after a few days of not clicking.

## Tools, including AI

**Paper first.** Index cards, graph paper, a brad fastener, a box and padlock. Most of this chapter needs nothing else.

**CS Unplugged**, from the University of Canterbury, is a free collection of computing activities without computers, including binary cards and picture compression, if you want more practice.^[56](#note-56)^

**Thonny**, the free offline Python editor, handles the `ord()`, binary, compression, and Caesar programs.^[2](#note-2)^

**The micro:bit Python Editor** is free, runs in the browser, needs no logins, and includes a simulator.^[64](#note-64)^ A real micro:bit is optional; borrow one from a library or co-op before buying, and check the current price yourself.

**AI.** For the rules, see the AI box in **The Computing Hour**. Nothing in this chapter needs AI. At 13–14, with you present, a student might ask an AI to explain an idea like DNS in other words *after* doing the activity, then check that explanation against a real source like MDN. At 11–12, AI stays parent-operated. Check the current terms of any service.

## What "done enough" looks like

Your student has met this chapter's goals when they can:

- count to 255 with binary cards and explain why eight bits stop there;
- explain how text and pictures become numbers;
- name the processor, memory, storage, and operating system, and say what each does;
- organize files in folders with clear names, saved in two places;
- explain packets, routers, IP addresses, and DNS using the relay game;
- encrypt and break a Caesar message, and explain why it's weak;
- say what the browser lock means and doesn't mean;
- at 13–14, explain the padlock idea and why longer keys are stronger.

**AS checks.** Try AS-01 Cold Trace on the Caesar program, AS-02 Debug on the Spot with a planted bug in the compression code, and AS-03 Explain Your Code on any micro:bit program. All three are AI-free.

**Placement by skill.** A twelve-year-old who explains why 255 is the limit is ready for compression and the padlock. A fourteen-year-old still unsure about binary needs more card time, and there's no shame in that.

**The life of the habit.** Keep asking the opening question whenever a new device comes into the house: what is it actually doing, and where does the information go? A student who asks that about every gadget is thinking like an engineer.

\newpage

# Chapter 7

## Safe, Smart, and Kind Online: Passwords, Scams, Social Media, and Your Footprint

![A common room with two phones lying face down on a charging shelf, a sofa, a chair, a plant and a window — still life, no people, no logos, no readable text.](art/ch07-opener.jpg){width=100%}

## Why this matters

Here is an illustration, not a real family. A twelve-year-old is at the kitchen table on a Tuesday when a text arrives on the family tablet: "Your game account will be DELETED in 24 hours. Verify your login now," with a link underneath. She almost taps it. Then she slides the tablet across the table and says, "Is this real?"

That slide across the table is the whole chapter. She didn't need to know every trick a scammer uses. She noticed the hurry, felt the little tug of worry, and brought it to someone she trusts. Everything in these pages makes that move easier and more automatic.

These are the years of first phones, first accounts, and first group chats. They are also the years when students can understand *why* the habits work. Habits learned now, with you beside them, last long after they've stopped asking your permission.

CSTA, the Computer Science Teachers Association, expects middle-schoolers to "evaluate common types of cyber attacks and preventions," and to weigh the benefits and risks of letting personal data and metadata be collected.^[17](#note-17)^ In ordinary words: know how people get tricked, know how to stop it, and know what you're giving away when you share.

The tone here is calm. No horror stories, no scary videos. Four habits do most of the work: spot the phish, use strong passphrases, turn on two-step login, and keep things updated. Around them sit three more: think before you share, be kind when nobody is watching, and when something feels wrong, Stop, Save, Tell. None of this makes anyone perfectly safe. Nothing does. It gives your student a plan and keeps you in the loop.

**A timing note.** If a phone is arriving this month, teach this chapter now, out of order, starting with SAFE-08 Family Device Agreement. Chapters 4 through 6 will wait.

**This week you can learn** the four habits well enough to explain each in a sentence. **Today your student can try** sorting three printed messages into "phish" and "fine," and saying why.

## For the parent: understand it yourself

You don't need to be a security expert. You need the few habits that matter most, why they work, and what to say when your student pushes back.

### The everyday picture

Think about your front door. Most break-ins happen because a door was left open, a key was under the mat, or someone knocked, sounded official, and was let in. Most online break-ins work the same way: a reused password, a missing second lock, or a convincing knock: a message that says "urgent" and asks for your key.

### The precise picture

An **account** is a locked room on someone else's computer; logging in proves who you are. A **phishing** message is a fake knock: an email, text, or chat pretending to be a company, a friend, or a teacher, built to get a password, a code, money, or personal details. **Personal data** is anything that points to a real person: name, address, school, birthday, face, location. **Metadata** is data about data: a photo file can carry the date, the device, and sometimes the exact place it was taken.

### Four habits that do most of the work

The Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA), a federal agency, boils home security down to "four easy ways": recognize and report phishing; use strong passwords and a password manager; add a second login step; and update software.^[67](#note-67)^ This chapter is built on those four.

**1. Spot the phish.** Phishing messages hurry you ("within 24 hours"). They threaten a loss or promise a prize. They come from a sender that's almost right, greet you vaguely ("Dear customer"), and ask for a password, a payment, a gift card, or a login code. The Federal Trade Commission's sample scam email shows exactly these signs: a generic greeting, an account "on hold" for a billing problem, and a link to "update" payment details.^[68](#note-68)^ Your student doesn't have to judge every message alone. The rule is simple: if it hurries you and asks for something, slow down and show a parent.

**2. Strong passphrases, one per account.** The federal password guidelines, from the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST), have changed in ways that make life easier. A password used on its own should be at least 15 characters; services shouldn't demand mixtures of symbols and capitals; and nobody should be forced to change passwords on a schedule, only when one has leaked.^[69](#note-69)^ Length beats tricks. Four or five unrelated words strung together are strong and memorable. The real danger is reuse: one leaked password unlocks every account that shares it. So: a different passphrase for each account, and a password manager (an app that remembers them behind one strong passphrase) in the parent's hands at 11–12 and shared at 13–14.

**3. Two-step login.** You'll see it called **multifactor authentication**, which means logging in with a password plus a second thing. This book calls it two-step login. The FTC sorts the extra pieces into three kinds: something you know (a PIN), something you have (a code on your phone or a security key), and something you are (a fingerprint or face).^[68](#note-68)^ A stolen password alone then isn't enough. Say one rule out loud at the table: a login code is typed only into a login page you opened yourself, never read out to someone who asks for it.

**4. Updates on.** Updates fix holes criminals already know about. Turn on automatic updates and don't put off the ones that ask. At 11–12, the parent approves new apps.

### Privacy settings and the photo that says too much

Every app and device has privacy settings: who can see the profile, who can send messages, whether location is shared. The company sets the defaults, not your family. A settings tour together once a semester (SAFE-06) is one of the most useful twenty minutes in this book.

A picture of your student in a team shirt in front of your house, with location data still in the file, tells a stranger a great deal. In this family, faces, school names, and towns stay out of anything shared beyond the family, and screenshots are cropped to the work. See Family safety basics in **The Computing Hour**.

### The 13 shift

> **COPPA in two sentences.** A federal law, the Children's Online Privacy Protection Act, says websites and apps aimed at children under 13 (or that know they're collecting from a child that young) must get a parent's consent before collecting the child's personal information, and photos, videos, and voice recordings count.^[4](#note-4)^ That protection ends at 13, which is why so many services set 13 as their minimum age.

The rule was updated in 2025, and companies have had to follow most of the new version since 22 April 2026.^[70](#note-70)^ COPPA governs how companies collect data. It doesn't rate content or make any site safe.
