So 13 is a rule for companies, not a readiness test. The day before and the day after a thirteenth birthday, the same child is sitting at your table. The 13 shift changes what companies may do; your family's rules sit on top of it, and you decide how fast they change.

In this book, at 11–12 the parent creates and holds every login, and the student never signs up for anything alone. At 13–14, the student may hold passwords for family-approved tools, the parent keeps a sealed copy or a shared password-manager entry, and two-step login is on. First accounts happen together, under the service's terms, with you at the table.

### Social media, phones, and age rules

You'll probably be asked this year, "When can I get Instagram?" Here is what the evidence says.

**Platform minimums.** Most social media platforms in the United States set 13 as their minimum age.^[71](#note-71)^ So at 11–12, the answer is "not yet; the platform's own rules say 13."

**Phones.** The American Academy of Pediatrics' 2026 policy says research does not point to a specific age for teen smartphone readiness, and that it varies by teen. It suggests weighing digital media literacy, truthfulness with caregivers, the ability to handle conflict with friends, and practical needs. It asks caregivers to set and monitor privacy, download, content, purchase, and time settings, and notes that a flip phone or watch can limit access to apps a teen isn't ready for.^[6](#note-6)^ The AAP's PhoneReady questions turn this into a conversation: Is your child responsible about things like homework? Do they say sorry when they've hurt a friend's feelings? How well do they follow the tech rules you already have? Are *you* ready to help?^[72](#note-72)^

**The advisory.** In 2023, the U.S. Surgeon General issued an advisory on social media and youth mental health. Its central line: "we cannot conclude social media is sufficiently safe for children and adolescents."^[71](#note-71)^ That was a statement about the evidence at the time, not a law or a ban.

**What teens report.** In a 2025 national survey of teens 13 to 17, roughly three-quarters said they use YouTube every day, and 44 percent of 13- and 14-year-olds said they use Instagram, compared with three-quarters of 15- to 17-year-olds.^[73](#note-73)^

**What this book does.** This book doesn't set a phone age or a social media age of its own. The evidence doesn't give one. It hands you the platform minimums, the AAP's readiness questions, the advisory, and a family device agreement your student helps write (SAFE-08). That agreement is where your family's answer lives, and it can change as your student grows.

### Your footprint, and kindness

A **digital footprint** is everything that can be found about a person online, including things other people posted. Some of it is permanent in practice, because screenshots outlast deletions. At 13–14, a Footprint Audit with a parent (SAFE-05) shows a student their own trail and usually starts a good conversation.

Kindness online is a skill. A message typed in a hurry lands on a real person who can't see your face or hear your tone. The habits are small: reread before sending; don't forward something about someone that they wouldn't want forwarded; and if a chat turns cruel, leave and tell someone. Your student doesn't have to fix the whole chat. They do have to not join in.

### Stop, Save, Tell

Your family already has this routine from Family safety basics in **The Computing Hour**. At 11–14 it grows up a little.

**Stop.** Stop replying. Don't argue, pay, or send anything. **Save.** Screenshot it, note the username and the date, and keep it, even if it's embarrassing. **Tell.** Tell a parent, or another trusted adult if a parent isn't reachable. Fast is good.

Then say, out loud, more than once: **"You are not in trouble for telling. You will not lose your device for telling."** A student who fears losing the phone will hide the problem.

> **A calm word for parents: if someone asks for pictures, or threatens your child**
>
> One online danger is worth naming plainly for this age, because knowing about it ahead of time is most of the protection. It's sometimes called **sextortion**: someone online, often pretending to be a teen, builds a quick friendship, pressures a young person into sending a private picture, and then threatens to share it unless the young person pays or sends more. The FBI says the money-driven version mainly targets teenage boys and tends to start where young people already spend time: social apps and gaming sites.^[74](#note-74)^
>
> **Before anything happens,** say this once, calmly, as part of your device talk: "If anyone ever asks you for pictures, or threatens you: Stop, Save, Tell. You are not in trouble. Don't pay. Come to us."
>
> **If it happens:**
> - Stay calm. Your child is the victim of a crime, not the cause of it. Say so.
> - Don't pay and don't send anything more. The FBI warns that going along with the demands rarely stops them.^[74](#note-74)^
> - Keep everything: messages, usernames, profiles.
> - Block the account and report it through the app's safety tools.
> - Report it to the FBI at tips.fbi.gov or 1-800-CALL-FBI, or to your local police.^[74](#note-74)^
> - If an image has been shared or might be, the free **Take It Down** service from the National Center for Missing & Exploited Children can help remove images taken before 18 from participating platforms, without anyone sending or viewing the image.^[75](#note-75)^
>
> That's the whole box. No more detail is needed at the table.

### Wrong answers you should be able to hear

**"Private mode means nobody can see."**
*What it usually means:* your student thinks a browser setting hides them from everyone.
*What you say:* "Private from whom? Let's list who could still see it: the website, the network, anyone looking over your shoulder." Private browsing mostly stops your own device keeping a history.

**"The lock icon means the site is safe."**
*What it usually means:* HTTPS confusion, a favorite from Chapter 6.
*What you say:* "It means the connection is private. Is the site honest? How would we check?" A scam site can have a lock too. NET-05 Look for the Lock, from Chapter 6, makes a good ten-minute revisit: your student finishes the sentence "The lock means… It doesn't mean…"

**"My password is strong. It has an exclamation point."**
*What it usually means:* the old advice about symbols stuck.
*What you say:* "Length beats tricks. How many characters is it? Could you make a longer phrase you'd still remember?"

**"I'd never fall for that."**
*What it usually means:* your student has only seen clumsy fakes.
*What you say:* "Good. Then let's see if you can catch this one." Run SAFE-03 with a well-made fake.

### Five-minute parent warm-up

1. Write three short sample messages: one obvious phish, one well-made phish, one ordinary message. Invent the company names. That's tomorrow's SAFE-03.
2. Decide where the family password list lives and who holds which passwords. A password manager or a sealed envelope in a drawer you control both work.
3. Open the privacy settings on one device you own, just to know where they are.
4. Say the talk-box opening aloud once: "Who can see this, who could fake it, and who do you tell?" Then count to five in your head.

## How to teach it this week

The Computing Hour shape holds: Unplug and Plan, Predict, Build and Test, Debug and Log, Save and Explain. This week most of the work is on paper. The screen comes in briefly, with you beside it.

### A worked Hour (illustration)

Here is an illustration, not a real family.

**Unplug and Plan (5–10 min).** Lay three printed messages face down. "Today you're the security team. Before you look, write three warning signs a scam message might have." Your student writes the list in the Debug Log.

**Predict (2–5 min).** Turn the messages over. "For each one: phish or fine? Write your prediction before we discuss."

**Build and Test (15–20 min).** Your student checks each message against the list, adds new signs, and sorts. Then, on your device, hover over the link in your parent-made sample to see where it really goes (SAFE-04).

**Debug and Log (5–10 min).** A wrong prediction is the best part. "What did you expect, what was it actually, and what fooled you?" One Expected/Actual line goes in the Debug Log.

**Save and Explain (3–5 min).** File the sorted pile and the list. Your student says, "I predicted… It did… Next I'd change…" and "Help I used: …" (usually "none" this week).

### Exact wording you can say

- To open: "You're the security team today. Your job is to catch the fakes."
- When they're unsure: "What is this message asking you to do? And how fast?"
- To fade help: "What would you check before tapping anything?"
- To close: "Which warning sign was the strongest clue today?"

### When to stop talking

When your student is reading a message and deciding, say nothing. Look at the paper, not at them. Noticing the hurry and the ask on their own is the skill. If they ask you, ask back: "What's it asking you to do?"

### In-home — SAFE-01 Passphrase Builder

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** paper, a pencil, and any book.
- **Safety and privacy:** practice phrases only; never write down a real passphrase.
- **The fun:** ridiculous, memorable word chains ("violin-cactus-harbor-pancake").
- **The skill:** length beats tricks; one passphrase per account; password managers in parent hands.
- **The crumb:** a rules card in your student's words, such as "Four or more random words, at least 15 characters, never reused, never shared outside the family." The rule, never the phrase.

Open the book to a random page and pick a word; repeat four times; count the characters. Compare with "Fluffy2!" and ask which someone who knows the family dog's name could guess.

**11–12:** The parent holds every real password; the student builds practice phrases and explains why length matters.

**13–14:** The student may create one real passphrase for a family-approved tool, typed straight into the password manager with you present.

### In-home — SAFE-03 Phish or Fine?

- **Time:** 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** six to ten sample messages you've made up: a fake prize, a fake account warning, a "friend" asking for a login code, an ordinary reminder, a delivery notice, a normal note from a relative.
- **Safety and privacy:** paper only; no real brands, people, or links.
- **The fun:** catching a well-made fake before a parent does.
- **The skill:** spotting hurry, threats, prizes, odd senders, vague greetings, and requests for codes or money.
- **The crumb:** the sorted pile and the reasons.

**11–12:** Six messages, three obvious; sort into "phish," "fine," and "ask a parent."

**13–14:** Ten messages, some very good. Your student then writes a fake of their own, hiding three warning signs, for you to catch. You may be worse at this than you expect, which is good for everyone.

### In-home — SAFE-08 Family Device Agreement

- **Time:** 20 minutes to draft; a second sitting to sign.
- **Materials:** paper, and everyone who uses devices in the house.
- **Safety and privacy:** first names only if it hangs where visitors can see it.
- **The fun:** your student drafts it, and the adults sign it too.
- **The skill:** shared written rules that are about safety and fairness, not punishment.
- **The crumb:** the signed agreement, dated, with a review date.

**What usually goes on it** (in your family's words): where devices live (a shared room; phones out of bedrooms overnight; screens off an hour before bed); who holds which passwords; which apps and accounts need a parent's yes; two-step login on where offered; Stop, Save, Tell; first names only, with no faces, school, or town in anything shared; the AI rules from the AI box in **The Computing Hour**; and what happens if a rule is broken. Add one line everyone signs: "Telling a parent about a problem online never costs you your device."

**11–12:** The parent writes the main rules; the student proposes two and words the Stop, Save, Tell line.

**13–14:** The student writes the first draft and the parent edits. A teen who wrote the rule is likelier to keep it. Review it each semester.

### In-home — SAFE-09 Stop, Save, Tell

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** a few paper "messages" you write: mean ("nobody likes you"), pushy ("send me your login code"), nosy ("where do you live?"), threatening ("pay me or I'll post this"). Keep them mild and invented.
- **Safety and privacy:** pretend messages only.
- **The fun:** swapping roles until it's boring.
- **The skill:** a rehearsed response that doesn't depend on judging how bad a message is.
- **The crumb:** a script card in your student's words.

Hand over a paper message. Your student acts the three steps: hands off, pretend screenshot, comes to you. You say, "You are not in trouble." Then swap. Ordinary is the goal.

**13–14:** Add "Who else could you tell if I'm not here?" and write two names on the card.

### Band moves

**11–12:** No social media accounts; the platforms' own minimum is 13. The parent holds every password and approves every app. The work is paper-first: sorting messages, practice passphrases, rehearsing Stop, Save, Tell, and helping draft the agreement. Settings tours happen on the parent's device, with the student reading the options aloud.

**13–14:** The student may hold passwords for family-approved tools, with the parent's sealed copy. Two-step login goes on together (SAFE-02), a Footprint Audit happens with a parent (SAFE-05), and settings get reviewed each semester. Any first account happens under the service's terms, with you at the table.

**Ready 12:** A twelve-year-old who handles SAFE-03 easily can write fake messages for you to catch. Accounts still wait for 13.

## Practice that actually builds learning

**Blocked practice:** three days of Phish or Fine? with new samples, until your student names the warning signs quickly. **Mixed practice** once it's known: shuffle phish samples, settings questions, and Stop, Save, Tell messages into one pile, so each card asks "which habit is this?" before "what do I do?" Once a week, at the start of any Hour, ask for the four habits from memory. It takes thirty seconds.

### Talk box (this chapter)

**Opening (exact):** "Who can see this, who could fake it, and who do you tell?"

**Follow-ups (pick three to five):**

1. *Prediction:* "If you post this, who do you think will see it in a week? In a year?"
2. *Evidence:* "What in this message makes you think it's real, or fake?"
3. *Location:* "Point to the part that's asking you to do something."
4. *Change:* "What would you change before sharing it: crop it, remove the name, ask first?"
5. *Revision:* "You can change your answer. Let's look again."

**How to wait.** After you ask, count to five in your head. That pause is a habit drawn from classroom wait-time studies.^[5](#note-5)^ When the question is about code on the screen, let them read as long as they need, and the same goes for a message or a settings screen. Look at the screen, not at your student.

**What a stuck silence usually means.** The question was too big (ask one part: "Who could see this?"). There was no wait (ask again and count). Your student is worried about something already done (say, "Nobody's in trouble. We're just looking."). Or they're waiting for you to answer, because you have before. Wait longer.

**Don't say / say instead.** Don't say, "How could you fall for that?" Say, "That one was well made. Which sign would catch it next time?"

### In-home — SAFE-02 Two-Step Login Walk-Through

*For 13–14.*

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** one family-approved account, the device that will receive codes, and you beside them.
- **Safety and privacy:** no photos or notes of any codes; backup codes go into the password manager or the sealed envelope.
- **The fun:** watching a stolen password become useless.
- **The skill:** turning on two-step login, and knowing what to do if an unexpected code arrives (tell a parent and change the passphrase).
- **The crumb:** a dated note that two-step login is on for that account. No codes.

### In-home — SAFE-04 Hover Before You Click

- **Time:** 10 minutes.
- **Materials:** a sample email or document you made on your own device, with a link whose words say one thing and whose address says another (point it somewhere harmless, like a library page).
- **Safety and privacy:** you make the sample and hold the device.
- **The fun:** catching the mismatch.
- **The skill:** checking where a link really goes before tapping, and finding the real site name in an address.
- **The crumb:** a cropped screenshot of the sample link.

### In-home — SAFE-05 Footprint Audit

*For 13–14.*

- **Time:** 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** the parent's device, a search engine, and a private notebook.
- **Safety and privacy:** the parent drives. If something upsetting turns up, stop and talk privately. The list stays private; it isn't a portfolio piece.
- **The fun:** detective work on yourself.
- **The skill:** seeing what's public and deciding what to tighten. Ask the talk-box opening for each result.
- **The crumb:** a private list of what was found and one change made.

### In-home — SAFE-06 Privacy Settings Tour

- **Time:** 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** one device or app the student uses.
- **Safety and privacy:** the parent holds the device at 11–12; at 13–14 the student drives with the parent beside them.
- **The fun:** finding settings neither of you knew existed.
- **The skill:** who can see me, who can contact me, and what's shared (location, contacts, microphone, camera).
- **The crumb:** a dated checklist. Repeat each semester and on every new app.

### In-home — SAFE-07 Age Rules Map

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** a three-column paper table (service, minimum age, date checked) and the parent's device.
- **Safety and privacy:** the parent looks up the terms; nobody signs up for anything.
- **The fun:** spotting the pattern.
- **The skill:** noticing that 13 shows up again and again, and explaining why (the 13 shift).
- **The crumb:** the table with dates. Terms change, so check the current terms before relying on any row. Chapter 8 adds AI services to this map.

### Out-of-home — OUT-05 Scam Spotting Out and About

- **Time:** 15 minutes, folded into an errand.
- **Materials:** a notebook; a store, parking lot, post office, or library.
- **Safety and privacy:** stay together; follow road rules in parking lots; no photos of strangers or staff; don't scan any QR code you find.
- **The fun:** finding warnings that are already everywhere.
- **The skill:** noticing gift-card scam warnings near the checkout, QR stickers on parking meters (sometimes stuck over the real one), "urgent" signs, and posted rules about who to call.
- **The crumb:** three examples, each with its warning sign. Ask the talk-box opening about each one.

### One incorrect example to diagnose (illustration)

Here is an illustration, not a real family. A thirteen-year-old films a short how-to video about his model rockets. He's in the backyard, he says "Hi, I'm Sam from Riverside Middle," and the house number shows behind him. He asks to post it.

**What went wrong:** together, his name, school, and house tell a stranger where to find him.

**What to do, calmly:** praise the rocket video first; it's a real project. Then run the talk-box opening together and let him find the school name and house number himself. Reshoot with no school, no house, and a first name only, or keep it in the family. He made the safety call himself.

## For the student

Here's the honest truth: most online problems don't come from movie-style hackers. They come from ordinary tricks that work on ordinary people, adults included. A reused password. A message that says "URGENT." A photo that shows more than you meant. A few habits stop most of it, and you can learn them this week.

**The four habits.**

1. **Spot the phish.** A scam message almost always wants two things: for you to hurry, and for you to give something (a password, a code, money, your address). Hurry plus a request is your signal to slow down and show a parent.
2. **Long passphrases, never reused.** Four or five random words beat a short password full of symbols. "harbor-violin-cactus-pancake" is stronger than "Fluffy2!" and easier to remember. Every account gets its own.
3. **Two-step login.** A second step, usually a code, means a stolen password isn't enough. A login code is only ever typed into a page *you* opened. If someone asks you to read a code out to them, that's the person the code is protecting you from.
4. **Updates on.** Updates patch holes criminals already know about.

**A worked example.** Here's a message:

> "Hey! It's Jordan from the team. I got locked out, can you send me the 6-digit code that just came to your phone? Need it fast!!"

Check it against the habits. It hurries you. It asks for a login code. It *says* it's a friend, but you can't see who actually sent it. That code came to you because someone typed *your* username; whoever wrote this is trying to get into your account. The right move: don't reply, take a screenshot, show a parent. If it really was Jordan, a phone call sorts it out in thirty seconds.

**Your password is yours, and your parent's.** Not your best friend's. At 11 or 12, your parent holds your passwords. That's not about trust; it's how accounts work for under-13s.

**If you're 13 or older:** you may hold passwords for tools your family has approved, with your parent keeping a sealed copy. Turn on two-step login together, and check your privacy settings each semester. Thirteen is when companies' rules change; your family's rules change at the speed your family decides.

**If you clicked something you shouldn't have,** tell a parent right away. You're not in trouble. A password changed in the first ten minutes saves a lot of trouble later.

**Stop, Save, Tell.** If anything online is mean, scary, pushy, or threatening: stop replying, save it (screenshot, don't delete), and tell a parent. If anyone ever asks you for pictures or threatens you, the same three words apply, plus one more: **don't pay.** You are not in trouble. You will not lose your device for telling. Your parents would much rather hear about it than not.

**Kindness when nobody's watching.** Before you send something, read it in a flat voice, as if you were the person getting it. In a group chat you don't have to fix everything, but you do have to not join in when it turns cruel. Leaving the chat and telling someone is a strong move.

**This part is hard for everyone.** Good fakes fool grown-ups too. Getting fooled once doesn't mean you're careless; telling someone fast is what keeps you safe.

**Try it today.** Ask your parent for three sample messages. For each, write "phish" or "fine" and one reason *before* you discuss them. Then check.

**Explain it back.** Tell someone in your family why a long passphrase beats a short password with symbols. Use the word "guess."

**Challenge.** Write a fake scam message on paper, from a made-up company, with three warning signs hidden in it. See if your parent finds all three.

**If you're 13 or older,** a coding challenge. This function checks whether a practice phrase is long enough. Predict the three lines it prints before you run it.

```python
def long_enough(phrase):
    return len(phrase) >= 15

tests = ["sunflower", "purple-otter-jumps-tuesday", ""]
for t in tests:
    print(t, long_enough(t))
```

It prints `sunflower False`, then `purple-otter-jumps-tuesday True`, then a line that starts with a blank (the empty phrase) followed by `False`. Add a test of your own, using a made-up phrase, never a real one.

**What to save.** Your passphrase rules card (never a real passphrase), your sorted Phish or Fine? pile with reasons, your Stop, Save, Tell card, and your copy of the signed agreement.

## If it isn't clicking

### "That would never happen to me."

**What it usually means:** your student has only seen clumsy fakes.

**Next move:** make a very good fake for SAFE-03, built around an invented club or hobby they care about. Let them get it wrong once at the table, where it's free, then talk about what fooled them.

### Eye-rolling, or "You don't trust me."

**What it usually means:** the rules feel like a verdict on their character.

**Next move:** hand over the pen. Let your student draft the agreement and explain each rule's reason, and make clear the adults sign too. Say plainly that the 13 shift and the password rules are about how accounts work, not about trust. If "all my friends have it" comes up, keep it short and kind: "Other families decide for themselves. In our house, the agreement is the rule, and we'll look at it again next semester." Then do.

### Fear: "I don't want to go online at all now."

**What it usually means:** something landed too heavily.

**Next move:** come back to the four habits and to Stop, Save, Tell. "You have a plan, and I'm right here." Spend a few days on the fun parts, like writing fakes for you to catch. If a fear seems tied to something real they saw, talk privately, outside the lesson.

### When to slow down, go ahead, or step back

**Slow down** if Stop, Save, Tell isn't automatic or the four habits don't come from memory. **Go ahead** when your student sorts a mixed stack confidently and explains why. **Step back to paper** whenever a screen activity turns into scrolling.

The rescue order holds: ask → wait → hint → short demo on your own copy → hand it back. Here, "your own copy" means your own sample message or your own settings screen.

## Tools, including AI

- **A password manager** in the parent's hands at 11–12 and shared at 13–14, or a sealed envelope in a drawer you control. What matters is one strong, unique passphrase per account.
- **Parental controls** on the router, phones, tablets, game consoles, and accounts. The AAP lists time, downloads, contacts, and purchases as settings caregivers can manage.^[6](#note-6)^
- **Two-step login** on every account that offers it, starting at 13–14 with SAFE-02.

**AI in this chapter.** An AI-written list of safety rules doesn't replace your family agreement; your student's own words do the work. Private messages, chat screenshots, photos, and names stay out of chatbots, even when you're asking for advice about a problem. For everything else, see the AI box in **The Computing Hour**, and check the current terms of any service before you use it.

## What "done enough" looks like

Move on when most of these are true. Skill, not birthday.

- [ ] Your student names the **four habits** from memory, with a reason for each.
- [ ] They spot **three phishing signs** in a new sample without prompting.
- [ ] They state a **strong passphrase rule** in their own words, without revealing a real passphrase.
- [ ] They say and act out **Stop, Save, Tell**, including "don't pay," and can name who they'd tell.
- [ ] They explain **the 13 shift** in a sentence: a rule for companies, not a readiness test.
- [ ] The **family device agreement** is drafted with their help, signed by everyone, and dated with a review date.
- [ ] **13–14:** two-step login is on for at least one account, and they've done a Footprint Audit with a parent.
- [ ] They answer the talk-box opening about one real photo or message.

**A short check for the file** (Chapter 9 gathers these): **AS-03 Explain Your Code**, safety version. Your student explains why each rule in the agreement is there; at 13–14, they also explain the `long_enough` function line by line. Write down one sentence they say. Add any Expected/Actual line from a wrong Phish or Fine? prediction to the Debug Log: "Expected: fine. Actual: phish. Clue I missed: the code request."

**Keeping it alive.** Re-run SAFE-03 with fresh samples once a semester, and review the agreement whenever a new device arrives.

Nobody can make the internet perfectly safe. You've given your student four habits that stop most trouble, a routine for the rest, and a clear promise that telling is always safe. That's a great deal.

\newpage

# Chapter 8

## AI Up Close: Training Data, Bias, Prediction, and Whose Work It Is

![Shape cards sorted into two trays, blue circles in one and other shapes in the other, with one new card waiting to be sorted — still life, no people, no logos, no readable text.](art/ch08-opener.jpg){width=100%}

## Why this matters

Here is an illustration, not a real family. A thirteen-year-old asks a chatbot, with his mother beside him, for three books about the history of rockets. It answers at once: three titles, three authors, three tidy summaries. His mother suggests they look the books up at the library website. Two are real. The third doesn't exist. The title sounds right, the author's name sounds right, and the summary is lovely. It's just made up.

He's annoyed, then fascinated. "Why would it do that?"

That question is the door into this chapter. By the end, your student will be able to answer it in plain words: a chatbot is built to predict what words are likely to come next, and a likely-sounding answer isn't always a true one. They'll also know where an AI's "knowledge" comes from, why it can be lopsided, and why anything it produces is made by a tool, not by them.

Your student is growing up with AI in the search box, the photo app, the store, and the bank. Most teens already use chatbots: in a 2025 national survey, 64 percent of U.S. teens 13 to 17 said they had used one.^[73](#note-73)^ Whether your family uses AI a lot or hardly at all, understanding it is now part of computing, the way understanding how a network works is.

CSTA, the Computer Science Teachers Association, puts it in grown-up terms. Middle-schoolers should be able to guess how a machine learning model makes its predictions, investigate how better examples in the training data improve accuracy and reduce bias, and evaluate "when it is appropriate to use AI and other emerging technologies."^[17](#note-17)^ Its guidance for schools adds a sentence worth keeping: using an AI tool "only becomes CS learning when the lesson addresses how the tool works."^[15](#note-15)^ That's this chapter. Almost all of it happens on paper, with no chatbot at all.

**This week you can learn** how AI learns from examples and why that can go wrong. **Today your student can try** guessing a hidden rule from a stack of example cards, exactly the way a sorting model does.

## For the parent: understand it yourself

You don't need to know any math to teach this chapter. You need four ideas: AI learns from examples; the examples can be lopsided; chatbots predict likely words; and whose work it is depends on who made it and who can explain it.

### The everyday picture

Picture teaching a new helper at a farm stand to sort apples from pears without ever telling them a rule. You just hand over fruit, one at a time, saying "apple" or "pear." After a few dozen, they start getting it right. They've learned a pattern from examples. If you only ever showed them red apples, though, the first green apple might get called a pear. They learned exactly what you showed them, gaps and all.

That's most of what "machine learning" means.

### The precise picture

**Artificial intelligence (AI)**, as most people use the word today, usually means programs that learn patterns from examples instead of following rules a person typed one by one. The examples are the **training data**. What the program builds from them is a **model**: a big set of learned numbers that turn an input into a guess.

A **classifier** is a model that sorts things into groups: spam or not spam, cat or dog, fish or trash. You train it on labeled examples, then **test** it on examples it hasn't seen. The share it gets right is its **accuracy**. The test examples have to be new; testing on the training examples is like grading a quiz with the answer key the student studied.

**Bias**, in this chapter, means a model that is wrong more often for some groups or situations than others. It often comes from **lopsided training data**: too few examples of some things, or examples labeled by people with their own blind spots. CSTA asks middle-schoolers to analyze how choices made at each stage of working with data "can lead to biased data, misleading conclusions, and compromised AI models."^[17](#note-17)^ The key sentence for your student is short: **people choose the data.** A model isn't neutral because a computer runs it.

### Next-word prediction: how chatbots talk

A **chatbot** is built on a language model trained on an enormous amount of text. Underneath, its main job is to predict a likely next word (really, a likely next piece of a word), then the next, then the next. Done at huge scale, with careful training afterward, this produces answers that are fluent, organized, and often useful.

But notice what the job is. It's *likely*, not *true*. A chatbot that has seen thousands of book titles and author names can produce a new title and author that look exactly like real ones. That's how the rocket book appeared. **Fluent isn't the same as true.** A chatbot can make up facts, quotes, and sources, and it says them in the same confident voice as everything else. So the family rule is simple: check one claim against a real source before trusting it, and open any source it cites yourself.

### Chips and data centers

Training and running these models takes a great deal of computing. It happens on chips that do huge numbers of small sums at the same time, in big data centers that use a lot of electricity. The International Energy Agency estimates that data centers used about 1.5 percent of the world's electricity in 2024, and that a single AI-focused data center can draw as much electricity as a power-hungry factory.^[59](#note-59)^ Your student doesn't need the figures. They need the idea that every AI answer runs on real machines somewhere, using real energy, and that this is one of the things to weigh when deciding whether AI is the right tool.

### When AI is a good tool, and when it isn't

CSTA's standard asks students to weigh AI by its capabilities, its limits, and its environmental impact.^[17](#note-17)^ In family terms, AI tends to help with explaining an idea a second way, giving practice questions, or suggesting where a bug might be *after* your student has looked. It tends to hurt when it does the thinking the lesson exists to build, when the answer has to be true and nobody checks, or when the question involves private information.

The College Board's guidance for AP students warns that AI tools can produce "incomplete code, code that creates or introduces biases, code with errors, inefficiencies," or code too complicated to understand and explain.^[76](#note-76)^ That's good advice for a thirteen-year-old too.

### Whose work is it?

Here is the rule this book uses, adapted from the AP Computer Science Principles course. The College Board allows AI as a "supplementary resource" for understanding, building, and debugging code, but "it is the student's responsibility to review and understand any code co-written with AI tools," students must be "prepared to explain their code in detail," and they should acknowledge "any code created by generative AI tools."^[23](#note-23)^ In short: **understand it, explain it, acknowledge it.**

For this book, that becomes three habits:

1. Anything an AI or generator produces is made by a tool. It is never your student's own work and never a portfolio piece on its own.
2. Every project carries a Whose Work Is It? card (AI-06): who or what helped, what was asked, what came back, what was kept, what was changed, and how it was tested.
3. Every Save and Explain ends with "Help I used: …", even when the answer is "none."

The same goes for code borrowed from a book, a friend, or a website. Remixing is a fine way to learn, with credit. CSTA asks students to "justify the importance of attribution and intellectual property."^[17](#note-17)^ Credit isn't a confession. It's what honest builders do.

One more thing: AI "detectors" aren't used to judge a student in this book. You have something better. "Explain it to me" is the check.

### The age split, in one line

Most consumer AI services set 13 as their minimum age, and at least one major one sets 18.^[9](#note-9),[10](#note-10)^ So at 11–12 any AI use is parent-operated, on your account, with you reading every exchange; at 13–14 it's supervised use under the service's terms, with you nearby. Google offers a parent-enabled, supervised way for children under 13 to use its Gemini apps; this book names it so you know it exists. It isn't a recommendation, and this book still keeps the parent-operated rule at 11–12. CodeAI (formerly Code.org) labels its AI Tutor for students 13 and up, so it stays off at 11–12.^[11](#note-11),[55](#note-55),[12](#note-12)^ Check the current terms before you start. For the full rules, see the AI box in **The Computing Hour**, including its caution about AI helpers that share one computer.

### Wrong answers you should be able to hear

**"It's basically a person. It knows things."**
*What it usually means:* fluent language feels like a mind.
*What you say:* "It predicts likely words. Let's build a tiny one out of paper and see." Then run AI-04.

**"If it sounds sure, it's right."**
*What it usually means:* confidence is being read as evidence.
*What you say:* "Fluent isn't true. Pick one claim. How would we check it?" (AI-05)

**"Computers can't be biased. It's just math."**
*What it usually means:* your student hasn't seen where the data comes from.
*What you say:* "Who chose the examples? Who's missing from them?" (AI-03, DATA-06)

**"More data always fixes it."**
*What it usually means:* half right.
*What you say:* "More of *what*? If we add a hundred more red apples, does the green apple get easier?"

**"The AI wrote it, but I get it."**
*What it usually means:* reading code feels like understanding it.
*What you say:* "Great. Explain line 6 without looking at the chat." (AI-07)

### Five-minute parent warm-up

1. Make twelve index cards for AI-02. Draw or write simple objects that follow a hidden rule you choose, such as "things with wheels" or "things bigger than a shoebox." Write the rule on a separate card and hide it.
2. Ask yourself one question you'll ask your student: "What did it learn from?"
3. Find the Whose Work Is It? card template from the AI box in **The Computing Hour**, or write the six lines on an index card.
4. Say the talk-box opening aloud once: "What did it learn from, where might it be wrong — and whose work is this?" Then count to five in your head.

## How to teach it this week

The Computing Hour shape holds: Unplug and Plan, Predict, Build and Test, Debug and Log, Save and Explain. Almost everything this week is unplugged.

### A worked Hour (illustration)

Here is an illustration, not a real family.

**Unplug and Plan (5–10 min).** "Today you're the AI. I'll show you example cards marked yes or no. Your job is to figure out the rule." Your student writes in the Debug Log: "Rule I'll be looking for: ___."

**Predict (2–5 min).** After eight training cards, before seeing the test cards: "Write your best guess at the rule."

**Build and Test (15–20 min).** Show four new test cards, one at a time. Your student says yes or no for each, then you reveal the answers. Count the hits: that's the accuracy.

**Debug and Log (5–10 min).** If a test card fooled them, ask, "What about the training cards led you there?" One Expected/Actual line in the Debug Log.

**Save and Explain (3–5 min).** "I predicted… It did… Next I'd change…" and "Help I used: …". Keep the rule guessed beside the real rule.

### Exact wording you can say

- To open: "You're the model today. Learn from the examples."
- When they're stuck: "What do all the yes cards have in common?"
- To connect it: "That's what a sorting model does, just with thousands of examples."
- To close: "What would have made the training cards better?"

### When to stop talking

When your student is staring at the cards, hunting for the rule, say nothing. That hunt is the lesson. If they ask for a hint, point at two cards and ask, "What's the same about these?"

### In-home — AI-02 Human Classifier

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** twelve to sixteen index cards you've made, with a hidden rule; a pencil.
- **Safety and privacy:** objects only. No people's faces or names on the cards.
- **The fun:** being the AI, and catching the parent's sneaky rule.
- **The skill:** learning a rule from labeled examples, then testing on new ones.
- **The crumb:** the rule guessed beside the real rule, and the accuracy (for example, "3 of 4").

**11–12:** An easy rule with clear examples. Then let your student make a deck for you.

**13–14:** A trickier rule (two features at once). Ask, "Could two different rules both fit the training cards?"

### In-home — AI-03 Lopsided Training Set

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** the AI-02 cards, plus new ones; a tally sheet.
- **Safety and privacy:** objects only.
- **The fun:** fooling the "model" on purpose.
- **The skill:** how training data that leaves things out produces wrong guesses.
- **The crumb:** an error tally: which test cards went wrong, and why.

Train your student on a lopsided deck: every "fruit" card is red, say. Then test with a green apple, a yellow banana, and a red ball. Tally the errors. Then ask the key question: "How would you fix the training deck?"

**11–12:** Keep it to fruit, vehicles, or animals.

**13–14:** Connect it to real systems in one sentence: "If a model learns from photos that mostly show one kind of thing, it does worse on what it rarely saw."

### In-home — AI-04 Next-Word Guesser

- **Time:** 25 minutes.
- **Materials:** paper, a pencil, a short sentence or two (a nursery rhyme works), and at 13–14, a computer with Python.
- **Safety and privacy:** use a public rhyme or your own made-up sentences; no private messages or diaries.
- **The fun:** making nonsense that sounds almost right.
- **The skill:** how a text predictor chooses a likely next word from what it has seen.
- **The crumb:** the word-pair table and one generated sentence labeled "made by our table."

Write the text out. Make a table: for each word, list every word that came right after it. "the" might be followed by "cat," "mat," "cat," and "door." Start with "the," pick one of its followers at random (roll a die or draw slips), then look up *that* word's followers, and keep going. Read the sentence aloud. It will sound like the text, and it may say something the text never said. That's the point: fluent, not necessarily true.

**11–12:** Paper only. Ask, "Did our table *know* anything about cats?"

**13–14:** Build the same table in Python. Predict what kinds of sentences it can make before running it.

```python
import random
text = "the cat sat on the mat and the cat ran to the door"
words = text.split()
word = "the"
sentence = [word]
for step in range(5):
    options = []
    for i in range(len(words) - 1):
        if words[i] == word:
            options.append(words[i + 1])
    if options == []:
        break
    word = random.choice(options)
    sentence.append(word)
print(" ".join(sentence))
```

The output changes each run. You might see `the cat sat on the mat`, `the cat ran to the cat`, or `the mat and the door`, which stops early because "door" has nothing after it. "The cat ran to the cat" is a fluent sentence the original text never said. Talk about that one.

### In-home — AI-06 Whose Work Is It? Card

- **Time:** 10 minutes.
- **Materials:** an index card and the project your student just finished.
- **Safety and privacy:** first names only on the card.
- **The fun:** a project's "credits," like the end of a film.
- **The skill:** saying plainly what a tool did and what you did.
- **The crumb:** the card, filed with the project.

The six lines: who or what helped; what was asked; what came back; what was kept; what was changed; how it was tested. A card that says "no help used" is a good card. Fill one out this week for any project from Chapters 3 to 6, even if nobody used AI. The habit matters more than the content.

### Band moves

**11–12:** All AI is parent-operated: your account, your device, you reading every exchange. The chapter is mostly unplugged (AI-02, AI-03, AI-04 on paper, AI-06). A trained model (AI-01) and checking a chatbot (AI-05) happen on your screen, with your student directing and you typing. No student accounts on chatbots or AI tutors.

**13–14:** Supervised use under the service's terms, with you nearby and the screen visible. Add the Python next-word guesser and AI-07 Explain Every Line. Your student fills out the Whose Work card without being reminded.

**Ready 12:** A twelve-year-old who explains the next-word table clearly can do the Python version with you at the keyboard. Accounts still follow the terms.

## Practice that actually builds learning

**Blocked practice:** two or three rounds of Human Classifier with new decks until your student talks about "training cards" and "test cards" without prompting. **Mixed practice:** a shuffled set of questions where each needs a different idea: "Why did it get the green apple wrong?" (lopsided data), "Is this book real?" (fluent isn't true), "Who wrote this function?" (whose work). Once a week, ask the talk-box opening about any AI your student met, at home or out.

### Talk box (this chapter)

**Opening (exact):** "What did it learn from, where might it be wrong — and whose work is this?"

**Follow-ups (pick three to five):**

1. *Prediction:* "Before we ask it, what do you think it'll say? What might it get wrong?"
2. *Evidence:* "How could we check that claim? Where's a source we trust?"
3. *Location:* "Point to the line you didn't write. Which part came from the tool?"
4. *Change:* "If we gave it different examples, what would change?"
5. *Revision:* "Want to change your answer about whose work this is?"

**How to wait.** After you ask, count to five in your head.^[5](#note-5)^ When the question is about code on the screen, let them read as long as they need. Look at the screen, not at your student.

**What a stuck silence usually means.** The question was too big (ask one part: "What did it learn from?"). There was no wait (ask again and count). Your student is worried that using a tool was wrong (say, "Using help is fine. We're just writing down what helped."). Or they honestly can't explain a line, which is useful information, not a failure. Say, "Then it isn't yours yet. Let's make it yours."

**Don't say / say instead.** Don't say, "Did you cheat?" Say, "Walk me through line 4. What does it do?"

### In-home — AI-01 Train a Sorting Model (Objects Only)

- **Time:** 25 minutes.
- **Materials:** your device and a free classroom tool for training a simple model. CodeAI's AI for Oceans, part of its Hour of AI and listed for grades 3 to 12, has students train a model to tell sea creatures from trash.^[77](#note-77)^
- **Safety and privacy:** objects only, never photos of people. At 11–12, the parent runs it on the parent's device, with no student account.
- **The fun:** training a model, then watching it make funny mistakes.
- **The skill:** training and testing a classifier, and seeing how the examples shape the result.
- **The crumb:** an accuracy note: what it got right, what it got wrong, and why you think so.

**11–12:** Your student decides each label; you click.

**13–14:** Ask your student to try to make the model worse on purpose with a lopsided set, then fix it.

### In-home — AI-05 Check the Chatbot

- **Time:** 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** the parent's device, a chatbot your family uses under its terms, and a library website or encyclopedia.
- **Safety and privacy:** no names, photos, or personal details typed in. At 11–12, the parent types and the student dictates.
- **The fun:** fact-checking a machine.
- **The skill:** checking three claims against sources; noticing confident errors.
- **The crumb:** a three-row claim/check table: claim, where we checked, true or not.

Ask the chatbot a question with checkable facts, such as dates or book titles on a topic your student likes. Pick three claims. Check each one.

### In-home — AI-07 Explain Every Line

*For 13–14; ready 12 with the parent at the keyboard.*

- **Time:** 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** a short piece of AI-suggested code (under 15 lines), on paper or screen; a pencil.
- **Safety and privacy:** the suggestion comes through the parent's or a supervised account under the terms.
- **The fun:** finding the line that's wrong before running it.
- **The skill:** explaining AI-suggested code line by line before using any of it.
- **The crumb:** the annotated code, with a note on each line and a "Help I used: …" line.

Your student writes, beside each line, what it does in plain words. Any line they can't explain gets a question mark, and nothing with a question mark goes into the project until it's understood or rewritten.

### In-home — AI-08 When Not to Use AI

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** a dozen scenario cards you write; three piles labeled "fine," "not fine," and "ask first."
- **Safety and privacy:** invented scenarios only.
- **The fun:** arguing about the tricky ones.
- **The skill:** weighing capabilities, limits, privacy, and energy before using AI.
- **The crumb:** the sorted cards, with a reason on each tricky one.

Sample cards: "Ask it to explain an error message after you've guessed." "Have it write your whole project." "Paste in a group chat to ask whether a friend is mad at you." "Use it during a Cold Trace." "Ask for five practice questions on loops." "Ask it the weather when a window is right there." There's no perfect sort. The reasons are the point.

### In-home — DATA-06 Who Is Missing?

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** a small data set from Chapter 4, or a made-up one, such as a survey of "favorite snacks" asked only of people at a basketball game.
- **Safety and privacy:** invented or family data with first names only.
- **The fun:** spotting the gap.
- **The skill:** biased samples: who the data leaves out.
- **The crumb:** a two-sentence note: who's missing, and how that changes the answer.

### In-home — SAFE-07 Age Rules Map

- **Time:** 15 minutes.
- **Materials:** the age-rules table from Chapter 7 and the parent's device.
- **Safety and privacy:** the parent looks up the terms; nobody signs up.
- **The fun:** finding the one that says 18.
- **The skill:** reading AI services' minimum ages and seeing why 13 keeps appearing (the 13 shift).
- **The crumb:** the table with new AI rows and dates checked. Check the current terms; they change.

### Out-of-home — OUT-06 Where Is AI Here?

- **Time:** 20 minutes, folded into an errand.
- **Materials:** a notebook; a store, bank, or library.
- **Safety and privacy:** stay together; no photos of staff or customers; ask questions politely and only if staff aren't busy.
- **The fun:** spotting the hidden machines.
- **The skill:** noticing automated decisions (self-checkout, recommended books, fraud alerts, chat help on a sign) and asking about them.
- **The crumb:** a list of what you spotted, with one question asked and the answer.

A good question for staff: "Does a computer decide that, or a person?" Afterward, ask the talk-box opening about one item.

### One incorrect example to diagnose (illustration)

Here is an illustration, not a real family. A thirteen-year-old needs the average of three quiz scores for a small project. She asks a chatbot, under supervision, and pastes in its answer:

```python
scores = [8, 9, 10]
total = 0
for s in scores:
    total = total + s
average = total / 2
print(average)
```

She expected `9.0`. It prints `13.5`. She had looked the code over and thought she understood it.

**What went wrong:** line 5 divides by 2 instead of by the number of scores. A careful line-by-line explanation (AI-07) would have caught it: "Line 5 divides the total by… wait, why 2?" The fix is `average = total / len(scores)`, which prints `9.0`. There's also no "Help I used: …" line and no Whose Work card.

**What to do, calmly:** praise her for testing it and noticing the wrong number; that's real debugging. Then ask her to explain each line aloud, and let her find line 5. She writes the Expected/Actual line in the Debug Log, fixes it herself, and fills out the card: "Help I used: a chatbot suggested the code; I found and fixed the divide-by-2 bug." That's honest, and now the fix is hers.

## For the student

AI is everywhere you look right now, so let's look at it closely. Not with fear, and not with hype. Just: how does it actually work?

**AI learns from examples.** Most of what people call AI today is a program that learns patterns from a huge pile of examples, called **training data**. Show a program thousands of photos labeled "cat" or "dog," and it builds a **model**, a big set of numbers, that can guess the label for a new photo. Nobody typed in a rule like "cats have pointy ears." The program found its own patterns. You'll do the same thing this week with index cards, playing the AI yourself.

**The examples decide what it learns.** If every apple in the training data is red, a green apple confuses it. That's not the computer being silly; it learned exactly what it was shown. When a model is wrong more often for some groups than others, that's called **bias**, and it usually starts with the data. And here's the important part: **people choose the data.** So "it's a computer, so it's fair" isn't true. A model is only as fair as its examples.

**Chatbots predict likely words.** A chatbot is trained on an enormous amount of text. Its main job is to predict a likely next word, then the next, then the next. Done at giant scale, that can produce answers that are clear and helpful. But "likely" isn't "true." A chatbot can invent a book, a quote, or a fact that sounds perfect and doesn't exist. So:

- If it sounds sure, check anyway.
- If it gives a source, open the source.
- If it matters, check one claim against something you trust.

**A worked example.** Here's a tiny "language model" you can make in two minutes. Take the sentence "the cat sat on the mat and the cat ran to the door." For each word, write down what came after it. After "the" came: cat, mat, cat, door. After "cat" came: sat, ran. Now start with "the," pick a follower at random, then pick a follower of *that* word, and keep going. You might get "the cat ran to the cat." That sentence sounds fine, and it's nonsense, and it was never in the original. Real chatbots are vastly bigger and much better trained, but the idea of predicting what's likely is the same.

**AI runs on real machines.** Every AI answer runs on chips in big data centers that use a lot of electricity. That's one reason not to use AI for things you could easily do yourself, like asking the time.

**Whose work is it?** This is the rule that matters most for you: **anything an AI produces is made by a tool. It isn't your work.** Your work is what you planned, wrote, tested, and can explain. If you can't explain a line, it isn't yours yet. That's the same rule the AP Computer Science Principles course uses, in three words: understand, explain, acknowledge.

So every project gets a Whose Work Is It? card, and every Save and Explain ends with "Help I used: …". Writing "Help I used: none" is great. Writing "Help I used: a chatbot explained what a KeyError means" is great too. The only bad card is a dishonest one.

**If you're 13 or older:** you may use AI services whose terms allow your age, with a parent nearby and the screen where they can see it. When AI suggests code, explain every line in writing before you use any of it. The checks in Chapter 9 (Cold Trace, Debug on the Spot, Build From a Blank) are always AI-free, so the skills have to live in your head, not in the chat.

**If you're 11 or 12:** AI happens on your parent's account, with your parent. You can still direct it: you decide the question, you read the answer, you check it. That's most of the skill anyway.

**This part is hard for everyone.** Fluent text feels smart, and it's easy to trust a confident answer. Adults fall for it too. The habit that protects you is small: check one thing.

**Try it today.** Ask your parent to make a deck of example cards with a secret rule. Learn the rule from eight cards, then test yourself on four new ones. Write down your accuracy.

**Explain it back.** Tell someone in your family why a chatbot can invent a book that doesn't exist. Use the words "likely" and "true."

**Challenge.** Make a lopsided deck for your parent: train them so they'll get a test card wrong. Then explain how you'd fix your deck.

**If you're 13 or older,** build the next-word guesser in Python (it's in this chapter). Feed it a longer text, such as a nursery rhyme, and see what it makes.

**What to save.** Your rule-guess sheet, your error tally, your word-pair table with one sentence labeled "made by our table," and a Whose Work card for this week's project.

## If it isn't clicking

### "It's basically a person."

**What it usually means:** fluent language feels like a mind. That's a very human reaction.

**Next move:** build the next-word guesser by hand (AI-04) and read its sentences aloud. Then ask, "Did our table know anything? Did it mean what it said?" Bigger models are vastly more capable, but the paper version shows that sounding sensible and knowing things aren't the same.

### "I'm never using AI. It's cheating."

**What it usually means:** your student has heard strong opinions and wants a clear rule.

**Next move:** AI-08 When Not to Use AI. Sorting real scenarios shows there are fine uses, bad uses, and "ask first" uses. Using AI isn't the problem; hiding it and not understanding it are. A student who chooses not to use AI is welcome to; the Whose Work card still says "none."

### "Everyone uses it for homework."

**What it usually means:** your student is testing whether the rule is real.

**Next move:** keep it calm. "Maybe. In our house, you explain every line, and the checks are AI-free." Then run AS-03 Explain Your Code on something recent. If they can explain it, fine. If they can't, it isn't theirs yet, and now you both know.

### "I don't get how it learns."

**What it usually means:** the abstract version came too early.

**Next move:** back to cards. Run AI-02 twice more with very easy rules, then once with a lopsided deck. The word "training" should mean "the cards I learned from" before it means anything bigger.

### When to slow down, go ahead, or step back

**Slow down** if your student still says the AI "knows" or "wants" things. **Go ahead** when they explain training data, lopsided data, and next-word prediction in their own words. **Step back to paper** whenever a chatbot session turns into chatting.

The rescue order holds: ask → wait → hint → short demo on your own copy → hand it back. Here, "your own copy" means your own card deck or your own copy of the code.

## Tools, including AI

Most of this chapter needs index cards, paper, and a pencil.

- **CodeAI's AI for Oceans** (Hour of AI) for AI-01, parent-run at 11–12.^[77](#note-77)^
- **CodeAI's AI and Machine Learning materials.** Its AI Discoveries course says its AI tools are "built for students ages 11 and up," but its AI Tutor is labeled for students 13 and up, so in this book the tutor stays off at 11–12.^[11](#note-11),[55](#note-55),[12](#note-12)^
- **Python** (Thonny or any editor from Chapter 2) for the next-word guesser at 13–14.
- **A library website or encyclopedia** for checking claims in AI-05.

**AI in this chapter.** The chapter is *about* AI, but most of it runs without one. When you do use a chatbot (AI-05, AI-07), follow the AI box in **The Computing Hour**: parent-operated at 11–12, supervised under the terms at 13–14, no private details, no photos of children. AI detectors aren't used to judge a student's work; "explain it to me" is the check.

## What "done enough" looks like

Move on when most of these are true. Skill, not birthday.

- [ ] Your student explains **training data** and a **classifier** using the card game as an example.
- [ ] They explain how **lopsided data** causes wrong guesses, and suggest one fix.
- [ ] They explain why a chatbot can **invent a source**, using the words "likely" and "true."
- [ ] They've **checked at least one AI claim** against a real source (AI-05).
- [ ] They can name one way AI uses **real resources** (chips, data centers, electricity).
- [ ] They fill out a **Whose Work Is It? card** for a project without reminders, and end each Save and Explain with "Help I used: …".
- [ ] **13–14:** they've explained AI-suggested code line by line before using any of it (AI-07).
- [ ] They answer the talk-box opening about one AI they met this week.

**A short check for the file** (Chapter 9 gathers these): **AS-03 Explain Your Code** on the next-word guesser (paper or Python). Your student explains each step or line; you write one sentence they say. The Whose Work card from AI-06 goes in the portfolio too.

**Keeping it alive.** Each semester, run one AI-05 check on a fresh topic and one OUT-06 errand. AI tools change fast. The questions in the talk box don't.

You've given your student something many adults don't have: a clear picture of what AI is, where it goes wrong, and an honest rule for using it. That will matter for a long time.

\newpage

# Chapter 9

## Records, Portfolio, and Transcript

![A portfolio binder, a manila folder, a blank calendar page, a USB stick, an index card and a pencil — still life, no people, no logos, no readable text.](art/ch09-opener.jpg){width=100%}

**This chapter is for the parent.** It gathers the habits from every other chapter into a file that shows what your student can do, answers anyone who needs to know, and doesn't take over your Fridays. The short version: a dated Debug Log, a handful of finished projects, a few honest checks each month, and a one-page summary at the end of the year.

## Why keep records

Records do three jobs.

**They show growth.** In September, your student's Debug Log entry might read, "It didn't work." By spring, it reads, "Expected 10, got 9. The loop stopped one early. Changed `range(1, 10)` to `range(1, 11)`." You won't remember that change unless it's written down. Neither will your student, and seeing it is one of the most encouraging things a young programmer can do.

**They answer a reader outside the house, if there is one.** Some states ask for a portfolio, a test, an evaluation, or just a list of subjects. Some ask for very little at this age.

**They give a future school, co-op, or program something readable.** A one-page summary with a plain course name, a short description, and a few strong pieces of work travels well.

### What the law usually asks

Homeschool law is set state by state, and it changes. This book can't tell you what your state requires, and this isn't legal advice. What it can do is show the range, using five states with very different rules.^[78](#note-78),[79](#note-79)^

- **New York** lists required subjects for grades 7 and 8: English, history and geography, science, mathematics, physical education, health, art, music, practical arts, and library skills. Computing isn't on the list.
- **Pennsylvania** lists required courses at the elementary and secondary levels, and asks for a portfolio that includes a log made at the time of instruction, samples of the student's work, and, in grade 8, standardized test results in reading and math. Computing isn't on the required list.
- **Texas** asks families to follow a written course of study that includes good citizenship, alongside reading, spelling, grammar, and math.
- **North Carolina** asks for an annual standardized test that measures English grammar, reading, spelling, and mathematics.
- **Virginia** asks the parent to send a list of the subjects to be studied each year.

None of the five names computer science for homeschooled students in grades 6 to 8. That doesn't mean computing doesn't count. It means teaching it is your choice, and your records show what happened. It's also a choice fewer families make than you might think: in a 2016 federal survey, about 17 percent of homeschooled students in grades 6 to 8 had computer science as part of their home instruction that year.^[80](#note-80)^ Read your own state's current law, and HSLDA's state pages are a convenient starting point.^[78](#note-78),[79](#note-79)^

## The portfolio

A portfolio is a folder of work that shows what your student can do. Think of it as a few strong pieces with notes, not a box of everything.

**What goes in, over a year:**

- **Six to eight finished projects** at 11–12 (up to ten at 13–14), each with:
  - version saves and a one-line changelog (PROJ-02);
  - a short test table: input, expected, actual;
  - a Whose Work Is It? card (AI-06), even when the card says "no help used."
- **The Debug Log**, the dated notebook (paper or a plain file) that holds predictions, bugs, clues, and fixes. Three or four sample pages per semester are plenty for the portfolio; the whole log stays at home (BUG-08).
- **A monthly Cold Trace** (AS-01), dated.
- **One Build From a Blank per semester** (AS-04), dated.
- **A demo-day feedback page** for at least one project (PROJ-03).
- **Screenshots cropped to the work.** First names only; no faces, school, or town. See Family safety basics in **The Computing Hour**.
- **For 13–14:** a note that two-step login is on for the accounts your student uses, and the signed family device agreement from Chapter 7.

**A sample Debug Log page.** Here is an illustration, not a real family.

> **12 November.** Guessing game v4.
> *Predicted:* "Too low" when I guess 3 and the number is 7.
> *Actual:* It said "Too high."
> *Clue:* I swapped the `<` and `>` in the if.
> *Fix:* Swapped them back. Tested 3, 7, and 9.
> *Learned:* Test a number on each side, not just one.

A page like that shows prediction, testing, and reasoning in six lines. Three or four of those per semester say more than a stack of finished programs.

**How to name files.** Date first, then a plain description: `2026-10-14-guessing-game-v3.py`. Files sort themselves into order, and anyone can find them (SYS-03, from Chapter 6). Keep a top folder per school year, with subfolders for projects, checks, and the log.

**What stays out.** Generated code, pages, or pictures filed as your student's work. Copied tutorial code without credit. Anything your student can't explain. Real passwords, login codes, or the private Footprint Audit list from Chapter 7.

## Projects and demo days

A project every four to six weeks is a good rhythm. Each one has a beginning, a middle, and an end you can file.

### In-home — PROJ-01 Project One-Pager

- **Time:** 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** one sheet of paper.
- **Safety and privacy:** first names only.
- **The fun:** planning something your student actually wants to build.
- **The skill:** problem, user, features, test plan, and schedule on one page.
- **The crumb:** the one-pager, filed at the front of the project folder.

**11–12:** You ask the questions and your student answers: "Who's it for? What does it need to do first?" Three features is plenty.

**13–14:** Your student writes it, and you, as the first user, sign off on the test plan.

### In-home — PROJ-02 Version Saves and Changelog

- **Time:** 10 minutes, at the end of each work session.
- **Materials:** the project folder and a plain text file named `changelog`.
- **Safety and privacy:** files live in two places.
- **The fun:** watching the project grow from v1 to v6.
- **The skill:** saving numbered versions, each with a one-line note.
- **The crumb:** the changelog file: "v3: added a score. v4: fixed the score resetting to zero."

### In-home — PROJ-03 Demo Day

- **Time:** 20 minutes.
- **Materials:** the finished project, an audience (family, grandparents on a video call, or a co-op group), and a feedback sheet.
- **Safety and privacy:** show the project, not personal details; video calls with family only.
- **The fun:** a real audience, applause included.
- **The skill:** explaining your work to users and collecting their feedback.
- **The crumb:** feedback notes, with one change your student would make next.

Your student shows the project, explains one part of the code, and asks two questions: "What was confusing?" and "What would you add?" The feedback page goes in the portfolio.

**Competitions.** If your student enters a contest from Chapter 10, such as Bebras or the Congressional App Challenge, file the entry, the date, and any certificate as an artifact. A program's certificate is evidence of something your student did. It isn't a course, a credit, or a grade.

## The check set

Six short checks, gathered from across the book, tell you more than any test. Three of them are always AI-free and tutorial-free: Cold Trace, Debug on the Spot, and Build From a Blank. They show what lives in your student's head.

- **AS-01 Cold Trace** (10 minutes, monthly). A short program on paper your student hasn't seen. They predict the output, line by line, without running it. Date the sheet.
- **AS-02 Debug on the Spot** (15 minutes). You plant one bug in a working program. Your student finds and fixes it while talking aloud. You write one line: what they did and how they found it.
- **AS-03 Explain Your Code** (10 minutes). Your student explains one of their own programs line by line. You write down one sentence they say.
- **AS-04 Build From a Blank** (30 minutes, once a semester). A small spec, an empty file, no tutorial, no AI. For example: "Ask for three numbers and print the largest." Date the file and file it as is, bugs and all.
- **AS-05 Whose Work Audit** (10 minutes). Pick a project, check its Whose Work card against the code, and ask about two lines. Your student signs an audit line: "This card is accurate."
- **AS-06 Portfolio Pick with Reasons** (15 minutes, end of semester). Your student chooses the pieces that best show what they can do and writes one sentence about each.

**Sample Build From a Blank specs.** At 11–12: "Ask for a name and print a greeting three times," or "Ask for two numbers and say which is bigger." At 13–14: "Write a function that takes a list of scores and returns the average, and test it with three lists," or "Make a page with a heading, a list, and one button that changes the text." Keep each spec to one or two sentences, and keep the old ones; next semester's spec should be a little harder.

**Recording what you see.** Once a semester, mark each big idea as seen, growing, or not yet. This is a record of what you've watched happen, not a score, a percentile, or a validated rubric.

| Big idea | Seen | Growing | Not yet |
|---|---|---|---|
| Planning (one-pagers, pseudocode) | | | |
| Python basics (variables, decisions, loops) | | | |
| Debugging (Debug Log, Expected/Actual) | | | |
| Functions and lists | | | |
| Data (collecting, charting, questioning) | | | |
| Web (HTML, CSS, a little JavaScript) | | | |
| Systems and networks | | | |
| Security habits | | | |
| AI understanding and honest use | | | |

Add a date and a one-line note for anything marked "seen": "Explained why the loop ran one short (AS-03, 12 March)."

### A portfolio-pick conversation (optional)

At AS-06 time, use the talk-box opening for this chapter: "Which piece shows what you can really do — and could you explain every line of it?"

Here is an illustration, not a real family.

> **Parent:** Which piece shows what you can really do — and could you explain every line of it?
> **Student:** The quiz game. Except the part that shuffles the questions. I copied that from the book.
> **Parent:** Good catch. Is it on the Whose Work card?
> **Student:** It is now.

Count to five in your head after you ask. When the question is about code on the screen, let them read as long as they need. If your student picks the flashiest project instead of the one they understand best, ask, "Which line in it would you most like to explain?"

## A year of records at a glance

- **September:** set up the year folder (two places), start the hours log and the Debug Log, and sign or review the family device agreement.
- **Every week:** the Friday routine (below).
- **Every month:** one Cold Trace.
- **Every four to six weeks:** a finished project with its one-pager, changelog, test table, and Whose Work card, and a demo when you can.
- **Each semester:** a Build From a Blank, a Whose Work Audit, a portfolio pick, and the seen/growing/not-yet table.
- **June:** the one-page year summary and the narrative.

That's the whole system. Most weeks, it's ten minutes.

## Hours and the year summary

**The hours log** is simple: date, minutes, and what you did, one line per session. "14 Oct, 55 min, guessing game v3, fixed the score bug." A paper calendar or a spreadsheet both work. It's a record, not a quota. Many families find that two or three Computing Hours a week, plus a few errands and short checks, add up faster than they expected.

**The year summary** is one page. It holds the course name, a one-line description, the main topics, a list of projects, the check set dates, the seen/growing/not-yet table, approximate hours, and a short narrative paragraph written by you.

### Writing the narrative

The narrative paragraph is where you sound like yourself. Three or four sentences are enough. These starters help:

- "This year, [name] learned to…"
- "The piece that best shows this is… because…"
- "A habit that grew: …"
- "Still growing: …"
- "Next year: …"

Be specific. "Learned loops" says little. "Can predict how many times a loop runs and fix it when it's off by one" says a lot. Name one thing that's still growing; an honest "not yet" makes the rest of the page more believable.

### If someone asks for a grade

Most families don't need letter grades at this age. If your state, an evaluator, or a school asks for one, set the criteria in writing at the start of the semester, and grade against them at the end. For example:

- projects finished, each with a test table and a Whose Work card;
- the monthly Cold Traces done, and the errors explained;
- one Build From a Blank that runs, even if it's simple;
- Debug Log entries that show Expected/Actual and a fix.

Then the grade describes work you can point to. Write the criteria on the summary page so a reader can see what the grade means.

## Transcript labels

Use a course name a stranger can read.

- **Computer Science** is the default for most years with this book.
- **Introduction to Programming** fits a year that was mostly Python and projects.
- **Digital Literacy** fits only a year that was mostly safety and everyday use.

Put the details in the description line, not the title. Never use a brand, a program, a tool, or a book title as the course name, and don't claim a certificate from this book; there isn't one. At ages 11 to 14, the summary doesn't need credits.

**If your fourteen-year-old's year will count for high school.** Some families count a fourteen-year-old's year as ninth grade. Whether this year counts toward high school is a family decision within your state's rules. If it does, title the course plainly (for example, "Introduction to Computer Science"), keep the hours log carefully, and keep the check set going. A common homeschool guideline from HSLDA suggests at least 150 logged hours for one full credit in a core course and 120 or more for an elective. That's a guideline, not a law.^[81](#note-81)^ Note on the record that the credit is family-assigned. For designing high-school computing courses, see *Computing for Emerging Adults*, the next book in this series.

## Honest and dishonest records

Records are only useful if they're true. That's not a moral lecture; it's practical. A record that overstates what your student can do sets them up for a class they're not ready for.

| Honest | Not honest |
|---|---|
| "Wrote a quiz game in Python; borrowed the shuffle code from the book, with credit." | "Wrote a quiz game in Python," with borrowed code unmarked. |
| "Help I used: a chatbot explained a KeyError; I fixed it myself." | Generated code filed as the student's own. |
| A Build From a Blank filed with its bugs. | Only the polished final versions. |
| "Bebras Cadets, participation certificate" under artifacts. | The certificate listed as a course or credit. |
| "About 90 hours, from the log." | A round number nobody wrote down. |

Anything an AI produces is made by a tool, never your student's own work, and never a portfolio piece on its own. The Whose Work card travels with every project, and the AI-free checks show what your student can do without help. For the full rules, see the AI box in **The Computing Hour**.

## Special situations

**Two students at different levels.** Keep separate folders and separate logs, even if they share a Computing Hour. An older sibling's demo day can be a younger sibling's audience, and that counts for both.

**A student who dislikes writing.** Let them dictate the Debug Log and the Whose Work card while you write, or record a short audio explanation of a project. Oral explanation is real evidence; the AS-03 check is spoken anyway.

**A long break.** Illness, a move, or a new baby happens. Note the dates in the log ("No Computing Hours, 3–28 February: family move") and pick up where you left off. A record with an honest gap is better than one that pretends.

**A student who races ahead.** Let the portfolio show it: harder projects, a competition entry, a Build From a Blank with a tougher spec. Keep the checks going. A fast student still benefits from explaining every line.

**A student who needs more time.** Move at their pace. Seen, growing, and not yet don't come with deadlines. A year where Python basics went from "not yet" to "seen" is a good year.

## Where records live

Every file lives in two places: the family drive (or a USB stick) and one backup. Not in a chatbot, and not on a shared bot computer. If you use Grok Bot, remember that all of your bots share one computer. Keep photos of your children and family off it, and keep your student's portfolio on a family drive.^[14](#note-14)^

A Friday routine keeps it painless. Ten minutes, once a week:

1. Copy the week's files to both places.
2. Add up the week's minutes in the hours log.
3. Pick one Debug Log page or test table for the portfolio.
4. Check that every finished project has its Whose Work card.
5. Ask your student, "What are you proudest of this week?" and write down the answer.

**Sharing the portfolio.** If an evaluator, a co-op teacher, or a school needs to see the work, share copies of the pieces they need, not the whole drive. Check that screenshots are cropped, that only first names appear, and that nothing private from Chapter 7 is in the folder you send.

## Two sample records

These are illustrations, not real students.

**Example A: an eleven-year-old in grade 6**

- *Course:* Computer Science
- *Description:* Algorithms and pseudocode; first Python programs (variables, decisions, loops); debugging with a log; spreadsheets and charts; binary and networks; online safety habits; AI concepts (parent-operated).
- *Evidence:* six projects with version saves; monthly Cold Traces; Debug Log samples; the family device agreement.
- *Hours:* about 90 (log attached).
- *Narrative:* "This year she learned to predict before running and to keep a Debug Log. Her best piece is a number-guessing game she can explain line by line. Next year: functions and a first website."
