**Example B: a thirteen-year-old in grade 8**

- *Course:* Introduction to Programming
- *Description:* Python (functions, lists, testing); a three-page website with HTML, CSS, and a little JavaScript; a data investigation; encryption concepts; cybersecurity habits; supervised AI use with disclosure.
- *Evidence:* nine projects with Whose Work cards; a Build From a Blank each semester; demo-day feedback.
- *Artifacts:* Bebras (Cadets) participation certificate.
- *Narrative:* "He plans with a one-pager, tests with a table, and explains AI-suggested code before using any of it. His demo-day feedback led to a clearer menu in his quiz game."

## If your student moves to a school

Bring the one-page year summary, two or three portfolio pieces your student can explain, and a recent Cold Trace and Build From a Blank. A teacher can read those in five minutes. Ask your student to be ready to explain one program aloud; that does more than any page.

## Looking ahead

Your student will leave this book with a year or more of honest records: projects they can explain, checks done without help, and a Debug Log that shows how they think. At fifteen or so, *Computing for Emerging Adults* picks up from here, with high-school course design, credits, and transcripts.

Keep the folder. Years from now, that September page that said "It didn't work" will be a pleasure to read.

\newpage

# Chapter 10

## Resources

![A library corner: a shelf of untitled books above a table with a laptop, a closed book, a library card and a small LED board — still life, no people, no logos, no readable text.](art/ch10-opener.jpg){width=100%}

**This chapter is for the parent.** It lists tools, books, programs, and references that fit this book, sorted by what they're for. It isn't a ranking, and nothing here is an advertisement. You can teach the whole book with a library card, paper, a pencil, and one shared computer. Everything below is optional.

Three habits make the list easy to use:

- **Library first.** Borrow before you buy, whether it's a book, a board, or an hour on a library computer.
- **Free first.** Every core tool in this book has a free option. Prices aren't listed here, because they change.
- **Check current terms and dates.** Sites rename, change their age rules, and occasionally close. Every link below was checked on 6 October 2026. Before you rely on one, look again, and check the current terms for your student's age.

## How to read this list

Each entry tells you what it's for, which ages it fits, and whether it needs an account. The account rules follow Family safety basics in **The Computing Hour**: at 11–12, you create and hold every login; at 13–14, your student may hold passwords for family-approved tools, with your sealed copy, and only on services whose terms allow their age.

When a resource includes an AI feature, the rules in the AI box in **The Computing Hour** apply, whatever the resource's own page says.

## Choosing a tool: five questions

Before adding anything new, run it past five questions. They take a minute, and they answer most of what you need to know.

1. **Is it free, or is there a free version that does what we need?**
2. **Does it need an account, and do its terms allow my student's age?** If an account is needed at 11–12, you create and hold it.
3. **Does it have AI features, and can I turn them off or supervise them?** The AI box in **The Computing Hour** decides, not the tool's own settings.
4. **Can my student's work be saved to our own computer?** Files that live only on a website disappear when the website does. Keep everything in two places.
5. **Does it help the habit, or replace it?** A tool that lets your student predict, test, and debug helps. A tool that writes the program for them doesn't, however impressive it looks.

## Start at the library

Your public library is the best computing resource most families have. It usually offers:

- **Books** on Python, web design, and how computers work, at several reading levels. Ask a librarian for beginner Python books written for young teens, and look at the publication date; for Python, newer is usually better.
- **Public computers** for a family with one device or none.
- **Programs and clubs**, sometimes including coding clubs or maker spaces with boards like the micro:bit to borrow or use on site.
- **Interlibrary loan** for the book your branch doesn't have.
- **E-books and audiobooks** through the library's apps, on a library card. Programming books read better on paper or a large screen, but books about computing history and how things work are fine on a tablet.

A good librarian is a resource in their own right. Tell them your student's age and what they're learning, and ask what's new.

**What to look for in a book.** A good beginner programming book for this age uses a current version of Python 3, shows short programs your student types in and changes, explains errors rather than hiding them, and includes small projects with room to make them your own. Skim a chapter before borrowing: if the examples are long and copied without explanation, keep looking. Books on how computers and networks work, and on the history of computing, make good reading-aloud companions for Chapters 6 to 8.

## Free, unplugged

About half of this book's work happens away from the screen. These keep it going.

**CS Unplugged.** A free collection of computer science activities with cards, string, chalk, and games, from the University of Canterbury in New Zealand. Activities are marked by age, and several fit this book well: its searching activities are listed for ages 11 to 14, and its sorting networks for ages 5 to 14. The materials are under a Creative Commons license that allows sharing with credit.^[25](#note-25)^ *Fit:* unplugged practice, co-op days, a sibling who's younger.

**Bebras Computing Challenge.** A free, international puzzle challenge that tests computational reasoning without coding. In the United States it usually runs in November (9 to 20 November in 2026); students have 45 minutes, and the age groups include Benjamins (10–12), Cadets (12–14), and Juniors (14–16). An adult coordinator registers students, and past puzzles make good practice.^[82](#note-82)^ *Fit:* a short, low-pressure first competition.

**This book's own unplugged try-its.** Every chapter has activities that need only paper, cards, or a walk. They're the backbone of the Hour.

## Free, on a screen

**Python and its documentation.** Python is free from python.org, and its official documentation, including a beginner's tutorial, lives at docs.python.org.^[83](#note-83)^ *Fit:* the main language of this book. The tutorial is dense; use it as a reference at 13–14 rather than a first lesson.

**Thonny.** A free, beginner-friendly Python editor that comes with Python built in, runs offline, needs no account, and lets your student step through a program line by line and watch the variables change.^[2](#note-2)^ *Fit:* this book's default Python tool, especially for Chapters 2 to 4.

**MDN Learn web development.** Mozilla's free, well-organized lessons on HTML, CSS, and JavaScript.^[47](#note-47)^ *Fit:* Chapter 5, mostly as a parent reference at 11–12 and a student reference at 13–14. All you need is a text editor and a browser.

**Raspberry Pi Code Editor and projects.** A free browser editor for Python and web pages, with a large library of free step-by-step projects from the Raspberry Pi Foundation.^[35](#note-35)^ *Fit:* Python in a browser when you can't install software. You can use the editor without an account; saving work online needs one, and for creators under 13 a parent or guardian sets it up with their own email address. Check the current rules before you start.

**micro:bit Python Editor.** A free browser editor for the micro:bit, a small programmable board. Its guide says it supports "students aged 11-14," and it includes a simulator, so your student can write and test programs even without a board. It runs in a web browser and can work offline.^[64](#note-64)^ *Fit:* physical computing, before or instead of buying hardware.

**Scratch.** MIT's free block-based language. At this age it's best for quick sketches: planning a game's logic before writing it in Python, or showing a younger sibling an idea. Anyone under 16 registering a new account needs a parent's or guardian's permission by email; without it, projects can be made and saved, but not shared.^[84](#note-84)^ *Fit:* sketches and siblings, not the main language.

**CodeAI (formerly Code.org) courses.** CodeAI offers a free middle-school course, CS Discoveries, listed for grades 6 to 10, and an AI Discoveries course whose AI tools are "built for students ages 11 and up." Both need an account; at 11–12, you create and hold it. CodeAI's AI Tutor is labeled for students 13 and up, so in this book it stays off at 11–12 and, at 13–14, is used only with you enabling and supervising it.^[54](#note-54),[55](#note-55),[11](#note-11),[36](#note-36),[12](#note-12)^ CodeAI's Python Lab is "designed for students aged 13+."^[54](#note-54),[55](#note-55),[11](#note-11),[36](#note-36),[12](#note-12)^ *Fit:* a ready-made course spine for families who want one, used alongside this book's habits.

**Not recommended now: Trinket.** It was a popular browser Python tool, but it shut down on 31 August 2026, and its content is no longer available.^[37](#note-37)^ If an older book or worksheet points to it, use Thonny or the Raspberry Pi Code Editor instead.

## A starter kit by band

**For 11–12.** Paper, pencils, index cards, and a Debug Log notebook. Thonny on the family computer. A library book on beginning Python. CS Unplugged for unplugged days. The micro:bit simulator if your student likes gadgets. Any accounts, such as a CodeAI course, are yours. That's a full year.

**For 13–14.** All of the above, plus MDN for web pages, the Raspberry Pi Code Editor for working away from home, and a first competition if your student wants one (Bebras is the gentlest). Accounts your student holds follow the terms, with your sealed copy and two-step login on. A Git preview if they're curious.

**When a tool changes or closes.** It happens; Trinket is the recent example. If your student's files live in two places, a closure is an inconvenience, not a loss. Open the project in another editor and carry on. When a site renames itself, as Code.org did when it became CodeAI, the bookmarks change and the lessons usually don't.

## Fit at a glance

| Need | Fit | Ages and accounts |
|---|---|---|
| Python at home, offline | Thonny | Free; no account |
| Python in a browser | Raspberry Pi Code Editor | Free; check current account rules |
| Python in a course | CodeAI Python Lab | Designed for 13+ |
| A full middle-school course | CodeAI CS Discoveries or AI Discoveries | Free; account, parent-made at 11–12; AI Tutor 13+ |
| Blocks for sketches | Scratch | Under-16 accounts need a parent's email |
| Web pages | Text editor, browser, MDN | No account |
| Physical computing | micro:bit Python Editor with simulator | Free; hardware optional |
| Unplugged | CS Unplugged | Free |
| Puzzle challenge | Bebras | Free; an adult coordinator registers |
| Security practice | CyLab Security Academy | 13–14, with a parent |
| Version control (preview) | Git with a hosted service | 13+ for most services; optional |

## Resources by chapter

| Chapter | Most useful here |
|---|---|
| 1. What computing looks like at 11–14 | CS Unplugged; a library book on how computers work |
| 2. From blocks to text: Python | Thonny; Scratch for a quick sketch; docs.python.org at 13–14 |
| 3. Debugging | Thonny's step-through view; the Debug Log |
| 4. Functions, lists, and data | Thonny; CS Unplugged searching and sorting |
| 5. Building for the web | A text editor, a browser, and MDN; Raspberry Pi projects |
| 6. How computers and networks work | CS Unplugged; the micro:bit simulator; a library book |
| 7. Safe, smart, and kind online | CISA Secure Our World; the FTC's COPPA page; the AAP Family Media Plan |
| 8. AI up close | CodeAI's AI for Oceans, parent-run at 11–12; the AI literacy framework (for you) |
| 9. Records | HSLDA's state pages; your own state's official text |

## Optional hardware

None of this is required. Borrow first if you can.

- **A micro:bit.** A small, sturdy board with lights, buttons, and sensors, programmed in Python. Try the simulator first; if your student loves it, a board is a natural next step. Some libraries lend them.
- **An Arduino-style board** for a 13–14-year-old who enjoys the micro:bit and wants wires, sensors, and motors. These take more patience. If an online account is needed for the editor, you create and hold it. Arduino links every account for a child under 18 to a parent, and a parent creates or approves accounts for children under 14. Check the current terms.^[85](#note-85)^
- **A used laptop.** Any reasonably recent used laptop that runs a current, updated operating system will run Thonny and a browser comfortably. An adult wipes and sets it up. See Family safety basics in **The Computing Hour** on old hardware.

**Before you buy anything, ask:** Can we borrow it? Is there a free simulator? Will we use it more than twice? Does it need an account, and who will hold it?

## Competitions and programs

All optional. Competitions suit some students and not others, and a student who'd rather build quietly is doing just as well. Dates and rules change every year; check with each program before you plan around it. Anything your student earns is filed as an artifact, never as a course or credit (Chapter 9).

**Deciding whether to enter.** Ask your student, not just yourself. A good first contest is short, free, and low-stakes, and the point is the puzzles, not the ranking. Watch how your student feels afterward. If it was fun, try another next year. If it was stressful, that's useful to know, and building projects at home is every bit as good.

- **Bebras.** Above. A gentle first contest.
- **American Computer Science League (ACSL), Junior Division.** For "junior high and middle school students who are learning programming or who have coding experience," with no student beyond grade 9. It combines a short written test with a programming problem. An adult advisor registers, not the student; registration typically closes at the end of December (31 December 2026 for the 2026–27 season).^[86](#note-86)^ *Fit:* a 13–14-year-old who likes Python puzzles.
- **CyberPatriot, Middle School Division.** A team cybersecurity competition from the Air & Space Forces Association, with two to six students and an adult coach. The current rules book lists homeschools among the groups that may field middle school teams, with verification steps for independent homeschools. Check with the program about eligibility and dates before you plan; registration typically closes in early October.^[87](#note-87)^ *Fit:* a co-op or a group of families, at 13–14.
- **Congressional App Challenge.** Students build an app in any language and enter it in their congressional district. Entrants must be middle or high school students, alone or in teams of up to four; the deadline is usually in late October (26 October in 2026).^[88](#note-88)^ *Fit:* a 13–14-year-old with a finished project and a reason to build it.
- **CyLab Security Academy (formerly picoCTF).** Carnegie Mellon University's free cybersecurity learning platform, with practice challenges and competitions. Older picoCTF logins created before 8 May 2026 still work.^[89](#note-89)^ *Fit:* 13–14, with a parent and an account that follows the terms.
- **USA Computing Olympiad (USACO).** Free online programming contests aimed at "high-school computing students at all levels."^[90](#note-90)^ *Fit:* a strong 13–14-year-old who's ready for harder problems. It's a stretch, and that's fine to wait on.

## A preview of version control

Professional programmers use a tool called **Git** to save versions of their work and share it. Hosted services such as GitHub keep copies online. At 13–14, a curious student can try it, with a parent-created account; GitHub's terms say a user "must be at least 13 years of age."^[38](#note-38)^ It's never required. The version saves and changelog in Chapter 9 teach the same idea with a folder and a text file.

## For the parent: references worth knowing

These are for you. Your student doesn't need them.

**CSTA standards for grades 6 to 8.** The Computer Science Teachers Association's 2026 standards describe what's age-reasonable for middle school. Use them as a map, not a checklist; they're written for schools. CSTA's guidance pictures middle-school computing as the equivalent of a yearlong course in the grade 6–8 band, which is roughly what this book covers. Licensed CC BY-NC-SA 4.0.^[17](#note-17),[15](#note-15),[16](#note-16)^

> **Reading a standard's code.** Take MS-PRO-TR-19. **MS** is the band (middle school, grades 6–8). **PRO** is the concept: Algorithms & Design (ALG), Programming (PRO), Data & Analysis (DAT), Systems & Security (SYS), or Computing & Society (SOC). **TR** is a two-letter topic tag within the concept (the TR standards are about testing and refining). **19** is the number. MS-PRO-TR-19 reads: "Use systematic strategies to test, refine, and document changes to a computing technology to meet the intended purpose." That's the Debug Log and the changelog, in standards language.

**AI literacy framework.** The OECD and the European Commission have published *Empowering Learners for the Age of AI*, an AI literacy framework for primary and secondary education. It describes AI literacy as knowledge, skills, and attitudes that help learners understand how AI works, evaluate its outputs, and use it ethically and creatively.^[91](#note-91)^ Useful if you want the bigger picture behind Chapter 8.

**Children's privacy.** The FTC's page on protecting your child's privacy online explains COPPA in plain words.^[4](#note-4)^

**Online safety.** CISA's Secure Our World pages cover the four habits from Chapter 7, with short printable tips.^[67](#note-67)^

**Phones and media.** The American Academy of Pediatrics' Family Media Plan is a free, fill-in tool for writing your family's rules, and its HealthyChildren article on a child's first phone walks through readiness.^[92](#note-92),[93](#note-93)^

**If an image is shared.** NCMEC's Take It Down service helps remove images taken of someone under 18 from participating platforms.^[75](#note-75)^

**Homeschool law.** HSLDA's state pages summarize each state's requirements; read the official text too.^[79](#note-79)^

> **A preview: AP Computer Science Principles.** Years from now, your student may take AP Computer Science Principles, a high-school course from the College Board. The College Board says its AP computer science courses require familiarity with concepts from Algebra 1 and don't require any prior coding experience.^[94](#note-94)^ AI is allowed as a supplement, but students must review and understand any code written with AI, be ready to explain it in detail, and acknowledge it.^[23](#note-23)^ That's years away, and the habits in this book are the right ones for it.

## Low-device and no-device homes, co-ops, and siblings

**One device, or none.** Most U.S. households had at least one type of computer in 2021, but "at least one" can mean a single phone.^[95](#note-95)^ If yours has one shared computer, this book works: about half the work is unplugged, and the screen parts are short. With no computer, library computers and a USB stick for files cover the Python chapters. A phone is a poor place to write code, but it's fine for reading this book's examples and taking photos of paper work for the portfolio.

**Co-ops.** Many try-its work better with a group: Phish or Fine? as a team game, demo days with a real audience, a CyberPatriot team. One parent can lead the Hour for several families. Keep each student's records separate, and first names only on anything shared.

**Siblings.** A younger sibling can play the user in a demo, try the unplugged activities, or work through *Computing for Little Thinkers* alongside. An older sibling can be the audience, the tester, or a patient helper, as long as they use the rescue order and don't take over the keyboard.

**Different learners.** Most editors let you enlarge the text, and a bigger font helps everyone. A student who finds typing slow can plan on paper first and type less. A student who reads slowly does well with the unplugged try-its and with spoken explanations, which count fully in the check set (Chapter 9). Move at your student's pace; the "done enough" lists are about skill, not speed.

## Where to go next in this series

- ***Computing for Little Thinkers*** for a younger sibling, or for a gentle unplugged start with an older child.
- ***Computing for Emerging Adults*** for ages 15 to 18: high-school courses, credits, and transcripts.
- ***Computing Refresh for Adults*** for a parent who'd like to understand the ideas in this book more deeply.

Pick one tool, one library book, and one project. That's enough to start.

\newpage

# A Note on Sources

Standards, studies, government pages, program pages and platform terms named in the chapters are listed in Notes, in one series at the back, so the teaching pages can keep a plain, practical voice. When the same source comes up in several chapters, it has one note, and every mark that cites it carries that note's number.

Sources were re-read on 6 October 2026. A few pages refused automated reading that day, and the notes say how their wording was checked. The CDC's page on young teens was checked against an archived copy of the same page. Meta's AI terms were checked against a search engine's copy of the official page, because the age section does not open for automated readers. The PRIMM study's numbers (its control group and effect size) come from the authors' accepted manuscript as shown in a search engine's excerpt, because the publisher's copy would not load. The Parsons-problem study is described from its abstract. The high-school math study in the AI box comes from a different age and subject, and the book says so where it uses it.

Some things rest on the best available source rather than a perfect one, and the book says so instead of filling the gap. The share of homeschooled middle-schoolers taught computer science comes from a 2016 federal survey, the most recent found that asked. The CSTA standards are written for schools; this book uses them as a map, not a requirement. The research on transfer from programming to other subjects is mixed, so the book treats it as possible, not promised. The Python samples were run in Python 3.13 to check their output, while Thonny installs Python 3.14, so an error message may be worded slightly differently on your screen. The micro:bit programs follow the micro:bit's official documentation but were not run on a physical board. The five-step Hour, the count-to-five pause, the dose suggestions, the try-its, the age rules for AI and accounts, and the other practice rules are this book's own suggestions, not research findings, and the families in the examples are illustrations, not real people. HSLDA is an advocacy organization; its credit-hour figures are guidelines, not law.

Competition dates in the notes belong to the 2026–27 season and are labeled that way; the chapters give the usual pattern instead, because dates change every year. Program pages, platform terms, age rules, state rules and dates change; the date in each note says when it was read. Nothing here is legal advice. Check your own state's homeschool rules with the state itself. Access date for web sources: 6 October 2026.

\newpage

[1.]{#note-1} National Center for Education Statistics, *Parent and Family Involvement in Education: 2023* (NCES 2024-113), U.S. Department of Education, 2024, <https://nces.ed.gov/pubs2024/2024113.pdf>, accessed 6 October 2026. Among students aged 5 through 17 in kindergarten through grade 12, "some 3.4 percent were reported as being homeschooled" in 2022–23, about 1,765,000 students.

Free, with no account needed; version 5.0.0 installers for Windows, macOS, and Linux include Python 3.14 ("Thonny comes with Python 3.14 built in, so just one simple installer is needed"). The page says to "select View → Variables" to watch variables; "Just press Ctrl+F5 instead of F5 and you can run your programs step-by-step, no breakpoints needed. Press F6 for a big step and F7 for a small step"; and that Thonny "highlights syntax errors. Unclosed quotes and parentheses are the most common beginners' syntax errors." Thonny is open-source software under the MIT License (Thonny project, "LICENSE.txt," GitHub, <https://github.com/thonny/thonny/blob/master/LICENSE.txt>, accessed 6 October 2026).

[3.]{#note-3} Colleen M. Lewis, "Is Pair Programming More Effective than Other Forms of Collaboration for Young Students?," *Computer Science Education* 21, no. 2 (2011): 105–134, <https://doi.org/10.1080/08993408.2011.579805>, accessed 6 October 2026. Two summer enrichment classes for students entering sixth grade, using Scratch. Students who pair-programmed and students who worked on their own computers with frequent collaboration "did not differ significantly in their performance on daily quizzes or responses to attitudinal survey questions," and students on their own computers "completed exercises more quickly." A small study of two classes, not a general verdict on pairing.

[4.]{#note-4} Federal Trade Commission, "Protecting Your Child's Privacy Online," FTC Consumer Advice, May 2026, <https://consumer.ftc.gov/articles/protecting-your-childs-privacy-online>, accessed 6 October 2026. "As a parent, you have control over the personal information companies collect online from your kids under 13." The Children's Online Privacy Protection Act (COPPA) Rule covers sites and services, including apps, "directed to children under 13," and general-audience sites that know they are collecting personal information from children that age; they must "notify parents directly and get their approval." Personal information includes a child's full name, address, phone number or email address, physical location, "photos, videos and audio recordings of the child," government identifiers, biometric identifiers, and persistent identifiers such as IP addresses. COPPA governs how companies collect children's data; it does not rate content or certify a site as safe.

[5.]{#note-5} Mary Budd Rowe, "Wait-Time and Rewards as Instructional Variables: Their Influence on Language, Logic, and Fate Control," paper presented to the National Association for Research in Science Teaching, April 1972, ERIC ED061103, <https://eric.ed.gov/?id=ED061103>; and Robert J. Stahl, "Using 'Think-Time' and 'Wait-Time' Skillfully in the Classroom," ERIC Digest, May 1994, ERIC ED370885, <https://eric.ed.gov/?id=ED370885>; both accessed 6 October 2026. Rowe's abstract reports that "Elementary science teachers allow an average of one second for a response to a question," and that when wait times "are extended to three to five seconds," responses grow longer and students offer more unprompted answers and ask more questions. Stahl's digest reports that "after at least three seconds of uninterrupted silence, a significant number of positive outcomes occur for students and teachers." These are classroom studies. "Count to five in your head" is a pause habit drawn from them, not a measured rule for a kitchen table.

[6.]{#note-6} Tiffany Munzer et al., Council on Communications and Media, American Academy of Pediatrics, "Digital Ecosystems, Children, and Adolescents: Policy Statement," *Pediatrics* 157, no. 2 (February 2026): e2025075320, published online 20 January 2026, <https://publications.aap.org/pediatrics/article/157/2/e2025075320/206129/>, accessed 6 October 2026. The statement suggests family-tailored limits ranging up to "1 to 2 hours/d or more of entertainment (not school-related) media for school-aged children and teens," adding that "the most important considerations are high-quality content and prioritizing healthy activities"; and advises families to "establish phone-free zones during mealtimes, in bedrooms, an hour before bed, and during homework." On phones: "Research does not point to a specific age for teen smartphone readiness, and it varies by teen. Considerations include: teen digital media literacy, truthfulness with caregivers, ability to navigate conflict in social situations, and practical needs. Caregivers should set and monitor privacy, download, content, purchase, and time settings. Various smartphones, flip phones, or watches can minimize access to apps teens may not be ready for." On controls: set "parental controls (time, downloads, contacts, purchases) using tools on wireless routers, smartphones, tablets, gaming consoles/platforms, social media accounts, and school devices."

[7.]{#note-7} World Health Organization, *WHO Guidelines on Physical Activity and Sedentary Behaviour*, WHO, 25 November 2020, <https://www.who.int/publications/i/item/9789240015128>, accessed 6 October 2026. For ages 5–17: "an average of 60 minutes per day of moderate- to vigorous-intensity" physical activity across the week, and children and adolescents "should limit the amount of time spent being sedentary, particularly the amount of recreational screen time." The guidelines give no numeric screen limit.

[8.]{#note-8} Shalini Paruthi et al., "Recommended Amount of Sleep for Pediatric Populations: A Consensus Statement of the American Academy of Sleep Medicine," *Journal of Clinical Sleep Medicine* 12, no. 6 (2016): 785–786, <https://aasm.org/resources/pdf/pediatricsleepdurationconsensus.pdf>, accessed 6 October 2026: "Children 6 to 12 years of age should sleep 9 to 12 hours per 24 hours" and "Teenagers 13 to 18 years of age should sleep 8 to 10 hours per 24 hours." The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention page "About Sleep" (15 May 2024, <https://www.cdc.gov/sleep/about/index.html>, accessed 6 October 2026) lists the same ranges (school age, 6–12 years, 9–12 hours; teens, 13–17 years, 8–10 hours) and cites this statement.

[13.]{#note-13} Hamsa Bastani, Osbert Bastani, Alp Sungu, Haosen Ge, Özge Kabakcı, and Rei Mariman, "Generative AI without Guardrails Can Harm Learning: Evidence from High School Mathematics," *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences* 122, no. 26 (2025): e2422633122, <https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.2422633122>, accessed 6 October 2026. A field experiment with nearly 1,000 high-school math students in Turkey. Students with unrestricted access to a chatbot did better on practice problems and worse on a later exam taken without it; a tutor version with guardrails largely avoided the harm. Off-age evidence for this book: not ages 11–14 and not programming. It is used once, in the AI box in **The Computing Hour**.

They share its files, browser sessions, and logins"; "The computer is assigned per user, not per Bot. Do not use separate Bots as a security boundary"; and after a bot is deleted, "Because Bots share a computer, files and logins on that computer may remain."

[15.]{#note-15} Computer Science Teachers Association (CSTA), *2026 CSTA PK–12 Computer Science Standards: Middle School Implementation Guidance*, CSTA, 2026, <https://csteachers.org/pk12standards/>, accessed 6 October 2026: "In middle school (grades 6–8) and high school (grades 9–12), students experience the equivalent of a yearlong course at each grade band." On AI, the guidance says that using an AI tool in another subject "only becomes CS learning when the lesson addresses how the tool works," and that students learn to "attribute AI-generated code, images, and other content they use." The guidance is written for schools and districts; it is a reference, not a homeschool requirement.

[16.]{#note-16} Computer Science Teachers Association (CSTA), *2026 CSTA PK–12 Computer Science Standards*, overview page, CSTA, 2026, <https://csteachers.org/pk12standards/>, accessed 6 October 2026. The standards name five concepts: Algorithms & Design; Programming; Data & Analysis; Systems & Security; Computing & Society. Under "Computer Science Is Not," the page lists "using technology tools like word processors, slide decks, or generative AI tools. These appear in CS and in every other subject too," and "being replaced by AI. CS is what powers AI." The standards are licensed CC BY-NC-SA 4.0. They are a map written for schools, not a homeschool requirement.

[17.]{#note-17} Computer Science Teachers Association (CSTA), "Middle School (Grades 6–8, Ages 11–14)," *Foundational Standards by Level*, 2026 CSTA PK–12 Computer Science Standards, CSTA, 2026, <https://csteachers.org/pk12standards/>, accessed 6 October 2026. Middle school is one grade band, grades 6–8. Standards quoted or matched in this book: MS-ALG-PS-02 "Model a given algorithm with a flowchart or pseudocode that includes a combination of control structures and procedures"; MS-ALG-PS-03 "Verify the accuracy of an algorithm for given inputs"; MS-ALG-ML-06 "Hypothesize how a machine learning model generates classifications or predictions"; MS-ALG-ML-07 "Investigate ways to improve the accuracy of a machine learning model and reduce bias by refining the quality of examples and nonexamples in the training data"; MS-ALG-ML-08 "Evaluate the features and limitations of a machine learning model"; MS-ALG-IM-10 "Examine evidence of beneficial and harmful impacts, ethical issues, and biases of algorithms encountered in daily life"; MS-PRO-PD-12 "Use a procedure to structure code for clarity and reusability"; MS-PRO-PD-13 "Use reference documentation in program development"; MS-PRO-PD-14 "Justify the importance of attribution and intellectual property when developing computing technologies"; MS-PRO-VD-16 "Use variables of multiple data types to store, access, and manipulate data within a program"; MS-PRO-RD-17 "Analyze the roles of iteration, selection, variables, and procedures in a segment of code"; MS-PRO-RD-18 "Analyze AI-generated code for accuracy and usability in a programming project"; MS-PRO-TR-19 "Use systematic strategies to test, refine, and document changes to a computing technology to meet the intended purpose"; MS-PRO-TR-20 "Refine a computing technology based on user feedback to improve its usability and accessibility"; MS-DAT-DC-21 "Evaluate how different levels of precision and granularity in data collection affect accuracy, storage, and analysis"; MS-DAT-DC-22 "Explain how data and its associated metadata can be used to answer questions"; MS-DAT-DC-23 "Use a computational tool to sort, filter, group, and summarize structured data"; MS-DAT-DI-26 "Create data visualizations to show how different design choices can impact the interpretation of the same data"; MS-DAT-DI-27 "Summarize a data investigation process, including potential biases, limitations, and supporting evidence"; MS-DAT-IM-28 "Explain the benefits and risks of allowing personal data and metadata to be collected and used in datasets, including issues of data ownership, privacy, and sovereignty"; MS-DAT-IM-29 "Analyze how decisions made at different stages of working with data can lead to biased data, misleading conclusions, and compromised AI models"; MS-SYS-HW-30 "Examine differences between computing systems based on user needs, system requirements, and potential societal, environmental, and ethical impacts"; MS-SYS-HW-31 "Describe computing devices used in various industries, their basic functions, and how they are used to accomplish tasks or solve problems"; MS-SYS-SE-32 "Explain the effects of not using the CIA triad when working with data"; MS-SYS-SE-33 "Evaluate common types of cyber attacks and preventions"; MS-SYS-NT-34 "Model how information in a network is broken down into packets, transmitted between devices, and reassembled"; MS-SYS-NT-35 "Explain how the resilience of the internet depends on interconnected devices and their roles and functions within the network"; MS-SOC-ET-40 "Evaluate when it is appropriate to use AI and other emerging technologies to solve a problem based on their capabilities, limitations, and environmental impacts"; MS-SOC-HU-43 "Analyze how the decisions humans make when using computing technologies have ethical and social consequences". How to read a code: MS is the middle-school band; ALG, PRO, DAT, SYS, and SOC are the five concepts (Algorithms & Design, Programming, Data & Analysis, Systems & Security, Computing & Society); then come a two-letter topic tag and the standard's number. In MS-SYS-SE-32, the CIA triad means confidentiality, integrity, and availability. The materials are licensed CC BY-NC-SA 4.0. The activities matched to these standards are this book's own teaching moves, and the standards are a reference map of what is age-reasonable, not a homeschool requirement.

Young teens "have more ability for complex thought" and "develop a stronger sense of right and wrong"; they "make more of their own choices about friends, sports, studying, and school" and "become more independent … although parents are still very important." The CDC suggests parents be clear about goals and "allow your teen input on how to reach those goals." The CDC site refused automated reading on the access date, so this wording was checked against an archived copy of the same page.

Somerville, "The Teenage Brain: Sensitivity to Social Evaluation," *Current Directions in Psychological Science* 22, no. 2 (2013): 121–127, <https://doi.org/10.1177/0963721413476512>, accessed 6 October 2026. A review of evidence that adolescents are especially sensitive to being evaluated by others. This book uses it for one practical habit, keeping feedback about the code, not the coder; it is not a claim about any one child.

[20.]{#note-20} Allison Master, Andrew N. Meltzoff, and Sapna Cheryan, "Gender Stereotypes about Interests Start Early and Cause Gender Disparities in Computer Science and Engineering," *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences* 118, no. 48 (2021): e2100030118, <https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.2100030118>, accessed 6 October 2026. Stereotypes that girls are less interested in computer science and engineering are endorsed by children and adolescents "as early as age six (first grade)"; the authors conclude that "addressing interest stereotypes may help improve educational equity."

[21.]{#note-21} Ronny Scherer, Fazilat Siddiq, and Bárbara Sánchez Viveros, "The Cognitive Benefits of Learning Computer Programming: A Meta-Analysis of Transfer Effects," *Journal of Educational Psychology* 111, no. 5 (2019): 764–792, <https://doi.org/10.1037/edu0000314>, accessed 6 October 2026. Across 105 studies and 539 effect sizes, the meta-analysis reports a moderate overall transfer effect (g = 0.49), larger for skills close to programming (g = 0.75) than for skills further away (g = 0.47); effects were smaller against active control groups, and "School achievement and literacy, however, benefited the least." Studies vary widely in age and design, so this book treats transfer as possible, not promised.

[22.]{#note-22} Shuchi Grover, Roy Pea, and Stephen Cooper, "Designing for Deeper Learning in a Blended Computer Science Course for Middle School Students," *Computer Science Education* 25, no. 2 (2015): 199–237, <https://doi.org/10.1080/08993408.2015.1033142>, accessed 6 October 2026. A seven-week course designed and studied by its developers with seventh and eighth graders in one middle school. Students showed learning gains and "were able to transfer their learning from Scratch to a text-based programming context"; loops and variables were the hardest concepts for many of them.

[23.]{#note-23} College Board, "What is the acceptable usage of generative AI?," AP Computer Science Principles FAQ, AP Central, 2026, <https://apcentral.collegeboard.org/courses/ap-computer-science-principles/course/faq/generative-ai-usage-guidelines>, accessed 6 October 2026: "Students can use Generative AI tools as supplementary resources for understanding coding principles, assisting in code development, and debugging. It is the student's responsibility to review and understand any code co-written with AI tools, ensuring its functionality. Additionally, students must be prepared to explain their code in detail, as required on the end-of-course exam. Students should include appropriate acknowledgment (i.e., through citation, attribution, and/or by reference) for any code created by generative AI tools." AP CSP is a high-school course; this book borrows its "explain it" rule because it fits ages 11–14 well.

Margulieux, and Jochen Rick, "Solving Parsons Problems versus Fixing and Writing Code," *Proceedings of the 17th Koli Calling International Conference on Computing Education Research* (2017): 20–29, <https://doi.org/10.1145/3141880.3141895>, accessed 6 October 2026. Undergraduates who solved Parsons problems spent less time than those who fixed or wrote the equivalent code, with similar learning. Not a study of middle-schoolers.

The same high-school classes, followed after they moved to Java: after ten weeks of Java, the difference between the blocks and text groups was no longer present.

A study in 13 schools with 493 students aged 11 to 14, compared with a control group of 180 students taught otherwise; after 8 to 12 weeks, the PRIMM group scored higher on a programming test, a small effect (r = .13). Developed and studied by the method's authors.

[38.]{#note-38} GitHub, "GitHub Terms of Service," GitHub Docs, 2026, <https://docs.github.com/en/site-policy/github-terms/github-terms-of-service>, accessed 6 October 2026: "A User must be at least 13 years of age."

[39.]{#note-39} Replit, "Terms of Service," Replit, 2026, <https://replit.com/terms-of-service>, accessed 6 October 2026: "you must be at least 13 years of age, and if you are under 18, you need permission from a parent or guardian."

[40.]{#note-40} Andrew Hunt and David Thomas, *The Pragmatic Programmer* (Addison-Wesley), section "Debugging," excerpt at <https://courses.cs.duke.edu/fall21/compsci307d/readings/pragmatic-programmer-debugging.pdf>, accessed 6 October 2026. The excerpt says, "No one writes perfect software, so it's a given that debugging will take up a major portion of your day"; advises testing boundary conditions; notes that if you "changed only one thing" and the system stopped working, "that one thing was likely to be responsible"; and, in "Rubber Ducking," says that "the simple act of explaining, step by step, what the code is supposed to do often causes the problem to leap off the screen and announce itself." Its footnote tells of a colleague who carried a small yellow rubber duck and placed it on his terminal while coding, and the text adds that if no person is around, "a rubber duck, or teddy bear, or potted plant will do." This is practitioner advice, not a study.

[41.]{#note-41} National Museum of American History, Smithsonian Institution, "Log Book With Computer Bug," object ID 1994.0191.01, <https://americanhistory.si.edu/collections/object/nmah_334663>, accessed 6 October 2026. The museum's description says Thomas Edison talked about bugs in electrical circuits in the 1870s; that in 1947 Mark II engineers at Harvard found a moth in a component, taped it into their logbook, and labeled it "first actual case of bug being found"; and that "this log book was probably not Hopper's, but she and the rest of the Mark II team helped popularize the use of the term computer bug and the related phrase 'debug.'" The Computer History Museum's "This Day in History" entry dates the logbook page to 9 September 1947 (https://www.computerhistory.org/tdih/september/9/, accessed 6 October 2026).

[42.]{#note-42} Python Software Foundation, "8. Errors and Exceptions," *The Python Tutorial*, Python 3.14 documentation, <https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/errors.html>, accessed 6 October 2026. The tutorial distinguishes syntax errors from exceptions; says the error arrow points to where the error was detected and that "this is not always the place that needs to be fixed"; and says "the last line of the error message indicates what happened." The error messages quoted in this book were produced by running each sample in Python 3.13; wording can differ slightly between versions, including the Python 3.14 that Thonny installs.

[43.]{#note-43} Python Software Foundation, "5. Data Structures," *The Python Tutorial*, Python 3.14 documentation, <https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/datastructures.html>, accessed 6 October 2026. Describes `append` ("Add an item to the end of the list"), `remove` ("Remove the first item from the list whose value is equal to value. It raises a `ValueError` if there is no such item"), and `index` ("Return zero-based index").

[44.]{#note-44} Python Software Foundation, "Built-in Functions," *The Python Standard Library*, Python 3.14 documentation, <https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html>, accessed 6 October 2026. Describes `ord()` (returns the Unicode code point of a character; `ord('a')` returns 97) and `chr()` (the reverse; `chr(97)` returns `'a'`), along with `len()`, `max()`, and `sum()`. Every Python sample in Chapters 4 and 6 was run in Python 3.13 to confirm the stated output; the micro:bit samples in Chapter 6 use functions from the micro:bit MicroPython documentation and were not run on a board.

[45.]{#note-45} Consumer Reports, "What You Need to Know About Exif Data," <https://www.consumerreports.org/electronics-computers/privacy/what-can-you-tell-from-photo-exif-data-a2386546443/>, accessed 6 October 2026. Explains that smartphones and digital cameras automatically capture details of when, where, and how a photo was taken; that this data travels with the photo file; and that users can turn off the camera's access to location.

[46.]{#note-46} CS Unplugged, "Searching Algorithms," <https://www.csunplugged.org/en/topics/searching-algorithms/>, accessed 6 October 2026. Free unplugged lessons on sequential searching (checking items one at a time) and binary searching (halving a sorted list), and on choosing between algorithms for the same purpose.

[47.]{#note-47} MDN Web Docs (Mozilla), "Learn web development," <https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Learn_web_development>, accessed 6 October 2026. A free course and reference for HTML, CSS, and JavaScript, designed to take learners "from 'beginner' to 'comfortable.'"

[48.]{#note-48} MDN Web Docs (Mozilla), "rgb()," CSS reference, <https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/CSS/Reference/Values/color_value/rgb>, accessed 6 October 2026. Describes colors written as red, green, and blue values, each a number from 0 to 255. The space-separated form used in this book, such as `rgb(20 80 140)`, is the current syntax; the comma-separated form is described as legacy.

[49.]{#note-49} MDN Web Docs (Mozilla), "HTML: A good basis for accessibility," <https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Learn_web_development/Core/Accessibility/HTML>, accessed 6 October 2026. Notes that many screen readers let users jump between headings or list them as a table of contents; that alt text "should always provide a direct representation of the image and what it conveys visually"; that decorative images can use an empty `alt=""`; that link text should make sense out of context (contrasting "Find out more about whales" with "click here"); that text color "has to be significantly different from the background color (a 4.5:1 contrast)"; and that real buttons and links should be used for controls.

[50.]{#note-50} MDN Web Docs (Mozilla), "What are browser developer tools?," <https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Learn_web_development/Howto/Tools_and_setup/What_are_browser_developer_tools>, accessed 6 October 2026. Gives the keyboard shortcuts and the right-click "Inspect" route for opening developer tools, notes that Safari's tools must be enabled first, and says the Console reports the errors the browser meets as it tries to run your code.

[51.]{#note-51} MDN Web Docs (Mozilla), "How does the Internet work?," <https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Learn_web_development/Howto/Web_mechanics/How_does_the_Internet_work>, accessed 6 October 2026. Explains routers and IP addresses (compared to street addresses); notes that a home router often combines a switch, a router, and a modem; says the Internet is designed so that "whatever happens, they find a way to stay connected"; and distinguishes the Internet (the infrastructure) from the Web (a service built on top of it).

[52.]{#note-52} MDN Web Docs (Mozilla), "How the web works," <https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Learn_web_development/Getting_started/Web_standards/How_the_web_works>, accessed 6 October 2026. Describes a browser requesting files from a server; DNS as "like an address book for websites"; data sent in "small chunks called packets," each with a header carrying addresses and its place in the sequence; packets taking different routes and arriving out of order, with missing packets requested again; and HTTPS.

[53.]{#note-53} MDN Web Docs (Mozilla), "EventTarget: addEventListener() method," <https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/EventTarget/addEventListener>, and "Document: querySelector() method," <https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Document/querySelector>, both accessed 6 October 2026. The button example in Chapter 5 was run in a current desktop browser to confirm that clicking fills in the paragraph.

[54.]{#note-54} CodeAI (formerly Code.org), "Computer Science Discoveries," CodeAI, 2026, <https://code.org/en-US/curriculum/computer-science-discoveries>, accessed 6 October 2026. A free curriculum listed for grades 6–10, including web design, that has students "building their own websites, apps, animations, games, and physical computing systems."

[55.]{#note-55} CodeAI (formerly Code.org), "Artificial Intelligence Discoveries," CodeAI, 2026, <https://code.org/en-US/curriculum/artificial-intelligence-discoveries>, and "CodeAI Launches AI Discoveries & AI Foundations," <https://code.org/en-US/codeai-launches-ai-foundations-and-ai-discoveries>, both accessed 6 October 2026. The course page lists Web Lab among its tools, says its Web Development unit uses "a new, AI-assisted version of Web Lab," and states that "Web Lab and AI tools are built for students ages 11 and up"; the launch page says "a free CodeAI account is required to access." This book's rule (AI helpers off or parent-run at 11–12) is stricter than the course's own description.

[56.]{#note-56} CS Unplugged, "Squeezing pictures into less space," <https://www.csunplugged.org/en/topics/image-representation/squeezing-pictures-into-less-space/>, accessed 6 October 2026. Explains run-length encoding with each row starting with white (a row beginning with black starts with 0); notes that fax machines "relied heavily on run-length encoding"; and explains that the simple version works well for black-and-white images but not photographs, while JPEG finds patterns in photos and then compresses them using RLE.

[57.]{#note-57} CS50, Harvard University, "How Computers Work," CS50 AP curriculum reference, <https://cs50.harvard.edu/ap/2021/curriculum/technology/references/how_computers_work.pdf>, accessed 6 October 2026. Describes the CPU as "responsible for performing calculations and following instructions"; RAM as "short-term memory that software can use to store data quickly and temporarily"; drives as storing data "more permanently"; and the operating system as "the computer's manager; it's in charge of translating input from your keyboard and mouse, displaying information on the screen, and moving things around in memory," deciding "how to delegate hardware resources for different software applications."

[58.]{#note-58} Stanford Institute for Human-Centered Artificial Intelligence (HAI), "What is a Graphics Processing Unit (GPU)?," AI definitions, <https://hai.stanford.edu/ai-definitions/what-is-a-gpu>, accessed 6 October 2026. Defines a graphics processor as a chip "originally designed to rapidly render graphics and images by performing many mathematical calculations simultaneously in parallel," containing "thousands of smaller cores," which makes it suited to the calculations used in AI.

[59.]{#note-59} International Energy Agency (IEA), *Energy and AI*, executive summary, IEA, April 2025, <https://www.iea.org/reports/energy-and-ai/executive-summary>, accessed 6 October 2026: "Data centres accounted for around 1.5% of the world's electricity consumption in 2024, or 415 terawatt-hours (TWh)," and consumption "is set to more than double to around 945 TWh by 2030"; "AI-focused data centres can draw as much electricity as power-intensive factories such as aluminium smelters." The IEA describes data centers' global share as small but their local effects as "far more pronounced." These are estimates and projections that change from year to year; the book uses them only to make the idea concrete.

[60.]{#note-60} MDN Web Docs (Mozilla), "Public-key cryptography," Glossary, <https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Glossary/Public-key_cryptography>, accessed 6 October 2026. "Keys come in pairs"; one is kept private and one is made public; "Anyone can encrypt a message to the owner of the private key, but only the owner of the private key can decrypt it"; and "it's common for an encryption system to use a symmetric algorithm to encrypt the messages, then a public-key system to encrypt the symmetric key."

[61.]{#note-61} MDN Web Docs (Mozilla), "HTTPS," Glossary, <https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Glossary/HTTPS>, accessed 6 October 2026. HTTPS "is an encrypted version of the HTTP protocol. It uses TLS to encrypt all communication between a client and a server."

[62.]{#note-62} Federal Bureau of Investigation, Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3), "Cyber Actors Exploit 'Secure' Websites in Phishing Campaigns," Public Service Announcement I-061019-PSA, 10 June 2019, <https://www.ic3.gov/PSA/2019/PSA190610>, accessed 6 October 2026. Notes that https and the lock icon "are supposed to indicate the web traffic is encrypted," that criminals use them on phishing sites, and advises: "Do not trust a website just because it has a lock icon or 'https' in the browser address bar."

[63.]{#note-63} Micro:bit Educational Foundation, "Features," <https://microbit.org/get-started/features/overview/>, accessed 6 October 2026. Lists buttons A and B; 25 LEDs in a 5x5 grid that also sense light; a temperature sensor in the processor; accelerometer; compass; radio and Bluetooth; a battery socket for two AAA batteries; and, on V2, a touch logo, microphone, and speaker.

[64.]{#note-64} Micro:bit Educational Foundation, "Python Editor" user guide, <https://microbit.org/get-started/user-guide/python-editor/>, and "New micro:bit Python Editor," <https://microbit.org/new-microbit-python-editor/>, both accessed 6 October 2026; editor at <https://python.microbit.org/>. The guide says the editor supports "students aged 11-14," is "totally free of charge and no logins or passwords are needed," and keeps working offline once loaded. Its simulator supports "buttons, gestures, light levels, temperature, compass, sound, radio, serial pins and data logging," so students can work on projects without a board; it shows a V2 board, so some code may not work on a V1.

[65.]{#note-65} Micro:bit MicroPython documentation, "Buttons" tutorial, <https://microbit-micropython.readthedocs.io/en/v2-docs/tutorials/buttons.html>; "microbit" module reference, <https://microbit-micropython.readthedocs.io/en/v2-docs/microbit.html>; and "random" module, <https://microbit-micropython.readthedocs.io/en/v2-docs/random.html>; all accessed 6 October 2026. Documents `button_a.is_pressed()`, `display.scroll()`, `display.show(Image.HAPPY)`, `sleep()` in milliseconds, `running_time()`, `temperature()` in degrees Celsius, and `random.randint()`.

[66.]{#note-66} Micro:bit MicroPython documentation, "Data Logging V2," <https://microbit-micropython.readthedocs.io/en/v2-docs/log.html>, accessed 6 October 2026. The `log` module saves data to a `MY_DATA` file on a micro:bit V2 that "can be viewed and plotted with a browser"; documents `log.set_labels()` and `log.add()`, with an example logging `temperature()` and `display.read_light_level()`.

[67.]{#note-67} Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA), "Secure Our World," CISA, 2026, <https://www.cisa.gov/secure-our-world>, accessed 6 October 2026. The four easy ways: "Recognize and Report Phishing"; "Stop Online Crime with Strong Passwords"; "Make Your Accounts Safer with Multifactor Authentication (MFA)"; "Update Software for Safety." The page adds that "using strong passwords and a password manager are some easy ways to protect ourselves."

[68.]{#note-68} Federal Trade Commission, "How To Recognize and Avoid Phishing Scams," FTC Consumer Advice, <https://consumer.ftc.gov/articles/how-recognize-avoid-phishing-scams>, accessed 6 October 2026. The sample scam email shows a generic greeting, an account "on hold because of a billing problem," and an invitation to click a link to update payment details. Multi-factor credentials "fall into three categories: something you know … something you have … something you are." Phishing can be reported at ReportFraud.ftc.gov; phishing texts can be forwarded to SPAM (7726).

[69.]{#note-69} National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST), *Digital Identity Guidelines: Authentication and Authenticator Management*, NIST Special Publication 800-63B-4, 2025, <https://pages.nist.gov/800-63-4/sp800-63b.html>, accessed 6 October 2026. Verifiers "SHALL require passwords that are used as a single-factor authentication mechanism to be a minimum of 15 characters in length"; "SHALL NOT impose other composition rules (e.g., requiring mixtures of different character types)"; and "SHALL NOT require subscribers to change passwords periodically," though they must force a change if there is evidence of compromise. NIST writes rules for services and agencies, not families; this book calls them "the federal password guidelines" and translates them into household habits.

[70.]{#note-70} Federal Trade Commission, "Children's Online Privacy Protection Rule," final rule, *Federal Register* 90 FR 16918, FR Doc. 2025-05904, 22 April 2025, <https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/04/22/2025-05904/childrens-online-privacy-protection-rule>, accessed 6 October 2026. The amended rule took effect 23 June 2025; with listed exceptions, regulated companies had until 22 April 2026 to comply.

[71.]{#note-71} Office of the U.S. Surgeon General, *Social Media and Youth Mental Health: The U.S. Surgeon General's Advisory*, U.S. Department of Health and Human Services, 2023, <https://www.hhs.gov/surgeongeneral/reports-and-publications/youth-mental-health/social-media/index.html>, accessed 6 October 2026. The advisory page states that "we cannot conclude social media is sufficiently safe for children and adolescents." The advisory's full text notes: "Although age 13 is commonly the required minimum age used by social media platforms in the U.S., nearly 40% of children ages 8–12 use social media." An advisory summarizes evidence and suggests actions; it is not a law. A January 2023 television remark by the Surgeon General about age 13 was an interview, not part of the advisory, and is not used here.

[72.]{#note-72} American Academy of Pediatrics, Center of Excellence on Social Media and Youth Mental Health, "Appropriate Age to Introduce a Mobile Device," Q&A Portal, 2026, <https://www.aap.org/en/patient-care/media-and-children/center-of-excellence-on-social-media-and-youth-mental-health/qa-portal/qa-portal-library/qa-portal-library-questions/appropriate-age-to-introduce-a-mobile-device/>, accessed 6 October 2026. The page says research "doesn't point to one perfect age" and points families to the Phone Ready quiz, whose signs of readiness include: "Is your child responsible about things like homework? Do they say sorry if they hurt a friend's feelings? How well do they follow other tech rules? Are you as a parent ready to help support them?"

[73.]{#note-73} Michelle Faverio and Olivia Sidoti, "Teens, Social Media and AI Chatbots 2025," Pew Research Center, 9 December 2025, <https://www.pewresearch.org/internet/2025/12/09/teens-social-media-and-ai-chatbots-2025/>, accessed 6 October 2026. An online survey of 1,458 U.S. teens ages 13 to 17, conducted 25 September to 9 October 2025. "Roughly two-thirds of teens (64%) say they ever use an AI chatbot," including about three in ten who do so daily; among 13- and 14-year-olds, 57 percent use chatbots. Of YouTube: "Roughly three-quarters of teens say they use it every day"; "three-quarters of 15- to 17-year-olds say they use Instagram, compared with 44% of 13- to 14-year-olds." The survey did not include children under 13. Pew is a nonpartisan research organization that takes no policy positions.

[74.]{#note-74} Federal Bureau of Investigation, "The Financially Motivated Sextortion Threat," FBI News, 16 January 2024, <https://www.fbi.gov/news/stories/the-financially-motivated-sextortion-threat>, accessed 6 October 2026; see also FBI, "Sextortion," <https://www.fbi.gov/how-we-can-help-you/scams-and-safety/common-scams-and-crimes/sextortion>. The FBI describes victims as "mostly boys" and says offenders "typically pretend to be alluring young girls" to befriend teens, often on social media and gaming platforms. Its advice to anyone targeted: "Understand you are not at fault and you are not alone"; "Ask for help from a trusted adult or law enforcement before sending money or more images. Cooperating with the predator rarely stops the blackmail and harassment"; report the account through the platform and block it; "Save all interactions"; and report to the FBI at tips.fbi.gov or 1-800-CALL-FBI (1-800-225-5324), or to local law enforcement.

[75.]{#note-75} National Center for Missing & Exploited Children (NCMEC), "Take It Down," 2026, <https://takeitdown.ncmec.org/>, accessed 6 October 2026: "Take It Down is a free service that can help you remove or stop the online sharing of nude, partially nude, or sexually explicit images or videos taken of you when you were under 18 years old. You can remain anonymous while using the service and you won't have to send your images or videos to anyone." It works on "public or unencrypted online platforms that have agreed to participate." NCMEC's CyberTipline (https://report.cybertip.org/) takes reports of online exploitation.

[76.]{#note-76} College Board, "Guidance for Artificial Intelligence Tools and Other Services," AP Central, 2026, <https://apcentral.collegeboard.org/exam-administration-ordering-scores/administering-exams/exam-policies/artificial-intelligence-tools>, accessed 6 October 2026. On AP Computer Science Principles: "Students should be aware that generative AI tools can produce incomplete code, code that creates or introduces biases, code with errors, inefficiencies in how the code executes, or code complexities that make it difficult to understand and therefore explain the code."

[77.]{#note-77} CodeAI (formerly Code.org), "Hour of AI," CodeAI, 2026, <https://code.org/en-US/hour-of-ai>, accessed 6 October 2026. AI for Oceans is listed for grades 3–12: "you train a real machine learning model to identify sea creatures and trash in the ocean." At 11–12 this book has the parent run it on the parent's device.

[78.]{#note-78} Five state examples, read on official pages and accessed 6 October 2026. Laws change; read your own state's current rules. This is not legal advice. New York, 8 NYCRR § 100.10 (Home instruction), via Cornell Legal Information Institute, <https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/new-york/8-NYCRR-100.10> (for grades seven and eight: "English (two units); history and geography (two units); science (two units); mathematics (two units); physical education (on a regular basis); health education (on a regular basis); art (one-half unit); music (one-half unit); practical arts (on a regular basis); and library skills (on a regular basis)"). Pennsylvania, 24 P.S. § 13-1327.1 (Home education program), <https://www.legis.state.pa.us/WU01/LI/LI/US/HTM/1949/0/0014.013.000.027.001..HTM> (lists required elementary and secondary courses; the portfolio "shall consist of a log, made contemporaneously with the instruction," samples of the student's work, and, in grades three, five, and eight, standardized test results in reading/language arts and mathematics). Texas Education Agency, "Home Schooling," <https://tea.texas.gov/families-and-students/finding-school-your-child/home-schooling> (parents "are required to follow a course of study that includes good citizenship"; under *Leeper*, the written curriculum covers reading, spelling, grammar, math, and good citizenship). North Carolina Division of Non-Public Education, "Requirements and Recommendations," <https://www.doa.nc.gov/divisions/non-public-education/home-schools/requirements-recommendations> (an annual nationally standardized achievement test that "must measure English grammar, reading, spelling, and mathematics"). Virginia, Va. Code § 22.1-254.1, <https://law.lis.virginia.gov/vacode/title22.1/chapter14/section22.1-254.1/> (a "description of the curriculum, limited to a list of subjects to be studied during the coming year"). None of the five names computer science or computing among required subjects for these grades.

[79.]{#note-79} HSLDA, "Homeschool Laws by State," <https://hslda.org/legal>, accessed 6 October 2026. Collects state summaries and advises that "You should follow the law of the state in which you are physically present." HSLDA is an advocacy organization; use its pages as a starting point and read your state's official text.

[80.]{#note-80} Meng Cui and Rachel Hanson, *Homeschooling in the United States: Results from the 2012 and 2016 Parent and Family Involvement Survey (PFI-NHES: 2012 and 2016)*, NCES 2020-001, National Center for Education Statistics, U.S. Department of Education, December 2019, Table 9, <https://nces.ed.gov/pubs2020/2020001.pdf>, accessed 6 October 2026. Among homeschooled students with a grade equivalent of 6th to 8th grade, 17 percent had "computer science (e.g., computer programming)" among the subject areas taught during home instruction in 2016. It is the most recent federal figure found, and it is from 2016.

[81.]{#note-81} HSLDA, "3 Ways to Determine My Teen's High School Course Credit," HSLDA, 15 January 2020, <https://hslda.org/post/3-ways-to-determine-my-teens-high-school-course-credit>, accessed 6 October 2026: "For a core course (English, science, history, math, or foreign language), you will want your teen to log at least 150 hours for 1.0 credit (roughly five hours a week for 30 weeks)"; for an elective course, "log 120+ hours for 1.0 credit and 60+ hours for 0.5 credit"; a lab science, 180 hours. These are guidelines from an advocacy organization, not law; states and schools set their own rules for high-school credit.

[82.]{#note-82} Bebras USA, "Bebras Computing Challenge," <https://bebraschallenge.org/>, accessed 6 October 2026. "Typically, Bebras USA is held in November"; the 2026 U.S. challenge was scheduled for 9–20 November. It is free; students have "45 minutes to complete as many tasks as they can"; age groups include Benjamins (10–12), Cadets (12–14), Juniors (14–16), and Seniors (16–18). A coordinator registers students; check the site for how a family can take part, and check the current dates and rules.

[83.]{#note-83} Python Software Foundation, "Python Documentation," <https://www.python.org/doc/> and <https://docs.python.org/3/>, accessed 6 October 2026. Python and its documentation, including "The Python Tutorial," are free.

[84.]{#note-84} Scratch Team, "Under 16," Scratch Help Center, MIT, <https://mitscratch.freshdesk.com/en/support/solutions/articles/4000228296-under-16>, accessed 6 October 2026: "anyone under 16 who registers a new Scratch account will need permission from a parent or guardian"; unverified accounts "can still make and save Scratch projects, but they cannot comment, share, or participate in the online community." Scratch itself is at <https://scratch.mit.edu/>.

[85.]{#note-85} Arduino Help Center, "About Arduino accounts for minors," <https://support.arduino.cc/hc/en-us/articles/4839080453148-About-Arduino-accounts-for-minors>, accessed 6 October 2026. Arduino offers "Junior accounts" for users below 14 and "Restricted accounts" for ages 14 to 17; both must be linked to a parent. "A junior account can be created by a parent, or at the request of the minor (with parental consent)," and a parent must approve a restricted account. Check the current rules before creating an account.

[86.]{#note-86} American Computer Science League, "Divisions," <https://www.acsl.org/get-started/divisions>, and "Registration," <https://www.acsl.org/Registration>, both accessed 6 October 2026. Junior Division: "Best for junior high and middle school students who are learning programming or who have coding experience. No student beyond grade 9 may compete in the Junior Division." Each contest combines a 30-minute short-answer test with an online programming problem to solve in 72 hours. For the 2026–27 season, "The deadline for registering for the season is December 31, 2026." Advisors register teams; fees apply and are not listed here.

[87.]{#note-87} Air & Space Forces Association, *CyberPatriot XIX Rules Book*, 2 September 2026, <https://www.uscyberpatriot.org/app/uploads/2026/09/CP19_RulesBook_Final-_-2-Sep-2026.pdf>, accessed 6 October 2026: "All middle school and junior high-level students may compete on a Middle School Division team, provided that team is fielded by one of the approved organization types," a list that includes "Homeschool*"; section 5, "Homeschools," sets verification steps (for an independent homeschool, a signed Parent/Guardian Affidavit, or registering as an "other organization" with a local library or similar group). "A CyberPatriot team consists of two to six competitors." For the 2026–27 season, team registration was extended to 8 October 2026. Check with the program at <https://www.uscyberpatriot.org/> before planning.

[88.]{#note-88} Congressional App Challenge, "Rules," <https://www.congressionalappchallenge.us/students/rules/>, accessed 6 October 2026. Students "must be a middle or high school student at the time of app submission"; teams may have up to four students; students compete in the district where they live or attend school; "All competing students must be U.S. residents at the time of submission." The 2026 submission deadline was 26 October.

[89.]{#note-89} Carnegie Mellon University, "picoCTF.org is now CyLab Security Academy," <https://picoctf.org/>, accessed 6 October 2026: "CyLab Security Academy is Carnegie Mellon University's free cybersecurity learning platform"; logins "created before May 8, 2026" still work. Check the current terms for your student's age.

[90.]{#note-90} USA Computing Olympiad, "USACO," <https://usaco.org/>, accessed 6 October 2026. Its mission is "identifying, motivating, and training high-school computing students at all levels"; it offers free "On-line programming contests (four per year) for students at all levels."

[91.]{#note-91} OECD and European Commission, *Empowering Learners for the Age of AI: An AI Literacy Framework for Primary and Secondary Education*, OECD Publishing, Paris, 18 June 2026, <https://doi.org/10.1787/65cd27d4-en>, accessed 6 October 2026. From the publication's abstract: "AI literacy, as a set of knowledge, skills and attitudes, equips learners to understand how AI systems work, critically evaluate their outputs and use them ethically and creatively."

[92.]{#note-92} American Academy of Pediatrics, "Family Media Plan," HealthyChildren.org, <https://www.healthychildren.org/English/fmp/Pages/MediaPlan.aspx>, accessed 6 October 2026. A free online tool for writing down a family's own choices about screens, places, and times.

[93.]{#note-93} Jenny Radesky, "Your Child's First Phone: Are They Ready?," HealthyChildren.org, American Academy of Pediatrics, 3 June 2026, <https://www.healthychildren.org/English/family-life/Media/Pages/Cell-Phones-Whats-the-Right-Age-to-Start.aspx>, accessed 6 October 2026.

[94.]{#note-94} College Board, "About AP Computer Science," AP Central, <https://apcentral.collegeboard.org/courses/about-ap-computer-science>, accessed 6 October 2026: the AP computer science courses "Require familiarity with concepts from Algebra 1. Do not require any computer science or coding experience."

[95.]{#note-95} U.S. Census Bureau, "Computer and Internet Use in the United States: 2021," press release CB24-TPS.61, 18 June 2024, <https://www.census.gov/newsroom/press-releases/2024/computer-internet-use-2021.html>, accessed 6 October 2026: "Most U.S. households had at least one type of computer (95%) and had a broadband internet subscription (90%) in 2021." The Census counts smartphones as a type of computer.
